DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 71
 Four areas: working tree, index, local repo, remote.
 fetch is safe, pull changes your working tree.
 Rebase your own branch; merge into shared branches; never rebase shared history.
 reset rewrites (local), revert adds an undo commit (shared).
 git reflog recovers almost anything lost locally.
 --force-with-lease, never bare --force.
 A committed secret must be rotated, not just deleted.
 Branch protection: PR + approval + green checks + no force-push, admins included.

Part 6. JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
Jenkins appears in both of your roles and as a named project on your CV - "Jenkins pipeline-as-code workflows
integrating Git, automated builds, testing and deployment stages". It is therefore the topic where you must be
strongest, and the one where an interviewer will feel entitled to go deep. This is the largest part of the handbook for
that reason.
6.1 Jenkins Architecture
   Developer pushes to GitHub
            |
            |  webhook (HTTP POST to /github-webhook/)
            v
  +--------------------------------------------------+

  |   - stores job config, build history, credentials |

  |   - SHOULD NOT execute builds itself              |

         v                    v                     v
   +-----------+        +-----------+        +--------------+
   |  AGENT 1  |        |  AGENT 2  |        |   AGENT 3    |
   |  linux    |        |  windows  |        | docker host  |
   | 4 executors|       | 2 executors|       | dynamic pods |
   +-----------+        +-----------+        +--------------+

Extends Jenkins - Git, Docker, Kubernetes, Credentials, Blue

break pipelines
WHY BUILDS SHOULD NEVER RUN ON THE CONTROLLER
The controller holds credentials, job configuration and build history. Any build script running there has access to the
whole Jenkins instance and can take it down by exhausting CPU, memory or disk. Best practice is zero executors on the
controller and all work on agents. Saying this unprompted signals real operational experience.

Critical
jobs/

Critical

6.2 Job Types
 Type
What it is
When to use
Freestyle
Click-configured job
Legacy; avoid in new work - config lives only in the

Pipeline
A Jenkinsfile defining the whole flow as code
The default choice - versioned, reviewable, diffable

Scans a repository and creates a pipeline per branch

Jenkinsfiles

Testing across OS or versions
"WHY PIPELINE-AS-CODE INSTEAD OF FREESTYLE?"
"Because a freestyle job's configuration only exists inside Jenkins - it is not reviewed, not versioned, and cannot be
rolled back. A Jenkinsfile lives in the repository next to the code it builds, so changing the build is a pull request like
anything else, every branch can have its own build definition, and rebuilding Jenkins from scratch does not lose the
pipeline. It also lets a change to the code and the change to its build ship as one reviewable unit."
6.3 Declarative vs Scripted Pipelines
 Declarative
Scripted
Syntax
Structured pipeline { } blocks
Groovy, starting with node { }

Validated before running - fails fast on syntax
Errors surface at runtime

Low
Requires Groovy

try/catch/finally

Constrained, but script { } lets you drop into Groovy
Complete
Recommendati

Default for everything
Only for genuinely dynamic pipelines
The minimal declarative skeleton
 pipeline {
    agent any
    stages {
        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                sh 'echo building'
            }
        }
    }
}
The same thing scripted
 node {
    stage('Build') {
        sh 'echo building'
    }
}

6.4 A Complete Jenkinsfile, Explained Line by Line
Production-shaped declarative pipeline
 pipeline {
 
    agent any
 
    options {
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '30'))
        timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES')
        disableConcurrentBuilds()
        timestamps()
        skipDefaultCheckout(false)
    }
 
    parameters {
        choice(name: 'ENVIRONMENT', choices: ['dev', 'staging', 'prod'], description: 'Target environment')
        booleanParam(name: 'RUN_TESTS', defaultValue: true, description: 'Run the test stage')
        string(name: 'IMAGE_TAG', defaultValue: '', description: 'Override image tag (blank = build
    number)')
    }
 
    environment {
        APP_NAME     = 'payments-api'
        REGISTRY     = 'registry.example.com'
        IMAGE        = "${REGISTRY}/${APP_NAME}"
        TAG          = "${params.IMAGE_TAG ?: env.BUILD_NUMBER}"
        REGISTRY_CRED = credentials('registry-credentials')
    }
 
    triggers {
        githubPush()
        cron('H 2 * * *')
    }
 
    stages {
 
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                checkout scm
                script {
                    env.GIT_SHA = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim()
                }
                echo "Building ${APP_NAME} at ${env.GIT_SHA} for ${params.ENVIRONMENT}"
            }
        }
 
        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                sh 'mvn -B -DskipTests clean package'
            }
        }
 
        stage('Test') {
            when { expression { return params.RUN_TESTS } }
            steps {
                sh 'mvn test'
            }
            post {
                always {
                    junit 'target/surefire-reports/*.xml'
                }
            }
        }
 
        stage('Static Analysis') {
            steps {
                sh 'mvn sonar:sonar -Dsonar.projectKey=${APP_NAME}'
            }
        }
 


===== PAGE 75 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 75
        stage('Docker Build') {
            steps {
                sh """
                   docker build \
                     --build-arg VERSION=${TAG} \
                     -t ${IMAGE}:${TAG} \
                     -t ${IMAGE}:latest .
                """
            }
        }
 
        stage('Image Scan') {
            steps {
                sh "trivy image --exit-code 1 --severity HIGH,CRITICAL ${IMAGE}:${TAG}"
            }
        }
 
        stage('Push Image') {
            steps {
                withCredentials([usernamePassword(credentialsId: 'registry-credentials',
                                                  usernameVariable: 'REG_USER',
                                                  passwordVariable: 'REG_PASS')]) {
                    sh '''
                       echo "$REG_PASS" | docker login ${REGISTRY} -u "$REG_USER" --password-stdin
                       docker push ${IMAGE}:${TAG}
                       docker logout ${REGISTRY}
                    '''
                }
            }
        }
 
        stage('Approve Production') {
            when { expression { params.ENVIRONMENT == 'prod' } }
            steps {
                timeout(time: 30, unit: 'MINUTES') {
                    input message: "Deploy ${IMAGE}:${TAG} to PRODUCTION?", submitter: 'release-managers'
                }
            }
        }
 
        stage('Deploy') {
            steps {
                withCredentials([file(credentialsId: 'kubeconfig-' + params.ENVIRONMENT, variable:
    'KUBECONFIG')]) {
                    sh """
                       kubectl set image deployment/${APP_NAME} ${APP_NAME}=${IMAGE}:${TAG} -n
    ${params.ENVIRONMENT}
                       kubectl rollout status deployment/${APP_NAME} -n ${params.ENVIRONMENT} --timeout=300s
                    """
                }
            }
        }
 
        stage('Health Check') {
            steps {
                script {
                    def url = "https://${params.ENVIRONMENT}.example.com/health"
                    retry(5) {
                        sleep 10
                        sh "curl -sf ${url} > /dev/null"
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
 
    post {
        success {
            echo "SUCCESS ${APP_NAME}:${TAG}"
        }
        failure {
            echo "FAILED - see ${env.BUILD_URL}"


===== PAGE 76 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 76
        }
        unstable {
            echo "UNSTABLE - tests failed"
        }
        always {
            archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'target/*.jar', fingerprint: true, allowEmptyArchive: true
            cleanWs()
        }
    }
}
6.4.1 Every block explained
 Line / block
What it does
Why it matters
pipeline { }
Declares a declarative pipeline
Required outermost block
agent any
Run on any available agent
agent none at top with per-stage agents lets
different stages use different machines
options { }
Pipeline-wide behaviour
buildDiscarder(logRotator(numTo
KeepStr: '30'))
Keep only the last 30 builds
Prevents $JENKINS_HOME filling with build history
- a real production issue
timeout(time: 30, unit:
'MINUTES')
Abort if the pipeline exceeds 30 minutes
Stops a hung build occupying an executor
forever
disableConcurrentBuilds()
One run of this job at a time
Essential when the job deploys - two concurrent
deploys corrupt state
timestamps()
Prefix every console line with a time
Makes log analysis and performance debugging
possible
parameters { }
Inputs the user supplies at build time
choice, booleanParam, string, password
environment { }
Variables available to all stages
Values are interpolated in double-quoted Groovy
strings
credentials('id')
Binds a credential to an env var
For username/password it also creates _USR and
_PSW suffixed variables
triggers { }
How the job starts automatically
githubPush(), pollSCM('H/5 * * * *'),
cron('H 2 * * *'), upstream(...)
cron('H 2 * * *')
Nightly around 02:00
H spreads load - it hashes the job name to pick a
minute, avoiding a thundering herd at exactly
02:00
stages { }
The ordered stages
Each appears as a column in the pipeline view
checkout scm
Clone the repo/branch that this Jenkinsfile
came from
Correct for multibranch; you do not hardcode a
branch
sh / bat / powershell
Run a shell command
sh on Linux agents, bat or powershell on
Windows - relevant to your mixed environment
returnStdout: true
Capture command output into a variable
Always .trim() the result
when { }
Conditional stage execution
branch 'main', expression { }, environment
name:, value:, changeset
post { }
Runs after the stage or pipeline
always, success, failure, unstable, changed,
aborted
junit
Publish test results
Marks the build UNSTABLE rather than FAILED
when tests fail
withCredentials([ ])
Bind secrets for a limited scope
Values are masked in the console output
input
Pause for human approval
Wrap it in timeout or it holds an executor
indefinitely
submitter: 'release-managers'
Only that group may approve
Separation of duties for production
retry(5)
Retry a block up to 5 times
Right for health checks; wrong for deploys, which
are not always idempotent

Line / block
What it does
Why it matters
archiveArtifacts
Store build output on the controller
fingerprint: true lets Jenkins trace which
build produced a given file

Wipe the workspace
Prevents stale files from a previous build affecting
this one
SINGLE QUOTES VS DOUBLE QUOTES IN A JENKINSFILE
Groovy interpolates ${VAR} in double-quoted strings but NOT in single-quoted ones. That is not a bug, it is the tool you
use for secrets: sh "echo ${PASSWORD}" interpolates the secret into the command line, where it can appear in process
listings and logs, while sh 'echo $PASSWORD' lets the shell expand it from the environment, keeping it out of the Groovy
string. Use single quotes for anything secret.
6.5 The Complete CI/CD Flow
 1 DEVELOPER        git push / open PR
        |
 2 GITHUB           webhook POST -> https://jenkins.example.com/github-webhook/
        |
 3 JENKINS          multibranch job for that branch is triggered, queued
        |
 4 AGENT            executor allocated, workspace prepared
        |
 5 CHECKOUT         checkout scm  (shallow clone, credentials from the store)
        |
 6 BUILD            mvn package / npm run build   -> artifact produced
        |
 7 TEST             unit + integration; junit publishes results -> UNSTABLE on failure
        |
 8 QUALITY          SonarQube / lint / dependency + image scan -> gate
        |
 9 PACKAGE          docker build -t image:BUILD_NUMBER  (tag = immutable version)
        |
10 PUSH             docker login (credential binding) -> docker push to registry
        |
11 DEPLOY           kubectl set image / ansible-playbook / helm upgrade  -> environment
        |
12 VERIFY           kubectl rollout status + curl /health with retry
        |
13 NOTIFY           Slack/email on success and failure, with BUILD_URL
        |
14 ON FAILURE       rollback: deploy previous tag / kubectl rollout undo
Git to production - the flow to narrate in interviews
"WALK ME THROUGH YOUR PIPELINE"
"It starts with a webhook from GitHub - a push or a pull request triggers the multibranch job for that branch, so I am
not polling. Jenkins allocates an executor on an agent and checks out that exact commit. Then build, producing a
versioned artifact. Then tests, with results published so the build goes unstable rather than silently green if a test fails.
Then quality and security gates. Then we package into a Docker image tagged with the build number, so the tag is
immutable and traceable back to the commit. The image is pushed to the registry using credentials from the Jenkins
credential store, never hardcoded. Deployment is a separate stage that updates the target environment and then waits
for rollout status, followed by a health check with retries. The post block notifies on success and failure. Rollback is
redeploying the previous image tag, because every build produces an immutable artifact."

Credential binding patterns
 withCredentials([string(credentialsId: 'slack-token', variable: 'SLACK_TOKEN')]) {
    sh 'curl -s -H "Authorization: Bearer $SLACK_TOKEN" https://slack.com/api/auth.test'

                                  usernameVariable: 'USER', passwordVariable: 'PASS')]) {
    sh 'mvn deploy -Dnexus.user=$USER -Dnexus.pass=$PASS'
}

    sh 'kubectl get nodes'
}

}
Jenkins security control
What it does
Matrix / role-based authorisation
Per-user or per-role permissions on jobs and folders
Folder-scoped credentials
A team's credentials are only usable by that team's jobs
Script approval
Sandboxed Groovy; anything outside the sandbox needs admin approval
Agent-to-controller access control
Stops build scripts reading controller files
CSRF protection
On by default - keep it on
Disable "Anyone can do anything"
The single most common Jenkins misconfiguration
Audit Trail plugin
Who ran or changed what
Regular plugin updates
Plugins are the main source of Jenkins CVEs
SECRETS THAT LEAK ANYWAY
Even with credential binding, a secret leaks if you echo it, if you interpolate it into a Groovy double-quoted string that
ends up in a command line, or if a tool prints it in a debug trace. Jenkins masks known credential values in console
output, but only exact matches - base64 or URL-encode it and the mask fails. Rules: single quotes, never set -x around
a secret, and rotate anything you suspect was printed.
6.7 Shared Libraries
When twenty repositories all need the same build logic, you do not copy the Jenkinsfile twenty times. A shared library puts
the logic in one versioned repository.
Shared library layout
 (root)
 +- vars/
 |   +- buildDockerApp.groovy     # a global step, callable as buildDockerApp(...)
 |   +- notifySlack.groovy
 +- src/
 |   +- com/example/Deployer.groovy   # full Groovy classes
 +- resources/
     +- com/example/deploy.sh         # files loaded with libraryResource
vars/buildDockerApp.groovy - a reusable step
 def call(Map config) {
    pipeline {
        agent any
        stages {
            stage('Build') {
                steps { sh "docker build -t ${config.image}:${env.BUILD_NUMBER} ." }


===== PAGE 79 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 79
            }
            stage('Push') {
                steps {
                    withCredentials([usernamePassword(credentialsId: config.credId,
                                     usernameVariable: 'U', passwordVariable: 'P')]) {
                        sh 'echo "$P" | docker login -u "$U" --password-stdin'
                        sh "docker push ${config.image}:${env.BUILD_NUMBER}"
                    }
                }
            }
        }
    }
}
Consuming it - the whole Jenkinsfile becomes three lines
 @Library('devops-shared@v2.1') _
 
buildDockerApp(image: 'registry.example.com/payments-api', credId: 'registry-credentials')
PIN THE LIBRARY VERSION
@Library('devops-shared@v2.1') pins to a tag. Using @main means someone else's commit can change every pipeline in
the organisation without a single one of those repositories changing. Pinning, and promoting versions deliberately, is
the mature answer.
6.8 Multibranch Pipelines, Webhooks and Triggers

1-31
Month
1-12
Day of week
0-7 (0 and 7 are Sunday)
WHY H EXISTS
cron('0 2 * * *') on fifty jobs starts fifty builds at exactly 02:00 and floods the agents. cron('H 2 * * *') hashes the
job name to pick a consistent minute in that hour, spreading the load. Interviewers love this because it shows you have
operated Jenkins at scale rather than just used it.
6.8.1 Multibranch behaviour
A multibranch pipeline scans the repository, finds branches containing a Jenkinsfile, and creates a job for each. It
automatically removes jobs when branches are deleted, and it can build pull requests - crucially, it can build the PR's
*merge* result, so you test what main will actually look like after the merge, not just the branch in isolation.
Branch-aware stages
 stage('Deploy to staging') {
    when { branch 'develop' }


===== PAGE 80 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 80
    steps { sh './deploy.sh staging' }
}
stage('Deploy to production') {
    when {
        allOf {
            branch 'main'
            expression { currentBuild.result == null || currentBuild.result == 'SUCCESS' }
        }
    }
    steps { sh './deploy.sh production' }
}
stage('PR checks only') {
    when { changeRequest() }
    steps { sh 'make lint test' }
}
6.9 Agents, Including Docker and Kubernetes Agents
Agent selection patterns
 pipeline {
    agent none                                  // no global agent - each stage picks its own
    stages {
        stage('Unit tests') {
            agent { label 'linux && jdk17' }    // label expression
            steps { sh 'mvn test' }
        }
        stage('Windows integration') {
            agent { label 'windows' }           // your environment has both
            steps { bat 'run-tests.bat' }
        }
        stage('Build in a container') {
            agent {
                docker {
                    image 'maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-17'
                    args '-v $HOME/.m2:/root/.m2'    // cache dependencies between builds
                }
            }
            steps { sh 'mvn -B package' }
        }
        stage('Kubernetes agent') {
            agent {
                kubernetes {
                    yaml '''
                      apiVersion: v1
                      kind: Pod
                      spec:
                        containers:
                        - name: docker
                          image: docker:24-dind
                          securityContext:
                            privileged: true
                    '''
                }
            }
            steps { container('docker') { sh 'docker version' } }
        }
    }
}
Agent connection
method
How
When
SSH
Controller connects out to the agent
Agent has a fixed address and the controller can reach it
JNLP / inbound
Agent connects in to the controller
Agent is behind NAT or a firewall, or is ephemeral
Docker plugin
A container per build
Clean environment every time, no toolchain drift
Kubernetes plugin
A pod per build, destroyed afterwards
Elastic capacity, no idle agents - the modern standard
THE "WHY CONTAINERISED AGENTS?" ANSWER


===== PAGE 81 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 81
"Static agents accumulate toolchain drift - one has Java 11, another Java 17, and builds mysteriously differ. With a
Docker or Kubernetes agent the environment is declared in the Jenkinsfile, so it is identical every run and versioned
with the code. It also means capacity is elastic: pods exist only while a build runs."
6.10 Jenkins Troubleshooting
 AGENT IS OFFLINE
Symptoms: builds queue with "waiting for an available executor on <agent>".
 1. Is the agent host up and reachable? ping, ssh.
2. Is the agent process running? On JNLP agents check the agent service and its log.
3. Java version mismatch between controller and agent - a very common cause after an upgrade.
4. Disk space on the agent: Jenkins takes a node offline automatically when free space falls below the threshold. df -h
- this is the single most frequent cause.
5. Network/firewall: JNLP needs the agent port open from agent to controller.
6. Check Manage Jenkins -> Nodes -> the node's log for the actual exception.
7. Credentials for SSH agents - a rotated key silently breaks the connection.
WORKSPACE PROBLEMS
Symptoms: stale files, "file already exists", builds passing locally but failing in Jenkins, or the reverse.
  Add cleanWs() in post { always { } }, or use deleteDir() at the start.
 Two jobs sharing one workspace: enable disableConcurrentBuilds() or use customWorkspace.
 Permission errors: files created inside a Docker build run as root and the Jenkins user then cannot delete them. Fix by
running the container with -u $(id -u):$(id -g), or clean up inside the container.
 Disk filling on the agent: buildDiscarder on the job, and a cleanup job for orphaned workspaces.
CREDENTIALS FAILURE
Symptoms: "denied: authentication required", 401, "could not read Username".
 1. Does the credential ID in the Jenkinsfile exactly match the one in the store? IDs are case-sensitive.
2. Is the credential in the right SCOPE? A credential in a folder is invisible to jobs outside it; a "System" scoped
credential is invisible to builds entirely.
3. Has the token or key expired or been rotated?
4. Does the credential have the required permission on the target - push rights to the registry, write access to the
repository?
5. Are you using single quotes so the secret is expanded by the shell rather than interpolated by Groovy?
6. For Docker: docker login state is per-user on the agent - make login part of the stage, not a manual one-off
someone did months ago.
GIT CHECKOUT FAILURE
 Host key verification failed - the agent has never seen this host. Pre-seed known_hosts, or use accept-new
deliberately.
 Permission denied (publickey) - wrong credential, or the deploy key lacks access to that repository.
 couldn't find remote ref main - the branch name changed (master vs main) or the branch was deleted.
 fatal: unable to access ... Could not resolve host - DNS or proxy on the agent.
 Detached HEAD confusing your script - use checkout scm and read env.GIT_COMMIT rather than assuming a branch
name.
 Slow clones - use a shallow clone and reference repositories.
DOCKER STAGE FAILURES


===== PAGE 82 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 82
 Cannot connect to the Docker daemon - the jenkins user is not in the docker group, or the socket is not mounted into
the agent. Remember that adding a user to the docker group is effectively granting root.
 no space left on device - images and layers accumulate. docker system df then a scheduled docker system prune
-af --filter "until=72h".
 denied: requested access to the resource is denied - not logged in, wrong registry, or no push permission.
 Build works locally but not in Jenkins - almost always build context or a .dockerignore difference, or a base image
tag that moved.
 manifest unknown on pull - the tag does not exist; check whether a previous push actually succeeded.
PIPELINE SYNTAX AND RUNTIME ERRORS
 java.lang.NoSuchMethodError / "expected to be within a step" - usually a declarative/scripted mix-up; wrap
imperative Groovy in script { }.
 Scripts not permitted to use method ... - the Groovy sandbox blocked it; either approve it in Manage Jenkins ->
In-process Script Approval or, better, avoid the call.
 Use the built-in Pipeline Syntax generator and Declarative Directive Generator to produce correct step syntax
rather than guessing.
 Validate before committing: curl -X POST -F "jenkinsfile=<Jenkinsfile"
https://jenkins/pipeline-model-converter/validate.
 Also: hudson.AbortException with no detail - read the full console log from the top; the real error is usually 50 lines
above the stack trace.
DEPLOYMENT FAILURE AND ROLLBACK
1. Read which stage failed and its exit code - deploy failures usually surface as a non-zero exit from kubectl, ansible or
a script.
2. kubectl rollout status timing out means the new pods never became Ready - go to kubectl describe pod and the
container logs, not to Jenkins.
3. Roll back first: kubectl rollout undo deployment/app, or redeploy the previous image tag, or re-run the previous
successful build.
4. Verify the rollback with the same health check the pipeline uses.
5. Then diagnose, with the failing image still available because you tagged it immutably.
6. Build the rollback INTO the pipeline: a post { failure { } } block that triggers the rollback removes the human
delay.
6.11 Jenkins Interview Questions
Q. 1. What is Jenkins and what problem does it solve?
 An open-source automation server that runs the build, test and deployment pipeline automatically on every change. It
solves the problem of manual, inconsistent releases - instead of a person remembering a sequence of steps, the sequence is
code, it runs identically every time, and it produces an auditable record of what was built and deployed.
Q. 2. Describe Jenkins architecture.
 A controller and agents. The controller serves the UI and API, stores job configuration, build history and credentials, and
schedules work. Agents execute the builds; each agent has a number of executors defining how many builds it runs
concurrently, and a workspace per job. Agents connect by SSH from the controller or inbound over JNLP. Best practice is
zero executors on the controller, because build scripts there have access to credentials and can take the instance down.
Q. 3. What is $JENKINS_HOME and what would you back up?
 The directory holding all Jenkins state - config.xml, jobs/ with definitions and history, credentials.xml with the secrets/
key material, plugins/ and users/. I would back up everything except workspace/ and caches. The critical detail is that
credentials.xml is useless without secrets/, so they must be backed up together and protected.
Q. 4. Freestyle versus pipeline?


===== PAGE 83 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 83
A freestyle job is configured through the UI, so its definition is not versioned, reviewed or portable. A pipeline is defined in a
Jenkinsfile in the repository, so the build definition is code - reviewed in pull requests, branch-specific, and recoverable if
Jenkins is rebuilt.
Q. 5. Declarative versus scripted pipeline?
 Declarative uses a structured pipeline { } syntax that Jenkins validates before running, with post blocks for conditions - it is
readable and hard to get wrong. Scripted is Groovy inside node { } with full programmatic control. I default to declarative
and drop into script { } for the rare dynamic part.
Q. 6. What is a Jenkinsfile?
 The pipeline definition as code, committed at the root of the repository. It defines agents, stages, steps and post-actions,
and because it lives with the code, changing the build is a reviewable pull request.
Q. 7. What is an executor?
 One concurrent build slot on an agent. An agent with four executors runs four builds at once. Setting it too high causes
builds to contend for CPU and memory and everything slows down; a common rule of thumb is around one per core, lower
if builds are memory-hungry.
Q. 8. How do you trigger a build automatically on commit?
 A webhook from GitHub to /github-webhook/ on the Jenkins URL, with a multibranch or GitHub-integrated job. Polling with
pollSCM is the fallback for when Jenkins is not reachable from the internet, but it adds latency and load - a webhook is push,
polling is asking repeatedly.
Q. 9. Difference between webhook and poll SCM?
 A webhook is pushed by the source system the moment a change happens - immediate and efficient. Poll SCM has Jenkins
ask the repository on a schedule; it works behind firewalls but wastes requests and delays builds by up to the polling
interval.
Q. 10. What does H mean in a Jenkins cron expression?
 Hash. Jenkins hashes the job name to pick a consistent value within the allowed range, so jobs spread out instead of all
firing at the same instant. H 2 * * * runs once between 02:00 and 02:59 at the same minute each day.
Q. 11. How do you handle secrets in Jenkins?
 In the credentials store, scoped as narrowly as possible - folder-level rather than global - and bound into a build only for the
block that needs them, using withCredentials or environment { X = credentials('id') }. In the shell I use single quotes
so the shell expands the variable rather than Groovy interpolating it into the command string. I never echo them, and I
rotate anything that might have been printed.
Q. 12. What is the difference between environment { X = credentials('id') } and withCredentials?
 environment makes it available to the whole pipeline or stage; withCredentials scopes it to one block, which is tighter and
therefore safer. withCredentials also supports more binding types such as secret files and SSH keys.
Q. 13. What are Jenkins parameters and when do you use them?
 Inputs supplied at build time - choice, string, booleanParam, password. I use them for target environment, image tag
overrides and toggles such as skipping tests. They are accessed as params.NAME.
Q. 14. What is the post section?
 Actions that run after a stage or the whole pipeline, keyed by result - always, success, failure, unstable, changed, aborted. It
is where I publish test results, archive artifacts, send notifications and clean the workspace, because those must happen even
when the build fails.
Q. 15. What is the difference between FAILURE and UNSTABLE?
 FAILURE means a step errored - the build could not complete. UNSTABLE means it completed but something reported a
problem, most commonly failing tests published through junit. Distinguishing them lets you treat a compile failure
differently from a flaky test.
Q. 16. How do you run stages in parallel?
 A parallel block containing named branches, each with its own steps and optionally its own agent. It is how you cut
pipeline time when unit tests, integration tests and static analysis are independent. failFast true aborts the others as soon
as one fails.
Q. 17. What is a shared library?
 A separate versioned repository containing reusable pipeline code - global steps in vars/, classes in src/, files in resources/.
It stops the same hundred lines being copy-pasted into twenty Jenkinsfiles. I pin it to a tag so a change to the library cannot


===== PAGE 84 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 84
silently alter every pipeline at once.
Q. 18. What is a multibranch pipeline?
 A job that scans a repository and creates a pipeline for every branch containing a Jenkinsfile, removing them when branches
are deleted. It also builds pull requests, and it can build the merge result rather than the branch alone, so you test what main
will look like after merging.
Q. 19. How do you implement a manual approval before production?
 An input step, wrapped in a timeout so it cannot hold an executor forever, and restricted with submitter so only the release
group can approve. I also put the approval in its own stage so the pipeline visualisation makes the gate obvious.
Q. 20. Why should the input step be outside an agent block or wrapped in a timeout?
 Because while it waits it occupies an executor. A pipeline waiting overnight for approval blocks a build slot. Wrapping it in a
timeout and, in declarative, placing it in a stage with agent none, avoids holding the agent.
Q. 21. How do you archive artifacts and why fingerprint them?
 archiveArtifacts artifacts: 'target/*.jar', fingerprint: true. The fingerprint is an MD5 record that lets Jenkins tell
you which build produced a given file and where it was used, which is genuinely useful when tracing what is running in an
environment.
Q. 22. How do you make a build fail when tests fail?
 junit marks the build unstable by default. If I want a hard failure, I let the test command's non-zero exit propagate, or set
the junit step's thresholds, or explicitly check the result and call error("..."). The important point is deciding deliberately
rather than discovering that failing tests ship.
Q. 23. How do you version your Docker images from Jenkins?
 With $BUILD_NUMBER plus the short Git SHA, and a semantic version tag for releases. Never just latest, because latest is
mutable - you cannot tell what is running and you cannot roll back to a specific build. I also push latest as a convenience
but deploy by the immutable tag.
Q. 24. How do you roll back a deployment from Jenkins?
 Re-deploy the previous immutable tag - either by re-running the last known-good build's deploy stage, or by a
parameterised rollback job that takes a tag. In Kubernetes kubectl rollout undo is faster still. The prerequisite is immutable,
versioned artifacts, which is why tagging matters so much.
Q. 25. How do you speed up a slow pipeline?
 Measure first with timestamps() to find the slow stage. Then: parallelise independent stages, shallow-clone, cache
dependencies (~/.m2, npm cache) on the agent or in a Docker volume, use Docker layer caching, only run heavy stages when
relevant paths changed, split long test suites across parallel agents, and move to ephemeral Kubernetes agents so queueing
stops being the bottleneck.
Q. 26. Your pipeline worked yesterday and fails today with no code change. How do you investigate?
 Compare the two console logs side by side and find the first line that differs - that usually names the cause. Then check what
else changed: a plugin update on the controller, an agent that was rebuilt or has a different tool version, a mutable base
image or dependency tag that moved, an expired credential or certificate, disk filling on the agent, or an external service
such as the registry or an artifact repository being down. Most "nothing changed" failures are a moving external
dependency or a disk.
Q. 27. What is the Blue Ocean plugin?
 A modern UI for pipeline visualisation that makes stage failures and logs much easier to read. It is a presentation layer - it
changes nothing about how pipelines run.
Q. 28. How do you back up and restore Jenkins?
 Back up $JENKINS_HOME excluding workspaces and caches - either filesystem snapshots, or the ThinBackup plugin, or, better,
keep configuration in code: job definitions as Jenkinsfiles, system configuration through Configuration as Code (JCasC), and
plugins pinned in a list. Restore then becomes redeploying Jenkins and applying the YAML rather than restoring a blob.
Q. 29. What is Jenkins Configuration as Code (JCasC)?
 A plugin that lets you define the whole controller configuration - security realm, agents, tools, credentials references, plugin
settings - in a YAML file. It makes a Jenkins instance reproducible and reviewable, which is the same argument as Terraform
for infrastructure.
Q. 30. How do you scale Jenkins?


===== PAGE 85 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 85
Move all builds off the controller onto agents; add agents by workload type with labels; use ephemeral agents from
Kubernetes or Docker so capacity scales with demand; keep $JENKINS_HOME on fast storage; prune build history with
buildDiscarder; and split extremely large instances into several controllers by team, because a single controller has real
limits.
Q. 31. What are the risks of plugins?
 They are the main source of Jenkins vulnerabilities and breakage. An upgrade can change a step's behaviour and break
every pipeline at once. Mitigations: pin versions, test upgrades on a staging controller, keep the plugin set minimal, and read
changelogs before updating - especially for the Git, Docker and Credentials plugins.
Q. 32. How would you give a team access to only their jobs?
 Folders per team, Role-Based Authorization Strategy granting each role rights only on its folder, and credentials scoped to
the folder. That way one team cannot see or use another's credentials, which is the real risk.
Q. 33. What is the difference between sh and bat?
 sh runs a shell command on a Unix agent; bat runs a Windows batch command; powershell runs PowerShell. In a mixed
estate like the one I work in, stages are pinned to agents by label and use the right step for that platform.
Q. 34. How do you capture command output into a variable?
 def out = sh(script: 'git rev-parse --short HEAD', returnStdout: true).trim(). returnStatus: true gives the exit
code instead of failing the build, which is how you handle an expected non-zero exit.
Q. 35. How do you stop two deployments running at the same time?
 disableConcurrentBuilds() on the job, or a lock resource from the Lockable Resources plugin when several different jobs
contend for the same environment. Concurrent deploys to one environment are a genuine outage cause.
Q. 36. What is a quality gate in the pipeline?
 A stage that fails the build when a measured threshold is breached - SonarQube coverage or code smells, a Trivy scan
finding HIGH or CRITICAL vulnerabilities, or a licence check. The point is that it fails automatically rather than depending on
someone noticing a report.
Q. 37. How do you handle a flaky test in CI?
 Short term, quarantine it so it does not block everyone, and make that visible. Medium term, fix it - flakiness is usually a
timing assumption, shared state or a real race condition. Blanket retries are the wrong default because they hide genuine
intermittent bugs; a retry with a report of how often it retried is acceptable as a stopgap.
Q. 38. How do you send notifications?
 A post block with Slack, email or Teams steps, including the build URL, branch, commit and the failing stage. I notify on
failure and on recovery - notifying on every success trains people to ignore the channel.
Q. 39. What happens if the controller restarts mid-build?
 Pipeline builds are designed to be durable - the Pipeline plugin persists state so many builds resume after a restart, but
anything mid-sh on an agent typically fails and must re-run. That is one more reason builds should be idempotent and
re-runnable.
Q. 40. How do you test a Jenkinsfile change without breaking everyone?
 Develop it on a feature branch - with a multibranch job, that branch gets its own pipeline automatically, so the change is
exercised in isolation. Use the Replay feature to iterate quickly on a failing run, validate syntax with the
pipeline-model-converter endpoint, and, for shared libraries, pin consumers to a tag so the change only reaches them when
the tag is promoted.
Q. 41. What is the difference between agent any and agent none?
 agent any allocates an executor for the whole pipeline. agent none allocates nothing globally and requires each stage to
declare its own agent, which is better when stages need different environments, or when a stage is a pure approval gate that
should not occupy an executor.
Q. 42. How do you cache dependencies between builds?
 Mount a persistent directory into the build - for Maven -v $HOME/.m2:/root/.m2 on a Docker agent, or a volume/PVC for
Kubernetes agents. The trade-off is that a cache is shared mutable state, so the pipeline must tolerate a cold cache and you
need a way to invalidate it.
Q. 43. What does cleanWs() do and when would you not use it?
 It deletes the workspace after the build, preventing stale files and reclaiming disk. You would skip it when the next step
needs the workspace, or when a full clean checkout is prohibitively slow and you deliberately rely on incremental builds -


===== PAGE 86 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 6  |  JENKINS COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 86
but then you accept the risk of stale state.
Q. 44. How do you integrate Jenkins with Kubernetes?
 The Kubernetes plugin, which runs each build in a dynamically created pod defined by a pod template - agents appear on
demand and are destroyed afterwards, so there are no idle machines and no toolchain drift. Deployment then uses kubectl
or Helm with a kubeconfig supplied as a secret file credential, ideally with a service account scoped to just the target
namespace.
Q. 45. Jenkins versus GitHub Actions - when would you use each?
 GitHub Actions is tightly integrated with the repository, requires no infrastructure to operate, and is excellent for building,
testing and publishing where the code already lives on GitHub. Jenkins is better where you need to orchestrate across many
systems, run on your own hardware or inside a private network, support complex approval workflows, or you already have
significant investment in pipelines and shared libraries. I use both - GitHub Actions for repository-scoped CI and Jenkins for
deployment orchestration.
Q. 46. How would you implement blue-green deployment in a Jenkins pipeline?
 Deploy the new version to the idle environment, run smoke tests against it directly, then flip the router - an ALB target
group, an nginx upstream, or a Kubernetes Service selector - in a separate stage. Keep the old environment running for a
defined window, with a rollback stage that flips back. I would gate the flip behind an input for production.
Q. 47. What do you do if a build hangs?
 The timeout option should abort it automatically - if there is none, that is the first fix. Then find why: a process waiting on
input (Git prompting for credentials, apt asking a question), a network call with no timeout, a test waiting on an unavailable
service, or a deadlock. Setting GIT_TERMINAL_PROMPT=0 and passing -y/--non-interactive flags removes an entire class of
hangs.
Q. 48. How do you ensure the artifact tested is the artifact deployed?
 Build once, early, and tag it immutably with the build number and commit SHA. Every subsequent stage - test, scan, deploy
to staging, deploy to production - references that same tag. Nothing is ever rebuilt per environment. Fingerprinting and
pushing the image to a registry rather than rebuilding is what makes this real.
Q. 49. Your pipeline deploys successfully but the application still serves the old version. What now?
 Confirm what is actually running rather than what the pipeline thinks: check the image digest on the running instances, not
just the tag - a mutable tag like latest can mean the node reused a cached image. Check imagePullPolicy, whether the
rollout actually created new pods, whether a load balancer is still routing to old targets, and whether a CDN or browser
cache is serving stale content. Nine times out of ten it is a mutable tag plus IfNotPresent, or traffic still going to the old
target group.
Q. 50. How would you design a pipeline for a brand-new service?
 Trigger on PR and on merge to main. On PR: checkout, build, unit tests, lint, dependency scan - fast feedback, no
deployment. On merge to main: build once, run the full test suite, scan the image, tag it with the build number and SHA,
push to the registry, deploy to staging, run smoke tests and a health check, then require approval for production, deploy
with a rolling update, verify rollout status and health, and notify. Failure at any stage stops the pipeline; a post failure
block triggers rollback for deployment stages. Credentials come from the store, the pipeline itself is a Jenkinsfile in the
repository, and common logic lives in a pinned shared library.
PART 6 RECALL POINTS
 Controller schedules, agents execute; zero executors on the controller.
 Jenkinsfile = build definition as code, reviewed like any other change.
 Declarative by default; script { } only when you must.
 H in cron spreads load; webhooks beat polling.
 Credentials from the store, bound with withCredentials, single-quoted in sh.
 disableConcurrentBuilds() on anything that deploys.
 Tag images with BUILD_NUMBER + SHA, never rely on latest.
 buildDiscarder and cleanWs() prevent the disk-full failure mode.
 Shared libraries pinned to a tag, never to main.
 Rollback = redeploy the previous immutable tag.

Architecture, images, Dockerfile, networking, volumes, Compose, troubleshooting
Part 7. DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
Your CV says you "containerized application components with Docker" and "packaged applications with Docker to
standardize environments and reduce inconsistencies". That phrase - reducing inconsistency - is the right reason, and
it is the answer interviewers want. This part makes sure you can back it up with Dockerfile detail, networking, volumes
and failure diagnosis.
7.1 Docker Architecture
  +----------------+        REST API over        +--------------------------------+
  |  DOCKER CLI    | ---- /var/run/docker.sock -->|      DOCKER DAEMON (dockerd)   |
  |  docker build  |                              |   - builds images (BuildKit)   |
  |  docker run    |                              |   - manages containers         |
  |  docker push   |                              |   - manages networks, volumes  |
  +----------------+                              |   - pulls/pushes images        |
                                                  +----------------+---------------+
                                                                   |
                                    +------------------------------+-------------------+
                                    |                                                  |
                          +---------v----------+                           +-----------v-----------+
                          |  containerd / runc |                           |   REGISTRY            |
                          |  actually creates  |                           |  Docker Hub, ECR,     |
                          |  the namespaced    |                           |  Nexus, Harbor        |
                          |  isolated process  |                           +-----------------------+
                          +--------------------+
  Isolation primitives:  NAMESPACES (pid, net, mnt, uts, ipc, user)  +  CGROUPS (cpu, memory, io)
Client, daemon, registry
Component
Role
Docker CLI
Sends commands to the daemon over a Unix socket or TCP
Docker daemon (dockerd)
The long-running service that does everything
containerd
Container lifecycle manager the daemon delegates to
runc
The low-level runtime that actually creates the isolated process
Image
A read-only, layered template
Container
A running (or stopped) instance of an image, with a thin writable layer
Registry
Where images are stored and distributed
Dockerfile
The recipe for building an image
IMAGE VS CONTAINER - SAY IT EXACTLY LIKE THIS
"An image is the immutable, layered template - like a class. A container is a running instance of it with a thin writable
layer on top - like an object. Many containers can run from one image, each with its own writable layer, and when a
container is deleted that writable layer goes with it. That is why anything you need to keep goes in a volume."
ACCESS TO THE DOCKER SOCKET IS ROOT
Anyone in the docker group can run docker run -v /:/host -it alpine chroot /host and become root on the host. So
"add the jenkins user to the docker group" is effectively granting root - a legitimate decision, but you should say it
knowingly. Rootless Docker and Podman exist precisely to avoid it.
7.2 Images and Layers
Every instruction in a Dockerfile that changes the filesystem creates a layer. Layers are content-addressed and cached: if
nothing above a layer has changed, Docker reuses it. That single fact drives every Dockerfile optimisation.


===== PAGE 88 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 88
  CONTAINER  [ thin writable layer ]  <- your runtime changes live here, and die with the container
             ------------------------
  IMAGE      [ COPY app/ /app       ]  <- changes on every code commit
             [ RUN pip install -r   ]  <- changes only when requirements.txt changes
             [ COPY requirements.txt]
             [ WORKDIR /app         ]
             [ FROM python:3.12-slim]  <- base layer, shared by every image built on it
  Shared, read-only layers are stored once on the host and reused by every container using them.
Layers and the writable container layer
THE DEPENDENCY-ORDERING RULE
Copy the dependency manifest and install dependencies BEFORE copying the application source. Source changes on
every commit; dependencies change rarely. Get the order wrong and every build reinstalls everything, turning a
10-second build into a 4-minute one. This is the single most common Dockerfile interview question.
7.3 Dockerfile Instructions
 Instructio
n
Purpose
Notes and traps
FROM
Base image
Pin a specific tag or digest; latest is mutable and destroys
reproducibility
RUN
Execute a command at BUILD time, creating a layer
Chain related commands with && to reduce layers; clean
package caches in the same RUN
COPY
Copy files from the build context into the image
The default choice
ADD
Like COPY, but also auto-extracts local tar archives and can
fetch URLs
Prefer COPY; ADD's magic causes surprises
WORKDIR
Set the working directory (creates it if missing)
Use this instead of RUN cd, which does not persist
CMD
Default command/arguments, overridable at docker run
Only the last CMD counts
ENTRYPOIN

The executable that always runs
CMD becomes its default arguments
EXPOSE
Documents the port the app listens on
Documentation only - it does NOT publish the port

Visible in docker inspect - never put secrets here

Build-time-only variable
Not present at runtime, but IS visible in image history - still
not for secrets

Security best practice; everything after runs as that user

Creates an anonymous volume if none is supplied

CK
How Docker tests container health
Sets status to healthy/unhealthy, which orchestrators and
Compose can act on
LABEL
Metadata
Useful for version, maintainer and provenance
ONBUILD

Rare, and surprising - avoid
7.3.1 CMD vs ENTRYPOINT - the guaranteed question
 CMD
ENTRYPOINT
Purpose
Default command or default arguments
The executable that always runs
Overridden by docker run image arg
Yes, completely replaced
No - the arguments are APPENDED to it
Override at runtime
docker run image /bin/bash
Requires --entrypoint
Typical use
A default that users may replace
A container that behaves like a single binary
The three behaviours
 # 1 - CMD only: fully overridable
CMD ["python", "app.py"]
#   docker run img              -> python app.py
#   docker run img /bin/bash    -> /bin/bash
 


===== PAGE 89 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 89
# 2 - ENTRYPOINT only: arguments are appended
ENTRYPOINT ["python", "app.py"]
#   docker run img --debug      -> python app.py --debug
 
# 3 - Both (the recommended pattern): ENTRYPOINT is the binary, CMD is the default args
ENTRYPOINT ["python", "app.py"]
CMD ["--port", "8080"]
#   docker run img              -> python app.py --port 8080
#   docker run img --port 9090  -> python app.py --port 9090
EXEC FORM VS SHELL FORM
CMD ["python", "app.py"] is the exec form - the process runs as PID 1 and receives signals, so docker stop works
cleanly. CMD python app.py is the shell form - it runs as /bin/sh -c "python app.py", so the shell is PID 1, it does not
forward SIGTERM, and docker stop waits 10 seconds then kills the container. Always use the exec form. This is the
reason behind "my container takes 10 seconds to stop".
7.3.2 COPY vs ADD
 COPY
ADD
Copies local files
Yes
Yes
Extracts local tar archives automatically
No
Yes
Fetches remote URLs
No
Yes (but does not extract them, and produces a layer you cannot
clean)
Recommendation
Use it by default
Use only when you genuinely want tar auto-extraction
7.3.3 A production Dockerfile, explained
Multi-stage build, non-root, cache-friendly
 # ---------- Stage 1: build ----------
FROM node:20-alpine AS builder
WORKDIR /app
 
# Copy ONLY the manifests first so this layer caches until dependencies change
COPY package.json package-lock.json ./
RUN npm ci --omit=dev
 
# Now copy the source - this layer rebuilds on every commit, which is fine
COPY . .
RUN npm run build
 
# ---------- Stage 2: runtime ----------
FROM node:20-alpine
ENV NODE_ENV=production \
    PORT=8080
 
# Create an unprivileged user
RUN addgroup -S app && adduser -S -G app app
 
WORKDIR /app
 
# Copy ONLY the built output and production dependencies from the build stage.
# The compiler, dev dependencies and source code never reach the final image.
COPY --from=builder --chown=app:app /app/node_modules ./node_modules
COPY --from=builder --chown=app:app /app/dist ./dist
 
USER app
EXPOSE 8080
 
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --start-period=20s --retries=3 \
  CMD wget -qO- http://127.0.0.1:8080/health || exit 1
 
ENTRYPOINT ["node"]
CMD ["dist/server.js"]
Python equivalent, with the RUN-chaining pattern


===== PAGE 90 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 90
FROM python:3.12-slim AS base
ENV PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 \
    PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1          # unbuffered stdout so logs appear immediately
 
WORKDIR /app
 
# System packages: install and clean the cache in ONE layer, or the cache is in the image forever
RUN apt-get update \
 && apt-get install -y --no-install-recommends curl build-essential \
 && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*
 
COPY requirements.txt .
RUN pip install --no-cache-dir -r requirements.txt
 
COPY . .
 
RUN useradd -m -u 10001 appuser && chown -R appuser:appuser /app
USER appuser
 
EXPOSE 8000
HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=5s --retries=3 CMD curl -fsS http://localhost:8000/health || exit 1
ENTRYPOINT ["python", "-m", "gunicorn"]
CMD ["-b", "0.0.0.0:8000", "app:application"]
Optimisation
Effect
Multi-stage build
Final image excludes compilers, dev dependencies and source - often 10x
smaller and a far smaller attack surface
Slim/alpine base
Smaller image, fewer CVEs. Caveat: alpine uses musl libc, which can break
some Python/Node native wheels
Manifest before source
Dependency layer stays cached across code commits
rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/* in the SAME RUN
Deleting in a later layer does not shrink the image - the data is already in the
earlier layer
--no-cache-dir for pip
No wheel cache left behind
.dockerignore
Keeps .git, node_modules and secrets out of the build context, speeding up
builds
Non-root USER
Container escape does not immediately give root
HEALTHCHECK
Orchestrators know whether the app is actually up, not just running
Pin base image tags/digests
Reproducible builds
A .dockerignore that actually matters
 .git
.gitignore
node_modules
__pycache__
*.pyc
.env
*.pem
*.key
.venv
dist
coverage
Dockerfile

README.md

docker build --no-cache -t myapp:1.0 .  # ignore the layer cache
docker build --build-arg VERSION=1.0 -t myapp:1.0 .

Containers
 docker run nginx                                     # foreground
docker run -d --name web nginx                       # detached, named
docker run -d -p 8080:80 nginx                       # publish hostPort:containerPort
docker run -d -p 127.0.0.1:8080:80 nginx             # publish only on loopback
docker run -it ubuntu bash                           # interactive TTY
docker run --rm alpine echo hi                       # remove the container on exit
docker run -e APP_ENV=prod -e DB_HOST=db myapp       # environment variables
docker run --env-file .env myapp
docker run -v /host/data:/data myapp                 # bind mount
docker run -v appdata:/var/lib/app myapp             # named volume
docker run --network mynet --name api myapp          # user-defined network
docker run --memory=512m --cpus=1.5 myapp            # resource limits
docker run --restart=unless-stopped -d myapp         # restart policy
docker run -u 1001:1001 myapp                        # run as a specific UID
 
docker ps                       # running containers
docker ps -a                    # including stopped - where exit codes live
docker ps -q                    # IDs only, for scripting
docker stop web                 # SIGTERM, then SIGKILL after the grace period
docker stop -t 30 web           # 30-second grace period
docker start web
docker restart web
docker kill web                 # immediate SIGKILL
docker rm web                   # remove a stopped container
docker rm -f web                # force: stop and remove
docker rm $(docker ps -aq)      # remove all containers
Inspecting and debugging a running container
 docker logs web                          # stdout/stderr of the main process
docker logs -f --tail 100 web            # follow the last 100 lines
docker logs --since 10m web
docker exec -it web bash                 # shell inside a running container
docker exec -it web sh                   # alpine has no bash
docker exec web env                      # check the environment variables it actually has
docker exec web cat /etc/app/config.yml  # check the config it actually got
docker inspect web                       # everything: mounts, network, env, state, exit code
docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}}' web
docker inspect -f '{{.NetworkSettings.IPAddress}}' web
docker inspect -f '{{json .Config.Env}}' web
docker stats                             # live CPU/memory/network per container
docker top web                           # processes inside the container
docker diff web                          # files changed since the image - what did it write?
docker port web                          # published port mappings
docker cp web:/app/logs/app.log ./       # copy a file out of a container
Housekeeping - the disk-full fix
 docker system df                        # what is using disk: images, containers, volumes, cache
docker system df -v                     # per-object detail
docker image prune                      # remove dangling (untagged) images
docker image prune -a                   # remove all images not used by a container
docker container prune                  # remove stopped containers
docker volume prune                     # remove unused volumes  (DATA LOSS - check first)
docker builder prune                    # clear the build cache
docker system prune                     # containers + networks + dangling images + build cache
docker system prune -a --volumes        # everything unused, INCLUDING volumes - know what you are deleting
docker system prune -af --filter "until=72h"    # safer: only objects older than 72 hours

Network driver
Behaviour
Use
bridge (default)
Private internal network on the host; containers get
172.17.x.x; published ports are NATed
Single-host applications
user-defined
bridge
Same, plus automatic DNS resolution by container name
The correct choice for multi-container apps
host
No network namespace - the container uses the host's
stack directly, no port mapping
Maximum performance, or when you need the host's
interfaces. Linux only
none

DEFAULT BRIDGE VS USER-DEFINED BRIDGE
On the DEFAULT bridge, containers can only reach each other by IP - there is no name resolution (the old --link flag is
deprecated). On a USER-DEFINED bridge, Docker runs an embedded DNS server, so http://api:8080 resolves by
container name. That is why docker network create is the first step in any multi-container setup, and it is a frequent
interview question.
Networking commands
 docker network ls
docker network create appnet                     # user-defined bridge, with DNS
docker network create --subnet 10.10.0.0/24 appnet
docker network inspect appnet                    # which containers are attached, and their IPs
docker network connect appnet web                # attach a running container
docker network disconnect appnet web
docker run -d --name db --network appnet postgres:16
docker run -d --name api --network appnet -e DB_HOST=db myapi    # "db" resolves by name
Port syntax
Meaning
-p 8080:80
Host port 8080 -> container port 80, on all host interfaces
-p 127.0.0.1:8080:80
Only reachable from the host itself
-p 80
Container port 80 mapped to a random high host port
-P
Publish every EXPOSEd port to random host ports
(none)
Reachable only from other containers on the same network
"THE CONTAINER RUNS BUT I CANNOT REACH IT"
Three causes, in order of frequency: (1) the application inside is bound to 127.0.0.1 instead of 0.0.0.0, so it only
accepts connections from inside the container's own namespace; (2) no -p mapping, so nothing is published; (3) the
port in -p host:container is backwards, or the container port does not match what the app actually listens on.
Diagnose with docker exec web ss -tulpn and docker port web.

Volume operations
 docker volume create pgdata

docker run --rm -v pgdata:/data -v $(pwd):/backup alpine \
       tar czf /backup/pgdata-$(date +%F).tgz -C /data .
CONTAINER DATA IS EPHEMERAL BY DESIGN
The writable layer dies with the container. Anything that must survive a restart, an image update or a reschedule goes in
a volume - or, better in a cloud context, in a managed database or object store. "Where does your container's data
live?" is a question many candidates fail.
7.7 Docker Compose
docker-compose.yml with healthchecks, limits and dependencies
 services:
 
  api:
    build:
      context: .
      dockerfile: Dockerfile
      args:
        VERSION: "1.4.2"
    image: registry.example.com/payments-api:1.4.2
    container_name: payments-api
    restart: unless-stopped
    ports:
      - "8080:8080"
    environment:
      APP_ENV: production
      DB_HOST: db
      DB_PORT: "5432"
    env_file:
      - .env
    depends_on:
      db:
        condition: service_healthy      # wait for the DB healthcheck, not just for it to start
    networks:
      - appnet
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "curl", "-fsS", "http://localhost:8080/health"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 5s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 20s
    deploy:
      resources:
        limits:
          cpus: "1.0"
          memory: 512M
    logging:
      driver: json-file
      options:
        max-size: "10m"
        max-file: "3"
 
  db:

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK

    image: postgres:16-alpine
    restart: unless-stopped
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: payments
      POSTGRES_USER: payments
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE: /run/secrets/db_password
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
