Compose commands
 docker compose up -d                 # build if needed, create and start everything
docker compose up -d --build         # force a rebuild
docker compose ps
docker compose logs -f api           # follow one service
docker compose exec api sh
docker compose stop                  # stop but keep containers
docker compose down                  # stop and REMOVE containers and networks
docker compose down -v               # ...and volumes - DATA LOSS
docker compose restart api
docker compose config                # render the final, merged configuration - use it to debug
docker compose pull                  # refresh images
docker compose up -d --scale api=3   # multiple instances of one service
DEPENDS_ON ALONE DOES NOT WAIT FOR READINESS
Plain depends_on only waits for the container to START, not for the application inside to be ready. A database container
is "started" long before Postgres accepts connections. The fix is condition: service_healthy with a real healthcheck, or
application-level retry logic - which you need anyway, because dependencies restart in production.
7.8 Container Lifecycle and Restart Policies
  created --start--> running --pause--> paused --unpause--> running
                        |                                      |
                        | stop (SIGTERM, grace, SIGKILL)       | kill (SIGKILL)
                        v                                      v
                      exited  --start--> running            exited
                        |
                        | rm
                        v
                     removed
States a container moves through
Restart policy
Behaviour
no (default)
Never restarts
on-failure[:max]
Restart only on a non-zero exit, optionally limited
always
Always restart, including after daemon restart - even if you stopped it manually


===== PAGE 95 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 95
Restart policy
Behaviour
unless-stopped
Like always, but respects a manual stop - usually what you want
Exit code
Meaning
0
Clean exit - the main process finished. A container is not a VM: when PID 1 exits, it is over
1
Generic application error - check docker logs
125
The docker command itself failed (bad flag)
126
Command found but not executable - permissions or wrong architecture
127
Command not found - wrong path, or missing in a slim base image
137
Killed by SIGKILL (128+9) - usually the OOM killer or a docker kill
139
Segmentation fault (128+11)
143
Terminated by SIGTERM (128+15) - a normal docker stop
7.9 Docker Troubleshooting Scenarios
 CONTAINER EXITS IMMEDIATELY
1. docker ps -a - read the exit code and the status.
2. docker logs <container> - the error is almost always here.
3. Exit 0 with no error usually means the main process finished. A container with CMD ["nginx"] exits immediately
because nginx daemonises - it must run in the FOREGROUND (nginx -g "daemon off;"). Same class of bug: a shell
script that ends, or CMD ["bash"] with no TTY.
4. Exit 127 - command not found. The binary is not in the slim base image, or the path is wrong. Check with docker
run --entrypoint sh -it image and look.
5. Exit 126 - not executable, or an architecture mismatch (an amd64 binary on arm64, which is increasingly common
on Apple hardware).
6. Exit 1 - read the application error; usually a missing environment variable or unreachable dependency at startup.
7. To inspect a container that will not stay up: docker run --entrypoint sh -it image to get a shell without running
the app.
CONTAINER RESTARTING IN A LOOP (THE DOCKER EQUIVALENT OF CRASHLOOPBACKOFF)
1. docker ps shows "Restarting (1) 5 seconds ago" - the restart policy is masking a crash.
2. docker logs --tail 100 <name> after each crash; add -f to watch it fail live.
3. Common causes: a dependency such as the database is not reachable yet, a required environment variable is
missing, a config file failed to parse, a port is already in use inside the container, or a permissions error on a mounted
volume.
4. docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}} {{.State.OOMKilled}}' <name> - OOMKilled: true means it exceeded its
memory limit.
5. Temporarily set --restart=no and run it in the foreground to see the failure clearly.
PORT IS NOT ACCESSIBLE FROM OUTSIDE


===== PAGE 96 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 96
1. Is it actually published? docker port <name> and docker ps - look at the PORTS column.
2. What is the app bound to inside? docker exec <name> ss -tulpn - 127.0.0.1:8080 means it will never be reachable;
it must be 0.0.0.0:8080.
3. Is the mapping the right way round? -p HOSTPORT:CONTAINERPORT.
4. Does the container port match what the app really listens on? EXPOSE documents, it does not publish or change
anything.
5. Host firewall / security group open for the host port?
6. Test in layers: docker exec <name> curl localhost:8080 (app is up) -> curl localhost:8080 from the host (mapping
works) -> from another machine (firewall).
CONTAINER CANNOT CONNECT TO THE DATABASE
1. Are they on the same user-defined network? On the default bridge there is no DNS, so DB_HOST=db will not resolve.
docker network inspect appnet to confirm both are attached.
2. Is the app using localhost for the database? Inside a container, localhost is the CONTAINER itself, not the host and
not the DB container. This is the single most common containerisation mistake.
3. To reach a service on the HOST from inside a container: host.docker.internal on Docker Desktop, or the bridge
gateway IP on Linux (ip route | grep default from inside the container).
4. Test from inside: docker exec -it api sh -c "nc -zv db 5432" or getent hosts db.
5. Is the database actually ready? Startup ordering - use healthchecks and retries.
6. Credentials and the database name - check with docker exec api env.
IMAGE BUILD FAILURE
 COPY failed: file not found in build context - the file is outside the context directory, or excluded by
.dockerignore. The context is the path you passed to docker build, and nothing outside it can be copied.
 Network failures during apt-get/npm install - proxy settings, DNS inside the build, or a rate-limited registry. Docker
Hub anonymous pull limits cause this in CI regularly; authenticate or use a pull-through cache.
 A layer that used to work now fails - a mutable base tag moved, or an upstream package version disappeared. Pin
versions.
 Out of disk during build - docker builder prune.
 RUN cd /app having no effect - each RUN is a new layer with its own shell; use WORKDIR.
 Debug by building up to the failing stage: docker build --target builder -t dbg . then docker run -it dbg sh.
IMAGE PULL FAILURE
 pull access denied / repository does not exist - wrong registry path, typo, private repo without login, or the
image was never pushed.
 unauthorized: authentication required - not logged in, or an expired token. For ECR the login token lasts 12 hours:
aws ecr get-login-password | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin
<acct>.dkr.ecr.<region>.amazonaws.com.
 manifest unknown - that tag does not exist. Check whether the push stage actually succeeded.
 toomanyrequests - Docker Hub rate limit. Authenticate, mirror, or use a private registry.
 Timeouts on a private subnet - the node has no route to the registry; it needs a NAT gateway or a VPC endpoint.
 Architecture mismatch - no matching manifest for linux/arm64. Build multi-arch with buildx.
PERMISSION DENIED INSIDE THE CONTAINER


===== PAGE 97 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 97
 A bind-mounted host directory keeps its host UID/GID. If the container runs as UID 10001 and the host directory is
owned by UID 1000, writes fail. Fixes: chown on the host to match, run the container with -u $(id -u):$(id -g), or use
a named volume, which Docker initialises with the right ownership.
 Files created by a root container on a bind mount end up root-owned on the host - which is why CI cannot clean its
workspace afterwards.
 SELinux on RHEL blocks bind mounts unless you append :z or :Z to the volume flag.
 docker: permission denied while trying to connect to the Docker daemon socket on the host means your user is
not in the docker group.
DISK FULL ON A DOCKER HOST
1. docker system df - is it images, containers, volumes or build cache?
2. docker system prune -af --filter "until=72h" for a safe cleanup; add --volumes only when you are certain
nothing needed is unused.
3. Unbounded container logs are a classic cause - the json-file driver grows forever by default. Set max-size and
max-file in /etc/docker/daemon.json so it applies to every container.
4. Exited containers holding writable layers - docker container prune.
5. Set a scheduled prune job on build hosts; on Kubernetes nodes, kubelet garbage collection handles images.
CONTAINER MEMORY ISSUES
 docker stats shows usage against the limit; docker inspect -f '{{.State.OOMKilled}}' confirms a kill.
 Exit code 137 means SIGKILL, which for a memory-limited container almost always means OOM.
 Without --memory, a container can consume all host memory and the kernel OOM killer may pick a different, innocent
process. Always set limits in production.
 A JVM in a container must be told about the limit - modern JVMs read cgroup limits, but older ones see the host's
total RAM and set a heap far larger than the container allows, guaranteeing an OOM kill. -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75 is
the standard fix.
7.10 Docker Interview Questions
Q. 1. What is Docker and what problem does it solve?
 A platform for packaging an application together with its dependencies into an image that runs identically anywhere the
Docker engine runs. It solves environment inconsistency - the "works on my machine" problem - because the runtime,
libraries and configuration travel with the application rather than being installed differently on each server.
Q. 2. Difference between a container and a virtual machine?
 A VM virtualises hardware and runs a full guest operating system with its own kernel, so it is gigabytes in size and takes
minutes to boot. A container virtualises the OS - it is a process on the host kernel, isolated with namespaces and limited with
cgroups - so it is megabytes and starts in under a second. The trade-off is isolation strength: containers share the host
kernel, so a kernel vulnerability is shared.
Q. 3. Difference between an image and a container?
 An image is an immutable, layered template. A container is a running instance of that image with a thin writable layer on
top. Many containers can run from one image; deleting a container deletes its writable layer, which is why persistent data
belongs in a volume.
Q. 4. What is a Docker layer and why does it matter?
 Each filesystem-changing instruction in a Dockerfile creates a read-only layer, cached by content. If nothing above a layer
changed, Docker reuses it. That is why instruction order matters: copying dependency manifests and installing dependencies
before copying source code keeps the expensive layer cached across code changes.
Q. 5. CMD versus ENTRYPOINT?
 CMD provides a default command that is completely replaced if the user supplies one at docker run. ENTRYPOINT defines
the executable that always runs, and any arguments at run time are appended to it. The recommended pattern is
ENTRYPOINT for the binary and CMD for its default arguments.


===== PAGE 98 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 98
Q. 6. What is the difference between exec form and shell form?
 Exec form, CMD ["python","app.py"], runs the process directly as PID 1 so it receives signals and docker stop works
immediately. Shell form, CMD python app.py, wraps it in /bin/sh -c, so the shell is PID 1 and does not forward SIGTERM - the
container then takes the full grace period and gets SIGKILLed. Always prefer exec form.
Q. 7. COPY versus ADD?
 Both copy files into the image, but ADD additionally auto-extracts local tar archives and can download URLs. That implicit
behaviour causes surprises, so the guidance is to use COPY unless you specifically want tar extraction.
Q. 8. What does EXPOSE actually do?
 Nothing functional - it documents which port the application listens on, and it is used by -P to pick ports and by some
tooling. Publishing is done with -p at run time, or by the orchestrator.
Q. 9. What is a multi-stage build and why use it?
 A Dockerfile with several FROM stages where the final stage copies only the built artifacts from earlier stages. The compiler,
build tools, dev dependencies and source never reach the final image, so it is dramatically smaller and has a much smaller
attack surface, while the build still happens in one reproducible step.
Q. 10. How do you reduce image size?
 Multi-stage builds, a slim or alpine base, chaining RUN commands and cleaning package caches within the same layer,
.dockerignore to shrink the build context, avoiding unnecessary packages, and --no-install-recommends. docker history
shows which layer is responsible when an image is unexpectedly large.
Q. 11. What is the difference between a volume and a bind mount?
 A named volume is managed by Docker in its own storage area, is portable between hosts and handles initial permissions
sensibly - it is the right choice for production state. A bind mount maps an arbitrary host path into the container, which is
ideal for mounting source code in development but couples you to the host's filesystem layout and its UID/GID ownership.
Q. 12. How do containers communicate with each other?
 On a user-defined bridge network, by container name - Docker provides embedded DNS. On the default bridge there is no
name resolution, only IPs, which is why you create a network explicitly. Across hosts you would use an overlay network or, in
practice, Kubernetes Services.
Q. 13. Inside a container, what is localhost?
 The container itself. It is not the host and not another container. Connecting to a database on localhost from inside an app
container is the most common containerisation mistake - the database must be addressed by its service name on the shared
network, or via host.docker.internal when it genuinely runs on the host.
Q. 14. What are namespaces and cgroups?
 Namespaces give isolation - separate views of process IDs, network stack, mounts, hostname, IPC and users. Cgroups give
resource control - limits and accounting for CPU, memory, I/O and PIDs. Together they are what makes a container a
container; Docker is the tooling around those kernel features.
Q. 15. How do you limit a container's resources?
 --memory=512m and --cpus=1.5 at run time, or deploy.resources.limits in Compose, or requests and limits in Kubernetes.
Without limits, one container can starve everything else on the host, and the kernel's OOM killer may kill an unrelated
process.
Q. 16. What does exit code 137 mean?
 128 + 9, so SIGKILL. In practice it means the container was killed for exceeding its memory limit, or someone ran docker
kill. docker inspect showing OOMKilled: true confirms it.
Q. 17. How do you debug a container that will not start?
 docker ps -a for the exit code, docker logs for the error, then docker run --entrypoint sh -it <image> to get a shell
without running the failing command, so I can check the filesystem, permissions and whether the binary is even present.
docker inspect gives the final configuration including mounts and environment.
Q. 18. How do you get a shell in a running container?
 docker exec -it <name> bash, or sh for alpine-based images. If the image has no shell at all - a distroless or scratch image -
I use docker debug where available, or run a sidecar/ephemeral container sharing the same namespaces.
Q. 19. Where do container logs go?
 Whatever the main process writes to stdout and stderr is captured by the logging driver - by default json-file on the host.
That is why applications in containers should log to stdout rather than to files: the platform collects it. Unbounded json-file


===== PAGE 99 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 99
logs are a classic disk-full cause, so set max-size and max-file.
Q. 20. How do you pass configuration and secrets to a container?
 Configuration as environment variables or mounted config files. Secrets should never be in the image or in ENV if avoidable,
because docker inspect and image history expose them - use Docker secrets, a mounted tmpfs file, or a secrets manager,
and in Kubernetes a Secret mounted as a file with the appropriate access controls.
Q. 21. Why should a container not run as root?
 Because a container escape or an exploited application then has root on the host namespace, and a root process can write
to bind-mounted host paths as root. Creating an unprivileged user and using USER costs nothing and removes an entire
class of escalation.
Q. 22. What is a distroless image?
 An image containing only the application and its runtime dependencies - no shell, no package manager, no utilities. It
minimises the attack surface and CVE count. The trade-off is that debugging is harder because you cannot exec into a shell.
Q. 23. What is HEALTHCHECK and who uses the result?
 An instruction defining a command that tests whether the application inside is actually working. Docker marks the container
healthy or unhealthy, and Compose's depends_on: condition: service_healthy and various orchestrators act on it.
Kubernetes ignores it - it has its own liveness and readiness probes.
Q. 24. How do you persist database data in Docker?
 A named volume mounted at the database's data directory. In real production I would question running the database in a
container at all on a single host - a managed service such as RDS removes the backup, failover and upgrade burden. If it
must be containerised, it needs a volume, a backup job, and an understanding that the container can be rescheduled.
Q. 25. What happens when you run docker run nginx?
 The CLI sends the request to the daemon; the daemon checks for the image locally and pulls it from the registry if missing; it
creates a container with new namespaces and cgroups and a writable layer; it sets up networking on the bridge and, if
requested, port mappings; then it starts the entrypoint process as PID 1 inside those namespaces and streams its output
back.
Q. 26. What is the build context?
 The directory you pass to docker build, which is sent to the daemon in its entirety before the build begins. That is why a
large context - .git, node_modules, build output - makes builds slow, and why .dockerignore matters. Nothing outside the
context can be COPYed.
Q. 27. How do you version and tag images?
 With an immutable tag derived from the build - the CI build number plus the short Git SHA - and a semantic version for
releases. latest is mutable and should never be what you deploy, because you cannot tell what is running or roll back
precisely. Even better is deploying by digest.
Q. 28. What is the difference between docker stop and docker kill?
 stop sends SIGTERM, waits for a grace period (10 seconds by default), then SIGKILL - so the application can shut down
cleanly. kill sends SIGKILL immediately with no chance to clean up.
Q. 29. How would you scan images for vulnerabilities?
 Trivy, Grype or the registry's own scanning such as ECR scan-on-push, run as a pipeline stage that fails the build on HIGH or
CRITICAL findings. Just as important is rebuilding regularly, because a base image that was clean three months ago is not
clean today.
Q. 30. What is Docker Compose for, and what are its limits?
 Defining and running a multi-container application on a single host from one YAML file - ideal for local development, CI
environments and small deployments. Its limit is exactly that: one host. There is no scheduling across nodes, no self-healing
across machines and no rolling update with readiness gating, which is where Kubernetes takes over.
Q. 31. How do containers get their IP addresses?
 The Docker daemon runs an embedded IPAM that assigns an address from the network's subnet - 172.17.0.0/16 for the
default bridge - and connects the container's veth pair to the bridge. Those addresses are not stable across restarts, which is
why you address containers by name on a user-defined network rather than by IP.
Q. 32. What is the difference between docker run and docker start?
 run creates a NEW container from an image and starts it. start restarts an EXISTING stopped container with its original
configuration. Repeatedly running docker run when you meant start is how hosts accumulate dozens of stopped


===== PAGE 100 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 7  |  DOCKER COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 100
containers eating disk.
Q. 33. How do you copy files in and out of a container?
 docker cp container:/path ./local and the reverse. It is useful for pulling out a log or a heap dump during an incident, but
it is not a deployment mechanism - anything that needs to be in the image belongs in the Dockerfile.
Q. 34. What does docker diff tell you?
 Which files have been added, changed or deleted in the container's writable layer compared with the image. It answers
"what has this container actually written?" - useful for finding state that should have been in a volume, and for spotting a
process writing logs inside the container.
Q. 35. How do you handle time zones and locales in containers?
 Most base images are UTC, which is usually correct - logs and timestamps should be UTC everywhere and converted for
display. If an application genuinely needs a local time zone, set TZ and install tzdata. Mismatched time zones between
containers and hosts make log correlation during an incident much harder.
Q. 36. Your image builds on your laptop but fails in CI. Why?
 Different build context or .dockerignore, a cached layer locally that CI does not have (so a broken step is hidden on your
machine), different architecture - arm64 laptop versus amd64 CI, a mutable base tag that moved between the two builds,
registry authentication or rate limits in CI, or network restrictions on the build agent. --no-cache locally usually reproduces it
immediately.
Q. 37. How would you set up a private registry?
 Use a managed one - ECR, Harbor, Nexus or Artifactory - with authentication, image scanning, retention policies to control
storage, and immutable tags so a tag cannot be overwritten. CI authenticates with a scoped credential; for ECR that is a
short-lived token from get-login-password, which is better than a long-lived secret.
Q. 38. What is the difference between docker system prune and docker system prune -a --volumes?
 The plain form removes stopped containers, unused networks, dangling images and build cache. Adding -a also removes
every image not currently used by a container, and --volumes removes unused volumes, which can destroy data that was
merely not attached at that moment. I use a time filter and I always run docker system df first.
Q. 39. How do you make containers start automatically after a host reboot?
 --restart unless-stopped (or always) so the daemon restarts them, and ensure the Docker service itself is enabled at boot.
For anything that matters, an orchestrator or a systemd unit managing the container is more reliable than the restart policy
alone.
Q. 40. What is the biggest mistake you see people make with Docker?
 Treating a container like a virtual machine - SSHing into it, patching it by hand, storing state inside it, and running multiple
services in one container with a supervisor. Containers should be immutable, single-purpose and disposable: to change
something you rebuild the image and replace the container, and anything that must survive lives in a volume or an external
service.
PART 7 RECALL POINTS
 Image = template, container = running instance with a writable layer.
 Manifest and dependencies BEFORE source, or you destroy the layer cache.
 ENTRYPOINT = the binary, CMD = its default arguments; always exec form.
 EXPOSE documents; -p publishes.
 Default bridge has no DNS - create a user-defined network.
 localhost inside a container is the container.
 137 = OOMKilled; 127 = command not found; 126 = not executable.
 Bind mounts keep host UID/GID - the usual permission-denied cause.
 Never deploy latest; tag with build number + SHA.
 docker system df before any prune.

Part 8. AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
Your CV lists AWS as EC2, S3, IAM, VPC, CloudWatch, Route 53 and Security Groups. That is exactly the right scope
for a 3-year DevOps engineer, and this part goes deep on those services rather than spreading thin across fifty.
Anything outside that list is marked as preparation material - know the concept, never claim the experience.

EC2 operations from the CLI
 aws ec2 describe-instances --filters "Name=instance-state-name,Values=running" \
  --query "Reservations[].Instances[].[InstanceId,InstanceType,PrivateIpAddress,Tags[?Key=='Name'].Value|[0]
    ]" \
  --output table
 
aws ec2 describe-instances --instance-ids i-0abc123
aws ec2 stop-instances --instance-ids i-0abc123
aws ec2 start-instances --instance-ids i-0abc123
aws ec2 reboot-instances --instance-ids i-0abc123
aws ec2 describe-instance-status --instance-ids i-0abc123   # system and instance status checks
aws ec2 get-console-output --instance-id i-0abc123          # boot log when SSH is impossible
aws ec2 create-image --instance-id i-0abc123 --name "app-2026-09-23"
aws ec2 describe-security-groups --group-ids sg-0abc
aws ec2 authorize-security-group-ingress --group-id sg-0abc --protocol tcp --port 443 --cidr 0.0.0.0/0
User data - bootstrapping at first boot
 #!/bin/bash
set -euo pipefail
exec > >(tee /var/log/user-data.log) 2>&1     # capture everything; this log is your first debug stop
 
dnf update -y
dnf install -y docker amazon-cloudwatch-agent
systemctl enable --now docker
usermod -aG docker ec2-user
 
REGION=$(curl -s http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/placement/region)
INSTANCE_ID=$(curl -s http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/instance-id)
echo "Bootstrapping ${INSTANCE_ID} in ${REGION}"
 
aws ecr get-login-password --region "$REGION" | docker login --username AWS --password-stdin "$ECR_REGISTRY"
docker run -d --restart unless-stopped -p 80:8080 "$ECR_REGISTRY/app:$APP_VERSION"
USER DATA RUNS ONCE BY DEFAULT
It executes on first boot only. If it fails, the instance still launches - it just is not configured, which is why a health check
that only tests "is the instance running" is useless. Debug with /var/log/cloud-init-output.log and
/var/log/user-data.log. Note also that user data is visible to anyone who can read instance metadata, so it must never
contain secrets - fetch them at boot from Secrets Manager or SSM Parameter Store using the instance's IAM role.
8.1.1 Auto Scaling
 Component
Role
Launch Template
Defines what to launch: AMI, instance type, security groups, user data, IAM profile
Auto Scaling Group (ASG)
Maintains desired capacity across subnets/AZs, replaces unhealthy instances
Scaling policy
Target tracking (keep average CPU at 60%), step scaling, or scheduled
Health check type
EC2 (is it running) or ELB (is it passing the load balancer's health check) - use ELB
Cooldown / warm-up
Prevents thrashing while a new instance starts
THE ASG ANSWER THAT SHOWS DEPTH
"An ASG gives me two things: elasticity and self-healing. Elasticity is the scaling policy - I prefer target tracking on a
meaningful metric rather than step scaling on raw CPU. Self-healing is the health check - and the important detail is
setting the health check type to ELB rather than EC2, because an instance can be running perfectly while the application
on it is dead. With EC2 health checks the ASG will happily keep a broken instance forever."

Anatomy of an IAM policy
 {
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "ReadAppArtifacts",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:ListBucket"],
      "Resource": [
        "arn:aws:s3:::my-artifacts",
        "arn:aws:s3:::my-artifacts/*"
      ],
      "Condition": {
        "IpAddress": { "aws:SourceIp": "10.0.0.0/16" }
      }
    },
    {
      "Sid": "DenyUnencryptedUploads",
      "Effect": "Deny",
      "Action": "s3:PutObject",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::my-artifacts/*",
      "Condition": {
        "StringNotEquals": { "s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption": "AES256" }
      }
    }
  ]
}
IAM EVALUATION LOGIC - A VERY COMMON QUESTION
An explicit DENY always wins. Otherwise, an explicit ALLOW is required - the default is deny. So the order is: explicit
deny beats everything; then, if any applicable policy allows it, it is allowed; if nothing allows it, it is denied. With
Organizations, an SCP that does not allow an action blocks it even if the IAM policy allows it, because SCPs are a ceiling,
not a grant.
ROLES OVER ACCESS KEYS - THE SECURITY ANSWER
"For anything running on AWS I attach an IAM role rather than creating access keys. The instance gets temporary,
automatically rotated credentials through the metadata service, so there is no long-lived secret to leak, commit to Git
or forget to rotate. Access keys are for things outside AWS, and even then the modern answer is OIDC federation - for
example GitHub Actions assuming a role - so there is still no stored secret."
Least privilege practice
Why
Grant specific actions, not "Action": "*"
Blast radius
Scope Resource to specific ARNs
A policy allowing s3:* on * is effectively admin over all data


===== PAGE 104 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 104
Least privilege practice
Why
Use conditions - source IP, MFA, tags
Contextual restriction
Start from CloudTrail/Access Analyzer findings
Build the policy from what was actually used
Rotate and audit credentials
The IAM credential report lists key age and unused credentials
Enforce MFA on all human users
Especially the root account, which should then be locked away
8.3 VPC - Virtual Private Cloud
                                  INTERNET
                                      |
                            +---------v----------+
                            |  Internet Gateway  |
                            +---------+----------+
   VPC 10.0.0.0/16                    |
  +---------------------------------- | ----------------------------------+
  |  AZ-a                             |                   AZ-b            |
  |  +----------------------------+   |   +----------------------------+  |
  |  | PUBLIC SUBNET 10.0.1.0/24  |<--+-->| PUBLIC SUBNET 10.0.2.0/24  |  |
  |  |  ALB node, NAT Gateway,    |       |  ALB node, NAT Gateway     |  |
  |  |  bastion                   |       |                            |  |
  |  |  route: 0.0.0.0/0 -> IGW   |       |  route: 0.0.0.0/0 -> IGW   |  |
  |  +-------------+--------------+       +--------------+-------------+  |
  |                |                                     |                |
  |  +-------------v--------------+       +--------------v-------------+  |
  |  | PRIVATE APP 10.0.11.0/24   |       | PRIVATE APP 10.0.12.0/24   |  |
  |  |  EC2 / worker nodes        |       |  EC2 / worker nodes        |  |
  |  |  route: 0.0.0.0/0 -> NAT   |       |  route: 0.0.0.0/0 -> NAT   |  |
  |  +-------------+--------------+       +--------------+-------------+  |
  |                |                                     |                |
  |  +-------------v--------------+       +--------------v-------------+  |
  |  | PRIVATE DATA 10.0.21.0/24  |       | PRIVATE DATA 10.0.22.0/24  |  |
  |  |  RDS primary               |       |  RDS standby (Multi-AZ)    |  |
  |  |  NO route to 0.0.0.0/0     |       |  NO route to 0.0.0.0/0     |  |
  |  +----------------------------+       +----------------------------+  |
  +----------------------------------------------------------------------+
The standard production VPC
Layer
Why it exists
Internet Gateway
The only thing that makes a subnet "public" - a subnet is public because its route table sends 0.0.0.0/0
to an IGW, not because of a setting called "public"
Public subnet
Holds only what must be internet-facing: load balancer nodes, NAT gateways, bastion
NAT Gateway
Lets private instances reach the internet outbound for patches and API calls, while remaining
unreachable inbound. One per AZ for high availability - a single NAT gateway is a single point of failure
AND a cross-AZ data charge
Private app subnet
Application servers, never directly reachable from the internet; only the ALB's security group may reach
them
Private data subnet
Databases; no internet route at all, reachable only from the app tier's security group
Two AZs
An AZ is a failure domain. ALB and RDS Multi-AZ both require two subnets in different AZs
VPC component
Purpose
CIDR block
The VPC's address space, e.g. 10.0.0.0/16. Cannot be shrunk later - size it generously
Subnet
A CIDR slice bound to ONE availability zone
Route table
Where traffic goes; associated with subnets
Internet Gateway
Two-way internet for resources with public IPs
NAT Gateway
One-way outbound internet for private resources
Security Group
Stateful, instance-level allow rules
NACL
Stateless, subnet-level allow and deny rules
VPC Endpoint (Gateway)
Private access to S3 and DynamoDB via a route table entry - free, and avoids NAT
charges


===== PAGE 105 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 105
VPC component
Purpose
VPC Endpoint (Interface/PrivateLink)
Private ENI for other AWS services or partner services
VPC Peering
Private one-to-one link between VPCs; not transitive; CIDRs must not overlap
Transit Gateway
Hub for many VPCs and on-premises connections; transitive routing
Flow Logs
Records accepted and rejected traffic - the tool for "why is this being blocked?"
THE S3 GATEWAY ENDPOINT IS A GREAT COST ANSWER
"If instances in private subnets pull large objects from S3 through a NAT gateway, you pay NAT data-processing
charges for traffic that never needed to leave AWS. A gateway VPC endpoint for S3 is free, adds a route, and keeps that
traffic on the AWS network - it improves security and removes the cost at the same time."
8.4 S3 - Simple Storage Service
 Concept
Detail
Bucket
Globally unique name, lives in one region
Object
Key plus data plus metadata, up to 5 TB; use multipart upload above 100 MB
Durability
99.999999999% (11 nines) - designed for durability, not for availability
Consistency
Strong read-after-write for all operations since 2020
Versioning
Keeps every version; protects against overwrite and delete. A delete creates a delete marker
Lifecycle policy
Transition to cheaper classes or expire objects on a schedule
Encryption
SSE-S3 (AES256), SSE-KMS (audited, key-controlled), SSE-C, or client-side
Bucket policy
Resource-based JSON policy - who may do what to this bucket
Block Public Access
Account and bucket-level override that defeats accidental public policies - leave it ON
Pre-signed URL
Time-limited URL granting temporary access without credentials
Storage class
Use
Retrieval
Standard
Frequently accessed
Immediate
Intelligent-Tiering
Unknown or changing patterns
Immediate; moves objects automatically
Standard-IA
Infrequent but needs instant access
Immediate, retrieval fee
One Zone-IA
Re-creatable data
Immediate; lost if that AZ is lost
Glacier Instant Retrieval
Archive, instant access
Immediate
Glacier Flexible
Archive
Minutes to hours
Glacier Deep Archive
Long-term compliance
Up to 12 hours; cheapest
S3 operations
 aws s3 ls
aws s3 ls s3://my-artifacts/releases/ --recursive --human-readable --summarize
aws s3 cp app.jar s3://my-artifacts/releases/1.4.2/app.jar
aws s3 cp s3://my-artifacts/releases/1.4.2/app.jar .
aws s3 sync ./build s3://my-site --delete                 # mirror, removing extras
aws s3 rm s3://my-artifacts/old/ --recursive
aws s3 presign s3://my-artifacts/report.pdf --expires-in 3600
 
aws s3api get-bucket-versioning --bucket my-artifacts
aws s3api put-bucket-versioning --bucket my-artifacts --versioning-configuration Status=Enabled
aws s3api get-bucket-policy --bucket my-artifacts
aws s3api list-object-versions --bucket my-artifacts --prefix releases/
aws s3api head-object --bucket my-artifacts --key releases/1.4.2/app.jar
A lifecycle policy that controls cost
 {
  "Rules": [{
    "ID": "archive-and-expire-logs",
    "Status": "Enabled",


===== PAGE 106 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK

DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation

    "Filter": { "Prefix": "logs/" },
    "Transitions": [
      { "Days": 30,  "StorageClass": "STANDARD_IA" },
      { "Days": 90,  "StorageClass": "GLACIER" }
    ],
    "Expiration": { "Days": 365 },
    "NoncurrentVersionExpiration": { "NoncurrentDays": 30 },
    "AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload": { "DaysAfterInitiation": 7 }
  }]
}
THE TWO S3 DETAILS THAT CATCH PEOPLE OUT
1) Versioning plus no lifecycle rule for noncurrent versions means your bill grows forever - every overwrite keeps the
old object. 2) Incomplete multipart uploads are invisible in the console's object list but you are billed for them; always
add AbortIncompleteMultipartUpload. Mentioning either of these marks you as someone who has actually owned an S3
bill.
8.5 CloudWatch
 Feature
What it is
Use
Metrics
Time-series numeric data, 1-minute (or 1-second
detailed) resolution
CPUUtilization, NetworkIn, ALB TargetResponseTime,
custom metrics
Logs
Log groups containing log streams
Application and system logs via the CloudWatch agent

Operational views

Metric filter

Count ERROR lines and alarm on the rate
MEMORY AND DISK ARE NOT DEFAULT EC2 METRICS
CloudWatch sees the hypervisor's view: CPU, network and disk I/O. It cannot see inside the guest OS, so memory used
and disk space used require the CloudWatch agent to be installed and configured. "Why is there no memory metric for
my EC2 instance?" is a standard interview question and the answer is exactly this.
CloudWatch from the CLI
 aws cloudwatch get-metric-statistics \
  --namespace AWS/EC2 --metric-name CPUUtilization \
  --dimensions Name=InstanceId,Value=i-0abc123 \
  --start-time 2026-09-23T00:00:00Z --end-time 2026-09-23T06:00:00Z \
  --period 300 --statistics Average Maximum
 
aws cloudwatch put-metric-alarm \
  --alarm-name app-high-5xx \
  --namespace AWS/ApplicationELB --metric-name HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count \
  --statistic Sum --period 300 --threshold 50 \
  --comparison-operator GreaterThanThreshold --evaluation-periods 2 \
  --alarm-actions arn:aws:sns:eu-west-1:123456789012:ops-alerts
 
aws logs describe-log-groups
aws logs tail /aws/app/payments --follow --since 10m
aws logs start-query --log-group-name /aws/app/payments \
  --start-time $(date -d '1 hour ago' +%s) --end-time $(date +%s) \
  --query-string 'fields @timestamp, @message | filter @message like /ERROR/ | sort @timestamp desc | limit
    50'

8.8 A Complete Architecture, and Why Each Layer Exists


===== PAGE 108 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 108
   USERS
     |
  [ Route 53 ]        DNS: apex ALIAS -> ALB. Health checks enable regional failover.
     |                WHY: a name that can be repointed without changing clients.
     v
  [ CloudFront ]      (optional) CDN: caches static content at the edge, absorbs spikes,
     |                terminates TLS close to users, provides DDoS buffering.
     v
  [   WAF   ]         Filters malicious requests - SQLi, XSS, bad bots - before the app.
     |
     v
  [   ALB   ]         Public subnets, multiple AZs. Terminates TLS with an ACM cert,
     |                routes by host/path, health-checks targets, spreads load.
     |                WHY: the single entry point, so app servers are never exposed.
     v
  [ ASG: EC2 app ]    Private subnets, multiple AZs. Security group accepts traffic
     |                ONLY from the ALB's security group. Scales on demand,
     |                replaces unhealthy instances automatically.
     |                WHY: elasticity plus self-healing, with no public exposure.
     v
  [ RDS Multi-AZ ]    Private data subnets, no internet route. Synchronous standby in
     |                a second AZ with automatic failover; automated backups.
     |                WHY: the database is the hardest thing to rebuild - protect it most.
     +--> [ ElastiCache ]   Reduces read load and latency.
     +--> [ S3 ]            Static assets, artifacts, backups - via a gateway endpoint.
 
  Cross-cutting: CloudWatch metrics/logs/alarms, CloudTrail audit, IAM roles per tier,
  NAT Gateway per AZ for outbound patching, Secrets Manager for credentials.
Internet to database - the standard three-tier design
"WHY DOES EACH LAYER EXIST?" - THE ANSWER THAT GETS YOU HIRED
"Every layer exists to remove a single point of failure or a class of exposure. Route 53 gives me a stable name and a
failover mechanism. The load balancer is the only public entry point, so the application servers have no public IPs and
cannot be attacked directly. The Auto Scaling Group gives elasticity and self-healing, so losing an instance is not an
incident. Spanning two availability zones means losing an AZ is not an outage. The database sits in a subnet with no
internet route and only accepts connections from the application tier's security group. And NAT gateways let private
instances patch themselves without becoming reachable. If you take any one of those out, you can name exactly which
failure it stops protecting you from."
8.9 AWS Troubleshooting Scenarios
 EC2 UNREACHABLE / SSH FAILURE
1. Instance state and status checks: aws ec2 describe-instance-status. A failed SYSTEM status check is AWS's
hardware - stop and start the instance to move it to new hardware. A failed INSTANCE status check is inside the OS -
check the console output.
2. aws ec2 get-console-output --instance-id i-xxx - the boot log. A bad /etc/fstab entry blocking boot shows here,
and it is the number one cause of "instance is running but nothing responds".
3. Security group: inbound TCP 22 from your IP. NACL: inbound 22 AND outbound on the ephemeral range, because
NACLs are stateless.
4. Route table: does the subnet have a route to the IGW, and does the instance have a public IP or an Elastic IP?
5. Key pair: correct .pem, chmod 600, correct default user - ec2-user, ubuntu, admin or centos depending on the AMI.
6. ssh -v and read where it stops: "Connection timed out" is a network/firewall problem; "Permission denied
(publickey)" means you reached sshd and the key or user is wrong. Never confuse those two.
7. Disk full on the instance can break sshd - the console output or SSM Session Manager will tell you.
8. If SSH is broken beyond repair: use SSM Session Manager, or stop the instance, detach the root volume, attach it to
a working instance, fix the file, and reattach.
HTTP UNAVAILABLE BEHIND AN ALB


===== PAGE 109 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 109
1. Target group health: are ANY targets healthy? An empty or all-unhealthy target group returns 503.
2. If unhealthy, read the reason - "Health checks failed with these codes: [404]" means the health check path is wrong;
a timeout means the security group or the app is not responding.
3. The target's security group must allow the ALB's security group on the target port. Referencing the ALB's SG rather
than a CIDR is the correct pattern.
4. Is the app listening on the port the target group uses, and bound to 0.0.0.0?
5. Listener rules: is there a rule matching this host/path, or is the default action returning a fixed 404?
6. TLS: certificate valid, and attached to the listener? Is the certificate in the same region as the ALB?
7. ALB access logs and the HTTPCode_ELB_5XX versus HTTPCode_Target_5XX metrics tell you whether the ALB or the
application produced the error - that distinction saves a lot of time.
IAM ACCESSDENIED
1. Read the error message - it names the principal, the action and the resource. That is usually 80% of the answer.
2. Use the IAM Policy Simulator, or aws sts get-caller-identity to confirm WHICH identity you are actually using (a
stale profile or environment variable is a common cause).
3. Check for an explicit Deny - in the identity policy, a permissions boundary, an SCP at the organisation level, or a
resource policy. Deny always wins.
4. Is the resource ARN in the policy exactly right? arn:aws:s3:::bucket and arn:aws:s3:::bucket/* are DIFFERENT -
bucket-level actions such as ListBucket need the first, object actions need the second. This is the single most common
S3 policy mistake.
5. KMS: if the object is encrypted with a customer-managed key, the principal also needs kms:Decrypt on that key. The
error still says S3 AccessDenied, which is confusing.
6. Cross-account: both the resource policy and the caller's identity policy must allow it.
7. CloudTrail shows the exact denied call with the full request context.
HIGH CPU ON AN INSTANCE
1. CloudWatch CPUUtilization - is it sustained or spiky, and when did it start? Correlate with a deploy or a traffic
change.
2. On the instance: top with 1 for per-core, then identify the process. High st (steal) on a t-family instance means CPU
credits are exhausted and you are being throttled - the fix is a different instance family or unlimited mode, not
application tuning.
3. High wa means it is really disk - check EBS metrics (VolumeQueueLength, BurstBalance) before blaming the CPU.
4. Decide: scale out through the ASG for a real load increase, or fix the application if a single process is looping.
5. Prevent: a target-tracking scaling policy, plus an alarm before saturation rather than at it.
APPLICATION CANNOT REACH THE INTERNET FROM A PRIVATE SUBNET
1. Does the private subnet's route table have 0.0.0.0/0 -> nat-xxxxx? This is the usual cause.
2. Is the NAT gateway in a PUBLIC subnet with a route to the IGW? A NAT gateway in a private subnet cannot work.
3. Security group outbound rules - default is allow all, but a hardened SG may not be.
4. NACL outbound AND the inbound ephemeral range for the return traffic.
5. Is it only S3 or DynamoDB failing? Then a gateway endpoint's route may be missing or misconfigured.
6. Confirm from the instance: curl -sI https://example.com and dig example.com to separate DNS from routing.
8.10 AWS Interview Questions
Q. 1. What is the difference between a region and an availability zone?
 A region is a geographic area, such as eu-west-1, containing multiple availability zones. An AZ is one or more discrete data
centres with independent power, cooling and networking, connected to the other AZs in the region by low-latency links. You
design across AZs for high availability and across regions for disaster recovery or latency.


===== PAGE 110 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 110
Q. 2. What makes a subnet public?
 Its route table has a route for 0.0.0.0/0 pointing at an Internet Gateway. There is no checkbox called "public" - it is purely the
route, plus the resource having a public IP.
Q. 3. Internet Gateway versus NAT Gateway?
 An IGW provides two-way internet connectivity for resources with public IPs and is what makes a subnet public. A NAT
Gateway provides outbound-only connectivity for private resources - they can reach the internet, but nothing on the
internet can initiate a connection to them.
Q. 4. How many IP addresses does AWS reserve in a subnet?
 Five - the network address, the VPC router, the DNS server, one reserved for future use, and the broadcast address. So a /24
gives 251 usable addresses.
Q. 5. Security group versus NACL?
 Security groups are stateful, operate at the instance/ENI level, support allow rules only, and can reference other security
groups. NACLs are stateless, operate at the subnet level, support allow and deny, and are evaluated in numeric order.
Because NACLs are stateless, you must explicitly allow the ephemeral return ports - which is the cause of many mysterious
timeouts.
Q. 6. Can a security group reference another security group? Why does that matter?
 Yes, and it is the correct pattern for tiering: the database's security group allows 5432 from the application tier's security
group rather than from a CIDR. It then keeps working as instances are replaced and IPs change, and it documents the
intended relationship.
Q. 7. What is an IAM role and when do you use one instead of a user?
 A role is an identity with permissions but no long-lived credentials, assumed temporarily. I use roles for anything running on
AWS - EC2, Lambda, containers - so credentials are temporary and auto-rotated through the metadata service, and for
cross-account access and federated human access. Users with access keys are a last resort for workloads outside AWS.
Q. 8. Explain IAM policy evaluation.
 Default deny. An explicit Deny in any applicable policy always wins. Otherwise the request is allowed only if some policy
explicitly allows it. Service Control Policies act as a ceiling across the account - if an SCP does not permit an action, no IAM
policy can grant it. Permissions boundaries work the same way for an individual principal.
Q. 9. What is the instance metadata service and why does IMDSv2 exist?
 A link-local endpoint at 169.254.169.254 that returns instance identity and the temporary credentials for the attached role.
IMDSv1 was a simple GET, which meant a server-side request forgery vulnerability in an application could be used to read
credentials. IMDSv2 requires a PUT to obtain a session token first and enforces a hop limit, which defeats that class of attack.
Enforcing IMDSv2 is a standard hardening step.
Q. 10. What is the difference between EBS and instance store?
 EBS is network-attached and persists independently of the instance's lifecycle - it survives a stop, and can be snapshotted
and reattached. Instance store is physically attached NVMe: very fast, but the data is lost when the instance stops or
terminates. Anything you care about goes on EBS or in a managed service.
Q. 11. How do you back up EBS?
 Snapshots, which are incremental and stored in S3. In practice I would use Data Lifecycle Manager or AWS Backup to
schedule and retain them automatically rather than relying on a manual process, and I would test a restore, because an
untested backup is a hypothesis.
Q. 12. What happens to data when you stop versus terminate an instance?
 On stop, EBS volumes persist and instance store is lost; the public IPv4 changes unless it is an Elastic IP. On terminate, the
root volume is deleted if DeleteOnTermination is true, which is the default, and the instance is gone permanently.
Q. 13. How do you resize an EC2 instance?
 Stop it, change the instance type, start it again - which means downtime for that instance. That is exactly why you run
behind a load balancer with an Auto Scaling Group: you change the launch template and roll instances, so the resize is not
an outage.
Q. 14. What is an AMI and how do you use one in a DevOps workflow?
 A template containing the root volume snapshot and launch configuration. The immutable infrastructure pattern is to bake
an AMI in the pipeline - with Packer, for example - containing the OS, agents and the application, then roll the ASG onto the
new AMI. Deployment becomes instance replacement and rollback becomes rolling back to the previous AMI.


===== PAGE 111 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 111
Q. 15. What is user data and what are its limits?
 A script that runs at first boot for bootstrapping. Limits: it runs once by default, it is capped at 16 KB, it runs as root, and it is
readable from instance metadata - so it must never contain secrets. Debug it through /var/log/cloud-init-output.log.
Q. 16. How does an Auto Scaling Group decide an instance is unhealthy?
 Either EC2 status checks, or the load balancer's health check if you set the health check type to ELB. ELB health checks are
the right choice because an instance can pass EC2 checks while the application on it is completely broken.
Q. 17. What is a launch template and how does it differ from a launch configuration?
 Both describe what to launch. Launch templates are the current mechanism: they support versioning, mixed instance types
and newer features; launch configurations are immutable, legacy and no longer recommended. Versioning matters because
rolling forward and back is just selecting a template version.
Q. 18. Difference between scaling out and scaling up?
 Scaling out adds more instances - horizontal, near-unbounded, no downtime, requires a stateless application. Scaling up
moves to a larger instance type - vertical, bounded by the largest type, and requires a restart. Cloud architecture prefers
scaling out.
Q. 19. ALB versus NLB?
 ALB is layer 7, routes on host, path and headers, terminates TLS, and suits web applications. NLB is layer 4, handles TCP and
UDP for any protocol, offers static IPs per AZ, preserves the client source IP and handles extreme throughput. For a
non-HTTP protocol such as RTSP, or a static-IP requirement, NLB is the answer.
Q. 20. How does an ALB know a target is healthy?
 It sends a configured health check - a path, port and protocol - at an interval, and moves the target to healthy or unhealthy
after consecutive successes or failures crossing the thresholds. Misconfiguring the path or setting the thresholds too
aggressively is a common self-inflicted outage.
Q. 21. What is connection draining / deregistration delay?
 When a target is removed, the load balancer stops sending it new requests but allows in-flight requests to complete for the
deregistration delay, 300 seconds by default. Without it, a deployment cuts live connections. Setting it too high slows
deployments, so it should roughly match your longest normal request.
Q. 22. What is S3 durability versus availability?
 Durability is the probability that an object survives - 11 nines, achieved by replicating across devices and AZs. Availability is
the probability you can access it at a given moment, which is lower. People conflate them: S3 will almost never lose your
data, but it can have an availability event.
Q. 23. How do you make an S3 bucket private and be sure of it?
 Enable Block Public Access at the account and bucket level - it overrides any bucket policy or ACL that would grant public
access - disable ACLs with Object Ownership set to bucket owner enforced, and write a bucket policy that grants only the
specific principals needed. Then verify with IAM Access Analyzer rather than by eye.
Q. 24. What is S3 versioning and what does it cost you?
 Every overwrite or delete keeps the previous version, protecting against accidental loss, with deletes creating a delete
marker. The cost is that storage grows indefinitely unless you add a lifecycle rule to expire noncurrent versions - a very
common bill surprise.
Q. 25. How would you serve a static website with low latency globally?
 S3 for storage with the bucket private, CloudFront in front with Origin Access Control so the bucket is only readable by the
distribution, an ACM certificate on CloudFront, and Route 53 with an ALIAS at the apex. Caching at the edge handles latency
and traffic spikes, and invalidations handle deployments.
Q. 26. What is a pre-signed URL?
 A time-limited URL that grants temporary access to a specific object without the recipient having AWS credentials. It is the
standard way to let a user upload or download a private object directly, keeping large transfers off your application servers.
Q. 27. Why can't CloudWatch show memory usage on EC2 by default?
 Because CloudWatch collects metrics from the hypervisor, which sees CPU, network and disk I/O but has no visibility into the
guest operating system. Memory and disk space used are inside the OS, so you install the CloudWatch agent to publish
them as custom metrics.
Q. 28. What is a CloudWatch alarm and what states does it have?


===== PAGE 112 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 112
A rule evaluating a metric against a threshold over a number of periods. States are OK, ALARM and INSUFFICIENT_DATA.
Actions can notify SNS, trigger Auto Scaling, or recover an instance. Configuring what happens on INSUFFICIENT_DATA
matters - it is often the state you get when the thing you are monitoring has died entirely.
Q. 29. How do you get application logs into CloudWatch?
 Install and configure the CloudWatch agent to tail the log files into a log group, or have the application write to stdout and
let the container platform's log driver forward it. Then use metric filters to turn patterns such as ERROR into metrics you can
alarm on, and Logs Insights to query during an incident.
Q. 30. What is CloudTrail and how is it different from CloudWatch?
 CloudTrail records API calls - who did what, from where, when - for audit and forensics. CloudWatch records operational
telemetry - metrics and logs about how things are performing. During an incident I use CloudWatch to see what is broken
and CloudTrail to see what someone changed.
Q. 31. What is the difference between a CNAME and an ALIAS record in Route 53?
 A CNAME points a name at another name and cannot be used at the zone apex. An ALIAS is an AWS extension that behaves
like a CNAME but is resolved server-side, works at the apex, can point at AWS resources such as ALBs and CloudFront
distributions, and is not charged for queries.
Q. 32. How would you do a blue-green deployment on AWS?
 Two target groups behind one ALB. Deploy green, health-check and smoke-test it directly through a test listener, then
switch the production listener's default action to the green target group. Rollback is switching back. Alternatively, weighted
target groups let you shift 10% first, which turns it into a canary. At the DNS level, weighted Route 53 records do the same
thing more slowly and with the TTL caveat.
Q. 33. What is the difference between horizontal scaling of the app tier and the database?
 The application tier is usually stateless, so adding instances behind a load balancer is straightforward. A relational database
is stateful: you scale reads with read replicas and scale writes vertically or by sharding, which is a significant architectural
change. That asymmetry is why the database is normally the first bottleneck.
Q. 34. What is RDS Multi-AZ and how is it different from a read replica?
 Multi-AZ maintains a synchronous standby in another AZ purely for availability - you cannot read from it, and failover is
automatic via a DNS change. A read replica is asynchronous and exists to serve read traffic, and it can be promoted
manually. Multi-AZ is for availability; read replicas are for scale.
Q. 35. How do you store database credentials for an application on EC2?
 In Secrets Manager or SSM Parameter Store as a SecureString, retrieved at runtime using the instance's IAM role, with the
KMS key permission scoped to that role. Secrets Manager adds managed rotation. What I would not do is put them in user
data, environment variables baked into an AMI, or a file in the repository.
Q. 36. What is a VPC endpoint and why use one?
 A private connection to an AWS service that keeps traffic on the AWS network. Gateway endpoints for S3 and DynamoDB
are free and work through route tables; interface endpoints create an ENI in your subnet for other services. They improve
security by removing the need for internet egress, and they cut NAT gateway data-processing costs.
Q. 37. Two VPCs are peered but cannot communicate. What do you check?
 Route tables on both sides must have routes to the other VPC's CIDR - peering does not create routes automatically.
Security groups and NACLs on both sides. Overlapping CIDRs, which make peering impossible. And remember peering is
not transitive: A-B and B-C does not give A-C.
Q. 38. How do you reduce AWS cost without reducing reliability?
 Right-size instances based on actual CloudWatch metrics rather than guesses; use Savings Plans or Reserved Instances for
steady baseline load and Spot for interruptible work such as CI agents; S3 lifecycle policies and expiring noncurrent versions;
delete unattached EBS volumes and old snapshots; release unused Elastic IPs; consolidate NAT gateways carefully while
keeping AZ redundancy in mind; use S3 gateway endpoints to avoid NAT data charges; and set budgets and anomaly
detection so surprises are caught early.
Q. 39. What is the AWS shared responsibility model?
 AWS is responsible for security OF the cloud - the hardware, the hypervisor, the physical facilities and managed service
infrastructure. The customer is responsible for security IN the cloud - IAM, security groups, patching guest operating
systems, encryption choices, and application security. The boundary moves with the service: for EC2 you patch the OS, for
RDS AWS does, but you still own access control.
Q. 40. How would you handle secrets in a CI/CD pipeline deploying to AWS?


===== PAGE 113 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 113
Ideally no stored secrets at all: GitHub Actions or Jenkins assumes an IAM role via OIDC, receiving short-lived credentials.
Where that is not possible, an access key scoped tightly to the deployment actions, stored in the CI credential store, rotated
regularly, with CloudTrail monitoring its use.
Q. 41. What is an Elastic IP and when should you avoid it?
 A static public IPv4 allocated to your account. You avoid it when a DNS name would do, because Elastic IPs are a limited
resource, you are charged for unattached ones, and pinning to an IP encourages designs that break when instances are
replaced. Legitimate uses are NAT gateways and whitelisting requirements where a partner needs a fixed source IP.
Q. 42. How do you patch a fleet of EC2 instances?
 Preferably by replacement - bake a new AMI with the patches and roll the ASG, so instances are immutable. Where in-place
patching is necessary, Systems Manager Patch Manager with maintenance windows and patch baselines gives controlled,
auditable patching without SSH access.
Q. 43. What is Systems Manager Session Manager and why is it better than SSH?
 It gives shell access through the SSM agent and the AWS API, so there is no open inbound port 22, no bastion host and no
SSH keys to manage, and every session is logged to CloudTrail and optionally recorded to S3. It removes an entire attack
surface, which is why it is now the recommended access pattern.
Q. 44. How would you design for disaster recovery?
 Start with the business requirements - RTO, how quickly you must recover, and RPO, how much data you can afford to lose.
Then choose the pattern: backup and restore for the cheapest and slowest, pilot light with a minimal standby, warm standby
with a scaled-down running copy, or active-active multi-region for near-zero RTO. The critical part is testing the failover
regularly, because an untested DR plan does not work.
Q. 45. What is the difference between a snapshot and an AMI?
 A snapshot is a point-in-time backup of a single EBS volume. An AMI is a launchable template that references one or more
snapshots plus the metadata needed to boot - block device mapping, architecture, virtualisation type.
Q. 46. Your EC2 instance is running but the website is down. Walk me through it.
 First, is it the instance or the path to it? I check the ALB target group health - if the target is unhealthy, the ALB is doing its
job and the application is the problem. On the instance, systemctl status for the service, ss -tulpn to confirm it is listening
and on which address, then application logs. If the service is fine, I work outward: security group, target group port, listener
rule, DNS. In parallel I check the boring causes - df -h for a full disk and free -h for memory, because both silently break
services. And I ask what changed in the last hour.
Q. 47. What is the difference between Application Auto Scaling and EC2 Auto Scaling?
 EC2 Auto Scaling manages the number of EC2 instances in an ASG. Application Auto Scaling is the generic service that scales
other resources - ECS services, DynamoDB capacity, Aurora replicas - using the same target-tracking concepts.
Q. 48. What is an SCP and how does it interact with IAM?
 A Service Control Policy applied at the AWS Organizations level, acting as a permissions ceiling for every principal in an
account, including the root user. It can only restrict, never grant. So if an SCP denies an action, the most permissive IAM
policy in the world will not allow it - and that is exactly the confusion behind many AccessDenied tickets.
Q. 49. How do you monitor and alert on AWS costs?
 AWS Budgets with alerts at percentage thresholds, Cost Explorer for trend analysis, Cost Anomaly Detection for unexpected
spikes, and consistent resource tagging so cost can be attributed to a team or environment. Tagging discipline is the part
that usually fails, and without it cost allocation is guesswork.
Q. 50. What is the difference between SNS and SQS?
 SNS is publish-subscribe: one message is pushed to many subscribers. SQS is a queue: messages are pulled by consumers
and each message is processed once. A common pattern is SNS fanning out to several SQS queues so each consumer gets
its own durable buffer. SQS also gives you retries and a dead-letter queue, which is why it is used to decouple components.
Q. 51. What happens if you delete a NAT gateway?
 Every private subnet whose route table pointed at it loses outbound internet access immediately - package installs, API calls
and image pulls start failing with timeouts. Since a NAT gateway is also charged hourly, people do delete them to save
money and then cannot work out why the application "randomly" cannot reach external services.
Q. 52. How do you give a pod or an application on EC2 access to S3 without keys?
 An IAM role - an instance profile for EC2, or IAM Roles for Service Accounts on EKS. The SDK automatically retrieves
temporary credentials from the metadata service or the token file, so there is nothing to store or rotate.


===== PAGE 114 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 8  |  AWS COMPLETE DEVOPS GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 114
Q. 53. What is CloudFormation and how does it compare with Terraform?
 CloudFormation is AWS's native IaC service using JSON or YAML templates, with managed state, drift detection and rollback
on failure, but it only manages AWS. Terraform is provider-agnostic with its own state file and a strong module ecosystem,
and it can manage AWS alongside other providers in one workflow. I use Terraform, and the main practical difference is that
I own the state file and its locking, whereas CloudFormation manages that for me.
Q. 54. How would you enforce tagging?
 Tag policies in Organizations, SCPs that deny resource creation without required tags, Config rules that flag non-compliant
resources, and enforcing tags in the Terraform modules themselves with default_tags on the provider so nothing is created
untagged in the first place. Enforcement in the module is the one that actually works, because it is the path of least
resistance.
Q. 55. What is the difference between vertical and horizontal scaling of RDS?
 Vertical is changing the instance class, which requires a brief failover or downtime. Horizontal for reads is adding read
replicas and directing read traffic at them, which requires the application to distinguish reads from writes. Writes cannot be
scaled horizontally without sharding or moving to a distributed engine such as Aurora.
Q. 56. Your S3 upload fails with AccessDenied but the IAM policy looks correct. What else?
 The bucket policy may explicitly deny. Block Public Access may be interfering if you are trying to set an ACL. The bucket may
require server-side encryption with a specific header, and a Deny condition rejects uploads without it. If it is a
KMS-encrypted bucket, the principal also needs kms:GenerateDataKey. And check that the resource ARN includes /* for
object-level actions. Finally, confirm which identity is actually being used with aws sts get-caller-identity.
Q. 57. What are CloudWatch Logs Insights used for?
 Querying log groups with a purpose-built query language - filtering, parsing fields, aggregating and sorting - without
exporting to another system. During an incident it lets me answer "how many 500s by endpoint in the last hour?" in
seconds, which is otherwise a grep across many instances.
Q. 58. How do you handle an AWS service limit being hit?
 Recognise the symptom - errors mentioning limit exceeded or throttling. Check Service Quotas for the current limit and
usage, request an increase, and in the meantime work around it. The real lesson is proactive: monitor quota usage for things
that grow, such as Elastic IPs, VPCs and instances per region, because hitting a limit mid-incident is a bad time to discover it.
Q. 59. What is the purpose of a bastion host and what replaces it today?
 A hardened jump box in a public subnet as the only SSH entry point to private instances. Today it is largely replaced by SSM
Session Manager, which removes the open port, the key management and the instance to patch, while adding full session
logging.
Q. 60. If you could make one change to improve the security of a typical AWS account, what would it be?
 Eliminate long-lived IAM access keys - move workloads to roles and human access to federated SSO with MFA - because
leaked keys are the most common root cause of real AWS breaches. Close behind that: enable CloudTrail in all regions with
log file validation, and turn on Block Public Access for S3 at the account level, because those two cover the next most
common failure modes.
PART 8 RECALL POINTS
 A subnet is public because its route table points 0.0.0.0/0 at an IGW.
 AWS reserves 5 IPs per subnet.
 Security groups stateful and can reference other SGs; NACLs stateless.
 Roles, not access keys - IMDSv2 to protect them.
 Explicit Deny wins; SCPs are a ceiling.
 arn:...:bucket versus arn:...:bucket/* - the classic S3 policy bug.
 CloudWatch cannot see memory or disk usage without the agent.
 ALIAS at the apex, not CNAME.
 ASG health check type should be ELB, not EC2.
 Instance store is ephemeral; EBS persists.

PART 9

HCL, state, modules, workspaces, CI/CD, drift, 40 questions
Part 9. TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
Your CV claims Terraform, Terraform modules and infrastructure provisioning in both roles, plus a named project:
"Built reusable Terraform modules to provision AWS infrastructure, enabling repeatable environment creation and
reducing manual configuration." Expect the interviewer to test state, modules and the plan/apply lifecycle hardest -
those are where real experience shows.
9.1 Terraform Architecture
  YOUR CODE (.tf)          TERRAFORM CORE                 PROVIDER            CLOUD API
  +-------------+         +----------------+          +-------------+      +-----------+
  | main.tf     |  parse  | build resource |  CRUD    | AWS provider|      |  AWS      |
  | variables.tf| ------> | dependency     | -------> | (plugin)    |----->|  API      |
  | outputs.tf  |         | graph          |          +-------------+      +-----------+
  +-------------+         +-------+--------+
                                  | compare
                          +-------v--------+
                          |  STATE FILE    |  desired state (code) vs recorded state (tfstate)
                          | terraform.tfstate|  vs real state (refreshed from the API)
                          +----------------+
                                  |
                          PLAN = the difference    ->   APPLY = make reality match
How an apply actually works
Concept
Definition
Provider
A plugin that knows how to talk to a platform's API - aws, azurerm, kubernetes, github
Resource
An infrastructure object Terraform creates and manages
Data source
A read-only lookup of something Terraform does NOT manage
Variable
Input to a configuration or module
Local
A named expression, computed once, used for readability
Output
A value exported from a configuration or module
Module
A reusable, parameterised group of resources
State
Terraform's record of which real resources correspond to which configuration addresses
Backend
Where state is stored - local, S3, Terraform Cloud
Provisioner
A last-resort escape hatch to run scripts - avoid
9.2 HCL - The Building Blocks
providers.tf - pin everything
 terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.6.0"
 
  required_providers {
    aws = {
      source  = "hashicorp/aws"
      version = "~> 5.40"          # allows 5.x, blocks 6.0 - pessimistic constraint
    }
  }
 
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "acme-terraform-state"
    key            = "prod/network/terraform.tfstate"
    region         = "eu-west-1"
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"
    encrypt        = true

provider "aws" {

variables.tf - typed, validated, documented
 variable "region" {
  description = "AWS region to deploy into"
  type        = string
  default     = "eu-west-1"
}
 
variable "environment" {
  description = "Environment name"
  type        = string
 
  validation {
    condition     = contains(["dev", "staging", "prod"], var.environment)
    error_message = "environment must be one of dev, staging, prod."
  }
}
 
variable "instance_count" {
  description = "Number of application instances"
  type        = number
  default     = 2
}
 
variable "subnet_cidrs" {
  description = "CIDR blocks for the application subnets"
  type        = list(string)
  default     = ["10.0.11.0/24", "10.0.12.0/24"]
}
 
variable "instance_config" {
  description = "Per-environment instance settings"
  type = object({
    type          = string
    volume_size   = number
    monitoring    = bool
  })
  default = {
    type        = "t3.micro"
    volume_size = 20
    monitoring  = false
  }
}
 
variable "db_password" {
  description = "Database master password"
  type        = string
  sensitive   = true          # redacted from plan output and CLI display
}
main.tf - resources, data sources, locals, meta-arguments
 locals {
  name_prefix = "${var.project}-${var.environment}"
 
  common_tags = {
    Name = local.name_prefix
  }

# Data source: look up something we do NOT manage
data "aws_ami" "amazon_linux" {
  most_recent = true
  owners      = ["amazon"]
 
  filter {
    name   = "name"
    values = ["al2023-ami-*-x86_64"]
  }

data "aws_availability_zones" "available" {
  state = "available"
}
 
resource "aws_vpc" "main" {
  cidr_block           = var.vpc_cidr
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  enable_dns_support   = true
 
  tags = merge(local.common_tags, { Name = "${local.name_prefix}-vpc" })
}
 
# for_each over a map: stable addressing by key
resource "aws_subnet" "private" {
  for_each = {
    for idx, cidr in var.subnet_cidrs :
    data.aws_availability_zones.available.names[idx] => cidr
  }
 
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.main.id
  cidr_block        = each.value
  availability_zone = each.key
 
  tags = merge(local.common_tags, { Name = "${local.name_prefix}-private-${each.key}" })
}
 
resource "aws_security_group" "app" {
  name_prefix = "${local.name_prefix}-app-"
  description = "Application tier"
  vpc_id      = aws_vpc.main.id
 
  dynamic "ingress" {
    for_each = var.allowed_ports
    content {
      from_port       = ingress.value
      to_port         = ingress.value
      protocol        = "tcp"
      security_groups = [aws_security_group.alb.id]
    }
  }
 
  egress {
    from_port   = 0
    to_port     = 0
    protocol    = "-1"
    cidr_blocks = ["0.0.0.0/0"]
  }
 
  lifecycle {
    create_before_destroy = true    # avoids "resource in use" when replacing
  }
}
 
resource "aws_instance" "app" {
  count = var.instance_count       # count: simple numeric repetition
 
  ami                    = data.aws_ami.amazon_linux.id
  instance_type          = var.instance_config.type
  subnet_id              = values(aws_subnet.private)[count.index % length(aws_subnet.private)].id

  vpc_security_group_ids = [aws_security_group.app.id]
  monitoring             = var.instance_config.monitoring
 
  root_block_device {
    volume_size = var.instance_config.volume_size
    volume_type = "gp3"
    encrypted   = true
  }
 
  user_data = templatefile("${path.module}/templates/userdata.sh.tftpl", {
    environment = var.environment
    app_version = var.app_version

    ignore_changes = [ami]     # do not replace instances just because a new AMI was published

  depends_on = [aws_nat_gateway.main]    # explicit dependency where it is not implied

  value = [for s in aws_subnet.private : s.id]
}

  value = aws_instance.app[*].private_ip     # splat expression
}

  sensitive = true

9.2.1 count vs for_each - a frequent question
 count
for_each
Input
A number
A map or a set of strings
Address
aws_instance.app[0]
aws_instance.app["web-a"]
Removing an
item
Re-indexes everything after it - Terraform destroys and
recreates unrelated resources
Only the removed key is affected
Best for
Truly identical, interchangeable copies; conditional creation
with count = var.enabled ? 1 : 0
Anything with a natural identity - subnets per AZ, users,
buckets
THE COUNT RE-INDEX TRAP
With count, removing the first element of a list of three shifts index 1 to 0 and 2 to 1, so Terraform plans to destroy and
recreate resources that did not actually change. With for_each, each resource is keyed by a stable name, so removing
one touches only that one. Prefer for_each for anything with identity. Interviewers use this to separate people who have
read the docs from people who have been burned.
9.3 The Command Workflow
The lifecycle, in the order you will use it
 terraform init                    # download providers, configure the backend, install modules
terraform init -upgrade           # update providers within their constraints
terraform init -reconfigure       # change backend without migrating state
terraform init -migrate-state     # move existing state to a new backend
