DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 119
 
terraform fmt -recursive          # canonical formatting - run it in CI as a check
terraform validate                # syntax and internal consistency (no API calls)
 
terraform plan                    # show what WOULD change
terraform plan -out=tfplan        # save the plan - what CI should apply
terraform plan -var-file=prod.tfvars
terraform plan -target=aws_instance.app     # limit scope - emergency use only
terraform plan -destroy                     # preview a destroy
 
terraform apply tfplan            # apply the SAVED plan - no re-evaluation, no surprises
terraform apply -auto-approve     # CI only, and only after a reviewed plan
terraform destroy                 # tear everything down
 
terraform output                  # all outputs
terraform output -raw vpc_id      # unquoted, for scripting
terraform output -json
 
terraform show                    # human-readable current state
terraform show -json tfplan       # machine-readable plan, for policy checks
terraform graph | dot -Tpng > graph.png
State commands - handle with care
 terraform state list                              # every managed resource address
terraform state show aws_instance.app[0]          # attributes of one resource
terraform state mv aws_instance.web aws_instance.app    # rename without destroying
terraform state rm aws_instance.legacy            # stop managing it (does NOT delete the real resource)
terraform state pull > backup.tfstate             # always back up before surgery
terraform state push backup.tfstate               # last resort
 
terraform import aws_instance.app i-0abc123def    # adopt an existing resource into state
terraform refresh                                 # deprecated; use: terraform apply -refresh-only
 
terraform force-unlock <LOCK_ID>                  # ONLY when you are certain no apply is running
terraform taint aws_instance.app[0]               # legacy; modern: terraform apply -replace=...
terraform apply -replace="aws_instance.app[0]"    # force recreation of one resource
9.4 Terraform State - Explained Deeply
State is a JSON file mapping each configuration address (aws_instance.app[0]) to the real resource (i-0abc123), along with
the attribute values Terraform last saw. Without it, Terraform cannot tell the difference between "create this" and "this
already exists".
State does
Why it is needed
Maps config addresses to real resource IDs
Terraform has no other way to know which instance is aws_instance.app[0]
Stores attribute values
Enables diffing without querying every attribute
Tracks dependencies
Correct destroy ordering even after the code is deleted
Caches metadata
Performance on large configurations
Holds outputs
So other configurations can consume them via terraform_remote_state
STATE IS A SECRET
The state file contains every attribute of every resource in plaintext - including database passwords, private keys and
any variable marked sensitive. sensitive = true only hides it from CLI output, not from state. Therefore: encrypt the
backend, restrict access with IAM, never commit .tfstate to Git, and add *.tfstate* to .gitignore. This is one of the
most important things you can say about Terraform in an interview.
9.4.1 Remote state and locking
The standard AWS backend
 terraform {
  backend "s3" {
    bucket         = "acme-terraform-state"
    key            = "prod/app/terraform.tfstate"    # one key per environment/component
    region         = "eu-west-1"


===== PAGE 120 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 9  |  TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 120
    encrypt        = true
    dynamodb_table = "terraform-locks"               # state locking
  }
}
Backend feature
Why
S3 with versioning
Durable, and you can roll back to a previous state version after a bad apply
encrypt = true plus SSE-KMS
State contains secrets
DynamoDB table for locking
Prevents two engineers or two pipeline runs applying at once and corrupting state
Separate key per environment
Blast radius - a mistake in dev cannot destroy prod
Bucket policy denying delete
Protects against accidental state loss
WHY LOCKING MATTERS
Two simultaneous applies both read the same state, both make changes, and the second write overwrites the first - so
Terraform's record no longer matches reality, and the next plan proposes to destroy resources it has "lost".
DynamoDB-based locking makes the second apply wait. If a process crashes mid-apply the lock persists, which is what
force-unlock is for - and you only use it after confirming nothing is actually running.
9.4.2 Drift
Drift is when reality no longer matches state - somebody changed a security group in the console, or an autoscaling
process altered something.
Detecting and handling drift
 terraform plan                       # any unexpected change is drift
terraform plan -refresh-only         # show ONLY what changed outside Terraform
terraform apply -refresh-only        # accept reality into state without changing infrastructure
# Then decide: codify the change, or re-apply to revert reality back to the code.
THE DRIFT ANSWER INTERVIEWERS WANT
"Drift happens when someone changes infrastructure outside Terraform. I detect it by running terraform plan on a
schedule in CI and alerting if the plan is non-empty. Then it is a decision, not an automatic action: if the manual change
was correct, I codify it and use -refresh-only to reconcile; if it was not, I re-apply to bring reality back to the code. The
real fix is preventing it - remove or restrict console write access in production, so Terraform is the only path."
9.5 Modules
Standard module layout
 modules/
  vpc/
    main.tf          # the resources
    variables.tf     # inputs
    outputs.tf       # exported values
    versions.tf      # provider constraints
    README.md        # what it does, inputs, outputs, example
environments/
  dev/
    main.tf
    terraform.tfvars
    backend.tf
  prod/
    main.tf
    terraform.tfvars
    backend.tf
modules/vpc/main.tf - a reusable module
 variable "name"        { type = string }
variable "cidr_block"  { type = string }
variable "az_count"    { type = number, default = 2 }
variable "enable_nat"  { type = bool,   default = true }
variable "tags"        { type = map(string), default = {} }

data "aws_availability_zones" "this" { state = "available" }
 
locals {
  azs = slice(data.aws_availability_zones.this.names, 0, var.az_count)
}
 
resource "aws_vpc" "this" {
  cidr_block           = var.cidr_block
  enable_dns_hostnames = true
  tags                 = merge(var.tags, { Name = var.name })
}
 
resource "aws_subnet" "public" {
  for_each          = toset(local.azs)
  vpc_id            = aws_vpc.this.id
  cidr_block        = cidrsubnet(var.cidr_block, 8, index(local.azs, each.key))
  availability_zone = each.key
  map_public_ip_on_launch = true
  tags              = merge(var.tags, { Name = "${var.name}-public-${each.key}", Tier = "public" })
}
 
output "vpc_id"            { value = aws_vpc.this.id }
output "public_subnet_ids" { value = [for s in aws_subnet.public : s.id] }
environments/prod/main.tf - consuming the module
 module "network" {
  source = "../../modules/vpc"            # local path
  # source = "git::https://github.com/acme/tf-modules.git//vpc?ref=v1.4.0"   # pinned git
  # source = "terraform-aws-modules/vpc/aws"                                  # registry
  # version = "5.5.1"
 
  name       = "acme-prod"
  cidr_block = "10.0.0.0/16"
  az_count   = 3
  tags       = { Environment = "prod" }
}
 
module "app" {
  source = "../../modules/app-tier"
 
  name              = "acme-prod-app"
  vpc_id            = module.network.vpc_id       # module outputs wire modules together
  subnet_ids        = module.network.private_subnet_ids
  instance_count    = 6
  instance_type     = "m5.large"
}
Module practice
Reason
Pin the source version (?ref=v1.4.0)
An unpinned module can change under you and alter every environment
No hardcoded values inside a module
That is what makes it reusable
No provider block inside a module
Providers are configured by the root and inherited; declaring them in a module
breaks composition
Outputs for everything a caller might need
Otherwise callers reach into internals or duplicate lookups
README with inputs, outputs and an example
Modules without docs do not get reused
Keep modules small and single-purpose
A module that creates "everything" is just a configuration with extra steps
"TELL ME ABOUT THE TERRAFORM MODULES YOU BUILT"
Tie it to your CV wording and keep it mechanical: "I built reusable modules so that creating an environment was
supplying variable values rather than writing new resource blocks. The module takes inputs like name, CIDR, AZ count
and instance sizing, creates the resources consistently with tagging applied centrally, and exports the IDs that other
modules need - so the app module consumes the network module's outputs rather than hardcoding subnet IDs. The
point was repeatability: the same code produces dev and production with different variables, so environments do not
drift apart, and a change to the pattern is made once in the module rather than in every environment."

9.6 Workspaces and Environment Separation
Workspaces
 terraform workspace list
terraform workspace new staging
terraform workspace select prod
terraform workspace show
# In code: terraform.workspace  -> "prod"
Approach
Pros
Cons
Workspaces
One codebase, one backend key prefix, quick to switch
Same code for every environment; easy to apply to the
wrong one - terraform workspace show is not a
safety mechanism
Directory per
environment
Explicit separation, separate state keys,
per-environment variables and backends, different
code where needed
Some duplication
Terragrunt / wrapper
DRY plus explicit separation
Another tool to learn
THE SAFE ANSWER
"Workspaces are fine for lightweight variations, but for dev versus production I prefer separate directories with separate
state keys and separate backends. The reason is blast radius and human error: with workspaces, a forgotten workspace
select applies dev changes to production, and nothing about the command you type tells you which one you are on."
9.7 Terraform in CI/CD
  PULL REQUEST                                    MERGE TO MAIN
       |                                                |
  terraform fmt -check           fails on formatting    |
  terraform init                                        |
  terraform validate                                    |
  tfsec / checkov                policy + security gate |
  terraform plan -out=tfplan                            |
       |                                                |
  post the plan as a PR comment  <- HUMAN REVIEWS THIS  |
       |                                                v
  approve + merge --------------------------------> terraform apply tfplan
                                                        |
                                                   (prod: manual approval gate first)
The standard pipeline
GitHub Actions - plan on PR, apply on merge
 name: terraform
on:
  pull_request:
    paths: ["infra/**"]
  push:
    branches: [main]
    paths: ["infra/**"]
 
permissions:
  id-token: write          # OIDC - no stored AWS keys
  contents: read
  pull-requests: write
 
jobs:
  terraform:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: infra/prod
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
 
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:


===== PAGE 123 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 9  |  TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 123
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/github-terraform
          aws-region: eu-west-1
 
      - uses: hashicorp/setup-terraform@v3
        with:
          terraform_version: 1.7.5
 
      - run: terraform fmt -check -recursive
      - run: terraform init
      - run: terraform validate
 
      - name: Plan
        id: plan
        run: terraform plan -out=tfplan -no-color
 
      - name: Apply
        if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main' && github.event_name == 'push'
        run: terraform apply -auto-approve tfplan
WHY YOU APPLY A SAVED PLAN
terraform apply with no plan file re-evaluates everything at apply time, so what it does may differ from the plan a
human reviewed minutes earlier. terraform plan -out=tfplan followed by terraform apply tfplan guarantees that
exactly what was reviewed is what runs. In an interview this is the answer that shows you have run Terraform in a
pipeline rather than only on a laptop.
9.8 What Actually Happens During terraform apply
Step
What Terraform does
1. Load
Parses all .tf files in the directory into a configuration
2. Init check
Verifies providers and modules are installed and the backend is configured
3. Lock
Acquires the state lock (DynamoDB) so no one else can apply concurrently
4. Read state
Downloads the current state from the backend
5. Refresh
Queries the provider API for the real current attributes of managed resources
6. Build graph
Constructs a dependency graph from implicit references and depends_on
7. Diff
Compares desired (code) with actual (refreshed state) to produce the plan
8. Execute
Walks the graph, creating, updating or destroying, in parallel where dependencies allow (10 by default)
9. Write state
Updates the state file after each resource operation
10. Unlock
Releases the lock and prints outputs
Plan symbol
Meaning
+
create
-
destroy
~
update in place
-/+
destroy and recreate - forced by an immutable attribute change
<=
read (data source)
ALWAYS READ THE PLAN FOR -/+
An in-place update is usually safe; a destroy-and-recreate on a database, an EBS volume or an Elastic IP is an outage.
The plan tells you why: "forces replacement" appears next to the attribute responsible. Anyone who runs terraform
apply -auto-approve on production without reading the plan will eventually delete something important - and
interviewers ask this specifically.
9.9 Terraform Interview Questions


===== PAGE 124 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 9  |  TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 124
Q. 1. What is Terraform and why use it?
 An infrastructure-as-code tool that provisions infrastructure declaratively across providers. You describe the desired end
state and Terraform works out the actions. The value is reproducibility - the same code creates identical environments -
reviewability, because changes go through pull requests, and safety, because plan shows exactly what will change before
anything happens.
Q. 2. Terraform versus CloudFormation?
 Terraform is provider-agnostic and can manage AWS alongside Kubernetes, GitHub and others in one workflow, with a
strong module ecosystem; you own and manage the state file. CloudFormation is AWS-native, with AWS-managed state,
built-in rollback on failure and drift detection, but it only manages AWS. I use Terraform primarily because of the module
reuse and because not everything we manage is AWS.
Q. 3. Terraform versus Ansible?
 Terraform provisions infrastructure - it creates the VPC, the instances, the load balancer - and tracks them in state so it
knows what it owns. Ansible configures existing systems - it installs packages, renders config files and manages services -
with no state file, checking reality per task each run. The usual pattern is Terraform builds the house, Ansible furnishes it.
Q. 4. What is the state file and why is it essential?
 A JSON record mapping configuration addresses to real resource IDs, along with the attributes last observed. Without it
Terraform cannot tell whether a resource in the code already exists, so it would try to create duplicates and could never
update or destroy correctly. It also stores dependency information used for correct destroy ordering.
Q. 5. Why store state remotely?
 So a team shares one source of truth, so it survives a lost laptop, so it can be encrypted and access-controlled, and - most
importantly - so it can be locked. Local state means two engineers can apply simultaneously and corrupt each other's work.
Q. 6. How does state locking work in the S3 backend?
 A DynamoDB table holds a lock item keyed on the state path. Terraform writes the lock before applying and removes it
afterwards; a second apply sees the lock and waits or fails. If a run crashes, the lock stays and must be cleared with
terraform force-unlock - but only after confirming nothing is genuinely running.
Q. 7. Is it safe to put the state file in Git?
 No. It contains every resource attribute in plaintext, including passwords and keys, so committing it leaks secrets. It also
breaks concurrency - Git has no locking semantics for this - and merge conflicts in state are effectively unrecoverable. State
belongs in an encrypted, versioned, locked backend.
Q. 8. What does terraform init do?
 Initialises the working directory: downloads the required provider plugins, installs modules, configures the backend and
creates the dependency lock file. It must be run before plan or apply, and re-run when providers, modules or the backend
change.
Q. 9. What is the difference between terraform plan and terraform apply?
 plan computes and displays the difference between the desired state in code and the actual state, changing nothing. apply
executes those changes. In CI I always save the plan with -out and apply that exact file, so what was reviewed is what runs.
Q. 10. What does terraform refresh do and why is it deprecated?
 It updated state from the real infrastructure without changing anything. It was deprecated as a standalone command
because it silently rewrote state; the explicit replacement is terraform apply -refresh-only, which shows you what changed
outside Terraform and asks you to approve reconciling it.
Q. 11. What is drift and how do you handle it?
 Reality diverging from state because something was changed outside Terraform. I detect it with a scheduled terraform plan
in CI that alerts on a non-empty plan. Then I decide: codify a legitimate change, or re-apply to revert an illegitimate one.
Prevention is better - restrict console write access in production so Terraform is the only path.
Q. 12. What is terraform import for?
 Bringing an existing resource that was created manually under Terraform management. You write the matching
configuration, then import it into state by ID. It does not generate the configuration for you - though newer versions
support import blocks and config generation - and the workflow is: import, then plan, then adjust the code until the plan is
empty.
Q. 13. How do you move a resource without destroying it?


===== PAGE 125 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 9  |  TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 125
terraform state mv old.address new.address, which updates the state mapping only. That is how you rename a resource
or move resources into a module without Terraform destroying and recreating them. Newer Terraform has moved blocks,
which do the same thing declaratively and reviewably in code - which is safer because it goes through a pull request.
Q. 14. What does terraform state rm do?
 Removes a resource from state so Terraform stops managing it - the real resource is untouched. It is used when handing
ownership to another configuration or team. The danger is forgetting that it is now unmanaged and orphaned.
Q. 15. Difference between count and for_each?
 count takes a number and addresses resources by index; for_each takes a map or set and addresses by key. The practical
difference is that removing an item from a count list re-indexes everything after it, causing Terraform to destroy and recreate
unrelated resources, whereas for_each keys are stable. I use for_each for anything with identity and count mainly for
conditional creation.
Q. 16. How do you create a resource conditionally?
 count = var.enabled ? 1 : 0, then reference it as resource.name[0], usually guarded with one() or a try() so references do
not explode when it is absent. For modules, the same pattern with count on the module block.
Q. 17. What is a data source?
 A read-only lookup of something Terraform does not manage - an existing VPC, the latest AMI, the current account ID. It
lets you reference existing infrastructure without importing it, which is how you compose with resources owned by other
teams.
Q. 18. What is the difference between a variable and a local?
 A variable is an input supplied from outside - tfvars, CLI, environment. A local is a named expression computed inside the
configuration for readability and to avoid repetition. You cannot override a local from outside, which is the point.
Q. 19. How are variables supplied, and what is the precedence?
 Defaults in the declaration, then terraform.tfvars and *.auto.tfvars, then TF_VAR_name environment variables, then
-var-file, then -var on the command line - with later sources winning. In CI I pass a -var-file per environment and inject
secrets as environment variables.
Q. 20. How do you handle secrets in Terraform?
 Never in the code or in a committed tfvars file. Mark variables sensitive so they are redacted from output, source them
from environment variables or a secret store, and prefer having Terraform reference a secret rather than set it - for example
creating an RDS instance with a password generated and stored in Secrets Manager. And remember that regardless of all
that, the value lands in state, so the state backend must be encrypted and access-controlled.
Q. 21. What are provisioners and why avoid them?
 local-exec and remote-exec run scripts as part of resource creation. They are discouraged because they are imperative, they
break Terraform's declarative model, they only run at create time so they do not converge, and a failure leaves the resource
tainted. The recommended alternatives are user data or cloud-init for bootstrapping and a configuration management tool
such as Ansible for ongoing configuration.
Q. 22. What is the dependency graph and how does Terraform build it?
 Terraform infers dependencies from references - if resource B uses aws_vpc.main.id, B depends on the VPC - and builds a
directed acyclic graph, then executes it with parallelism where possible. depends_on adds an explicit edge for dependencies
that are not expressed through a reference, such as an IAM policy that must exist before an instance can use a role.
Q. 23. What does lifecycle { create_before_destroy = true } do?
 It makes Terraform create the replacement before destroying the original, which avoids downtime and avoids "resource in
use" errors - for example when replacing a security group that is attached to instances. It requires that names do not collide,
which is why you use name_prefix instead of name.
Q. 24. What does ignore_changes do and when is it appropriate?
 It tells Terraform to ignore drift on specific attributes. It is appropriate when something legitimately changes outside
Terraform - an autoscaling desired count, or tags added by another system, or an AMI you do not want to force a
replacement for. It is inappropriate as a way to silence a plan you do not understand.
Q. 25. What is prevent_destroy?
 A lifecycle setting that makes Terraform error rather than destroy a resource. I put it on databases and state buckets. It is a
guard rail against a bad plan or a mistaken terraform destroy, and it forces a deliberate code change to remove.
Q. 26. How do you structure a Terraform repository?


===== PAGE 126 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 9  |  TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 126
Reusable modules in modules/, one directory per environment under environments/ with its own backend key and tfvars,
shared conventions enforced in the modules - tagging through default_tags, naming through a prefix variable. Small state
files per component rather than one enormous state, because a huge state is slow, risky and has a large blast radius.
Q. 27. Why split state into multiple files?
 Blast radius, speed and ownership. A single state for everything means every plan refreshes every resource, every apply risks
everything, and every team contends for one lock. Splitting by environment and by component - network, data, application
- keeps applies fast and failures contained. The components then reference each other through data sources or remote state
outputs.
Q. 28. What is terraform_remote_state used for?
 Reading outputs from another configuration's state, so the application configuration can consume the VPC ID produced by
the network configuration without hardcoding it. The alternative, which I prefer where possible, is looking the resource up
with a data source by tag, because it avoids coupling to another team's state file layout.
Q. 29. What happens if two people run apply at the same time?
 With locking, the second waits or fails with a lock error. Without locking, both read the same state, both write, and the last
write wins - so state no longer reflects reality, and the next plan may propose destroying resources it has lost track of. That is
exactly why the DynamoDB lock table is not optional.
Q. 30. Your apply failed halfway. What is the state?
 Terraform writes state incrementally, so resources created before the failure ARE in state; nothing is rolled back - Terraform
has no transactions. The recovery is to fix the cause and run apply again; it will create only what is missing. If a resource was
created but not recorded because of a crash, you may need to import it or delete it manually to unstick the plan.
Q. 31. How do you force recreation of a single resource?
 terraform apply -replace="aws_instance.app[0]". The older mechanism was terraform taint, which is now deprecated
because -replace is explicit and visible in the plan rather than being hidden state.
Q. 32. What is -target and why is it discouraged?
 It restricts an operation to specific resources. It is discouraged because it skips the dependency graph, so you can leave
infrastructure in an inconsistent state where the code and reality only partially agree. It is legitimate as an emergency tool to
recover from a broken apply, not as normal workflow.
Q. 33. How do you version modules?
 Git tags with a semantic version, referenced as ?ref=v1.4.0, or registry versions with a version constraint. Pinning matters
because an unpinned module reference means someone else's commit can change every environment that consumes it the
next time you run init.
Q. 34. What is the dependency lock file?
 .terraform.lock.hcl records the exact provider versions and their checksums selected during init. It should be committed
so every engineer and the CI pipeline use identical provider versions - without it, two people can generate different plans
from the same code.
Q. 35. How do you test Terraform code?
 Static checks first - fmt -check, validate, and policy scanning with tfsec or Checkov in CI. Then the plan itself as a review
artifact posted to the pull request. For modules, integration testing with Terratest or the native terraform test framework,
which provisions into a sandbox account, asserts, and destroys. Plus a scheduled plan against production to catch drift.
Q. 36. How do you handle multiple AWS regions or accounts?
 Provider aliases - multiple provider "aws" blocks with alias and different regions or assumed roles - and then provider =
aws.eu on the resources that belong to the second region. For multiple accounts, assume_role in the provider configuration,
with the pipeline's role trusted by each target account.
Q. 37. What is the difference between terraform destroy and removing the code?
 destroy deletes the real resources and clears state. Deleting the code and applying also destroys them, because Terraform
sees resources in state that are no longer in the configuration. Neither is reversible, which is why prevent_destroy on critical
resources and reading the plan matter.
Q. 38. How would you introduce Terraform into an environment that was built by hand?
 Incrementally. Start with new resources so everything new is managed from the start. Then import existing resources
component by component, writing the configuration and iterating until plan is empty - an empty plan is the proof that the
code matches reality. Meanwhile, restrict console write access so the manual drift stops growing. Trying to import an entire
estate in one pass is how these projects fail.


===== PAGE 127 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 9  |  TERRAFORM COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 127
Q. 39. What are the most common Terraform mistakes you have seen?
 Committing state or tfvars containing secrets; running -auto-approve on production without reading the plan; one giant
state file for the whole estate; unpinned provider and module versions so builds are not reproducible; using count where
for_each was needed and then recreating live resources; and using provisioners for configuration instead of a proper
configuration management tool.
Q. 40. Terraform wants to destroy production infrastructure. What do you do?
 Stop - do not apply. Read the plan carefully and find which resources and which attribute forces replacement; the plan says
"forces replacement" against the specific attribute. Then work out why: someone may have changed an immutable field such
as an availability zone or a name, or a module version bump changed a default, or the state was rolled back or partially
removed, or I am pointed at the wrong workspace or state key. I verify which state I am using and which account with aws
sts get-caller-identity. If it is a genuine immutable change I plan a safe migration - create before destroy, or a manual
cutover - rather than letting a single apply do it. And this is exactly why prevent_destroy on databases and a required
human review of plans exist.
PART 9 RECALL POINTS
 State maps code addresses to real resources - it is what makes update and destroy possible.
 State contains secrets in plaintext: encrypt it, lock it, never commit it.
 DynamoDB locking prevents concurrent applies corrupting state.
 plan -out=tfplan then apply tfplan - apply exactly what was reviewed.
 for_each for identity, count for interchangeable copies and conditionals.
 Pin providers, modules and the lock file.
 -/+ in a plan means destroy and recreate - read why.
 Drift: detect with a scheduled plan, reconcile with -refresh-only.
 Terraform provisions, Ansible configures.

PART 10
ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
Inventory, playbooks, roles, variables, vault, idempotency, 30 questions
Part 10. ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
Your CV says you "automated server and application configuration with Ansible for repeatable, controlled
environments" in both roles. The two things you must be able to defend are idempotency - why running a playbook
twice is safe - and the division of labour between Terraform and Ansible.
10.1 Ansible Architecture
  +-----------------------------------+
  |        CONTROL NODE               |
  |  ansible / ansible-playbook       |
  |  inventory (hosts)                |
  |  playbooks, roles, vars, vault    |
  +----------------+------------------+
                   |  SSH (Linux)  /  WinRM (Windows)
      +------------+------------+---------------+
      |                         |               |
  +---v----+              +-----v---+      +----v----+
  | web01  |              | web02   |      | db01    |
  |        |              |         |      |         |
  | python | <- modules are COPIED over, EXECUTED, then REMOVED
  +--------+              +---------+      +---------+
  MANAGED NODES - no agent installed, only Python (and SSH) required
Agentless push model
Component
Role
Control node
Where Ansible runs. Linux only - Ansible does not run its control process on Windows
Managed node
The target. Needs SSH and Python for Linux; WinRM or SSH plus PowerShell for Windows
Inventory
The list of hosts and groups
Module
A unit of work - apt, copy, service, user. Written mostly in Python
Task
One invocation of a module
Play
A mapping of a host group to a list of tasks
Playbook
A YAML file containing one or more plays
Role
A standard directory structure packaging tasks, handlers, templates, files and variables
Handler
A task triggered only when notified by a changed task - typically a service restart
Facts
Data gathered from the target at the start of a run
Collection
A distributable bundle of roles, modules and plugins (Ansible Galaxy)
AGENTLESS IS THE HEADLINE FEATURE
"Ansible is agentless - it connects over SSH, copies the module to the target, executes it with Python, collects the JSON
result and removes it. There is nothing to install or keep patched on the managed nodes, which is a large operational
advantage over agent-based tools like Puppet or Chef. The trade-offs are that it is push-based rather than continuously
enforcing, and that it is slower at very large scale because everything is SSH."
10.2 Inventory
Static inventory - INI format
 [webservers]
web01 ansible_host=10.0.11.10
web02 ansible_host=10.0.11.11
 
[dbservers]
db01 ansible_host=10.0.21.10

  vars:
    ansible_user: deploy
  children:
    production:
      children:
        webservers:

            web01: { ansible_host: 10.0.11.10 }
            web02: { ansible_host: 10.0.11.11 }
          vars:
            http_port: 8080
        dbservers:
          hosts:
            db01: { ansible_host: 10.0.21.10 }
Static inventory
Dynamic inventory
Source
A file you maintain
A plugin or script querying a live source
Suits

  - eu-west-1

  tag:Environment: production
  instance-state-name: running

    prefix: role

    prefix: az

  - private-ip-address

  ansible_host: private_ip_address
Inventory commands
 ansible-inventory -i inventory.ini --list          # full JSON view
ansible-inventory -i inventory.ini --graph         # tree of groups and hosts
ansible-inventory -i aws_ec2.yml --graph           # verify the dynamic plugin works
ansible all -i inventory.ini --list-hosts

10.3 Ad-Hoc Commands
One-off operations across a fleet
 ansible all -m ping                                   # connectivity + Python check
ansible webservers -m command -a "uptime"
ansible webservers -m shell -a "df -h | grep -v tmpfs"   # shell allows pipes and redirection
ansible webservers -m setup                           # dump all gathered facts
ansible webservers -m setup -a "filter=ansible_memory_mb"
ansible webservers -m apt -a "name=nginx state=present" --become
ansible webservers -m service -a "name=nginx state=restarted" --become
ansible webservers -m copy -a "src=./app.conf dest=/etc/app/app.conf mode=0644" --become
ansible webservers -m user -a "name=deploy groups=docker append=yes" --become
ansible all -m shell -a "systemctl is-active nginx" --one-line
ansible all -i inv -u ec2-user --private-key ~/.ssh/k.pem -m ping
COMMAND VERSUS SHELL
command runs the binary directly - no shell, so no pipes, redirects, globs or environment variable expansion, which makes
it safer. shell runs through /bin/sh, so all of that works but you inherit shell injection risks. The interview answer: "Use
command unless you specifically need shell features, and prefer a purpose-built module over both - apt rather than
command: apt-get install, because the module is idempotent and command is not."
10.4 Playbooks
A complete, realistic playbook
 ---
- name: Configure and deploy the application tier
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes                     # privilege escalation (sudo)
  gather_facts: yes
  serial: 2                       # rolling: 2 hosts at a time, not all at once
  max_fail_percentage: 0
 
  vars:
    app_name: payments-api
    app_dir: /opt/{{ app_name }}
    app_port: 8080
 
  vars_files:
    - vars/common.yml
    - vars/{{ ansible_facts['distribution'] | lower }}.yml
 
  pre_tasks:
    - name: Ensure the package cache is fresh
      ansible.builtin.apt:
        update_cache: yes
        cache_valid_time: 3600
      when: ansible_facts['os_family'] == "Debian"
 
  tasks:
    - name: Install required packages
      ansible.builtin.package:
        name:
          - nginx
          - python3
          - python3-pip
        state: present
 
    - name: Create the application group
      ansible.builtin.group:
        name: "{{ app_name }}"
        state: present
 
    - name: Create the application user
      ansible.builtin.user:
        name: "{{ app_name }}"
        group: "{{ app_name }}"
        home: "{{ app_dir }}"


===== PAGE 131 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 131
        shell: /usr/sbin/nologin
        system: yes
 
    - name: Create the application directories
      ansible.builtin.file:
        path: "{{ item }}"
        state: directory
        owner: "{{ app_name }}"
        group: "{{ app_name }}"
        mode: "0750"
      loop:
        - "{{ app_dir }}"
        - "{{ app_dir }}/releases"
        - /var/log/{{ app_name }}
 
    - name: Render the application configuration
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: app.conf.j2
        dest: "{{ app_dir }}/app.conf"
        owner: "{{ app_name }}"
        mode: "0640"
        backup: yes                     # keep the previous version
        validate: "/usr/bin/app --check-config %s"
      notify: Restart application        # handler fires ONLY if this task changed something
 
    - name: Copy the systemd unit
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: app.service.j2
        dest: /etc/systemd/system/{{ app_name }}.service
        mode: "0644"
      notify:
        - Reload systemd
        - Restart application
 
    - name: Ensure the service is running and enabled
      ansible.builtin.systemd:
        name: "{{ app_name }}"
        state: started
        enabled: yes
 
    - name: Wait for the application port to accept connections
      ansible.builtin.wait_for:
        port: "{{ app_port }}"
        host: 127.0.0.1
        timeout: 60
 
    - name: Verify the health endpoint
      ansible.builtin.uri:
        url: "http://127.0.0.1:{{ app_port }}/health"
        status_code: 200
      register: health
      retries: 5
      delay: 5
      until: health.status == 200
 
  handlers:
    - name: Reload systemd
      ansible.builtin.systemd:
        daemon_reload: yes
 
    - name: Restart application
      ansible.builtin.systemd:
        name: "{{ app_name }}"
        state: restarted

10.5 Idempotency - The Core Concept
 THE DEFINITION TO GIVE
"Idempotency means running the playbook a second time produces no further changes, because each task checks the
current state and only acts if reality does not already match the desired state. That is what makes automation safe to
re-run - I can apply the same playbook to a fresh server or an existing one and get the same result. The place it breaks
is command and shell tasks, which have no way to know the desired state, so they report 'changed' every run and may
actually re-do work. Where I have to use them I add creates:, removes: or a when: guard so they become conditionally
idempotent."
Non-idempotent versus idempotent
 # BAD - runs every time, always reports changed, appends duplicates
- name: Add a line to a config
  ansible.builtin.shell: echo "max_connections=200" >> /etc/app/app.conf
 
# GOOD - declarative module, only changes if needed
- name: Ensure the setting is present
  ansible.builtin.lineinfile:
    path: /etc/app/app.conf
    regexp: '^max_connections='
    line: 'max_connections=200'
    state: present
 
# BAD - extracts every run
- name: Unpack the release
  ansible.builtin.shell: tar xzf /tmp/app.tgz -C /opt/app
 
# GOOD - guarded, so it runs only once
- name: Unpack the release
  ansible.builtin.unarchive:
    src: /tmp/app.tgz
    dest: /opt/app
    remote_src: yes
    creates: /opt/app/VERSION
Task result
Meaning
ok
Already in the desired state - nothing done
changed
The module made a change - this is what triggers handlers
failed
The task errored; the play stops for that host by default
skipped
A when condition was false
unreachable
Could not connect - SSH, key or network problem

10.7 Roles
Standard role structure - created by ansible-galaxy init
 roles/
  nginx/
    tasks/main.yml        # the work
    handlers/main.yml     # restart/reload handlers
    templates/            # Jinja2 templates (.j2)
    files/                # static files to copy
    vars/main.yml         # high-precedence variables
    defaults/main.yml     # low-precedence, overridable defaults
    meta/main.yml         # dependencies and metadata
    tests/                # test playbook
    README.md
Using roles, with variables and tags
 ---
- name: Build the web tier
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes
 
  roles:
    - role: common
      tags: [base]
 
    - role: nginx
      vars:
        nginx_worker_processes: 4
        nginx_upstream_servers: "{{ groups['appservers'] }}"
      tags: [web]
 
    - role: monitoring
      vars:
        node_exporter_version: "1.8.1"
      tags: [monitoring]

 ansible-galaxy init roles/myrole                     # scaffold a new role

10.8 Ansible Vault
Encrypting secrets at rest
 ansible-vault create secrets.yml                 # create a new encrypted file
ansible-vault edit secrets.yml                   # decrypt, open in $EDITOR, re-encrypt
ansible-vault view secrets.yml                   # read without editing
ansible-vault encrypt vars/prod.yml              # encrypt an existing file
ansible-vault decrypt vars/prod.yml              # permanently decrypt - rarely what you want


===== PAGE 135 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 135
ansible-vault rekey secrets.yml                  # change the vault password
 
# Encrypt a single value to embed inline in a normal YAML file
ansible-vault encrypt_string 'S3cr3tP@ss' --name 'db_password'
 
# Running playbooks that use vault
ansible-playbook site.yml --ask-vault-pass
ansible-playbook site.yml --vault-password-file ~/.vault_pass      # CI, file mode 600
ansible-playbook site.yml --vault-id prod@~/.vault_prod            # multiple vault identities
VAULT VERSUS A SECRETS MANAGER
"Vault encrypts secrets at rest inside the repository, which is far better than plaintext, but it is still a static secret shared
by everyone who can decrypt it, and rotating it means re-encrypting files. In production I would prefer Ansible reading
from AWS Secrets Manager or SSM Parameter Store at run time using the control node's IAM role, so nothing sensitive
is in Git at all. Ansible Vault is a good fit for things like a bootstrap credential where a lookup is not possible."
10.9 Running Playbooks
Commands and flags that matter
 ansible-playbook site.yml
ansible-playbook -i inventory/prod site.yml
ansible-playbook site.yml --check                  # DRY RUN - predicts changes, changes nothing
ansible-playbook site.yml --check --diff           # dry run plus the actual file differences
ansible-playbook site.yml --diff                   # show what changed in files
ansible-playbook site.yml --limit web01            # one host only
ansible-playbook site.yml --limit 'webservers:!web03'
ansible-playbook site.yml --tags "config,deploy"
ansible-playbook site.yml --skip-tags "slow"
ansible-playbook site.yml --start-at-task "Render the application configuration"
ansible-playbook site.yml --step                   # confirm each task interactively
ansible-playbook site.yml -e "app_version=1.4.2"   # extra vars win over everything
ansible-playbook site.yml -e "@prod-vars.yml"
ansible-playbook site.yml -v                       # -v -vv -vvv -vvvv increasing verbosity
ansible-playbook site.yml --syntax-check
ansible-playbook site.yml --list-tasks
ansible-playbook site.yml --list-hosts
ansible-playbook site.yml -f 20                    # forks: 20 hosts in parallel
--CHECK --DIFF IS YOUR PRODUCTION SAFETY NET
"Before running a playbook against production I run it with --check --diff. Check mode predicts what would change
without changing anything, and diff shows the exact file differences. It is not perfect - tasks whose result depends on an
earlier task's change can report inaccurately in check mode, and command/shell tasks are skipped - but it catches most
mistakes. It is the Ansible equivalent of terraform plan."
10.10 Practical Playbook Examples
1. Install and configure a package with a service
 - name: Install and configure nginx
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes
  tasks:
    - name: Install nginx
      ansible.builtin.package: { name: nginx, state: present }
 
    - name: Deploy the site configuration
      ansible.builtin.template:
        src: site.conf.j2
        dest: /etc/nginx/conf.d/site.conf
        validate: 'nginx -t -c %s'
      notify: Reload nginx
 
    - name: Ensure nginx is running and enabled
      ansible.builtin.service: { name: nginx, state: started, enabled: yes }
 


===== PAGE 136 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 136
  handlers:
    - name: Reload nginx
      ansible.builtin.service: { name: nginx, state: reloaded }
2. Create users with SSH keys and sudo
 - name: Manage operations users
  hosts: all
  become: yes
  vars:
    ops_users:
      - { name: aasim, key: "ssh-ed25519 AAAA...", sudo: true }
      - { name: qa,    key: "ssh-ed25519 BBBB...", sudo: false }
  tasks:
    - name: Create users
      ansible.builtin.user:
        name: "{{ item.name }}"
        shell: /bin/bash
        groups: "{{ 'sudo' if item.sudo else omit }}"
        append: yes
        state: present
      loop: "{{ ops_users }}"
 
    - name: Install authorised keys
      ansible.posix.authorized_key:
        user: "{{ item.name }}"
        key: "{{ item.key }}"
        state: present
      loop: "{{ ops_users }}"
 
    - name: Grant restricted sudo for service restarts
      ansible.builtin.lineinfile:
        path: /etc/sudoers.d/ops
        line: "{{ item.name }} ALL=(root) NOPASSWD: /bin/systemctl restart payments-api"
        create: yes
        mode: "0440"
        validate: 'visudo -cf %s'
      loop: "{{ ops_users }}"
      when: item.sudo
3. Zero-downtime rolling application deployment
 - name: Rolling deploy
  hosts: webservers
  become: yes
  serial: 1                       # one host at a time
  max_fail_percentage: 0          # stop the whole run if any host fails
 
  tasks:
    - name: Remove this host from the load balancer
      ansible.builtin.uri:
        url: "http://lb.internal/api/drain/{{ inventory_hostname }}"
        method: POST
      delegate_to: localhost       # run on the control node, not the target
 
    - name: Wait for connections to drain
      ansible.builtin.pause: { seconds: 20 }
 
    - name: Pull the new image
      community.docker.docker_image:
        name: "registry.example.com/payments-api:{{ app_version }}"
        source: pull
 
    - name: Restart the container with the new image
      community.docker.docker_container:
        name: payments-api
        image: "registry.example.com/payments-api:{{ app_version }}"
        state: started
        restart_policy: unless-stopped
        recreate: yes
        published_ports: ["8080:8080"]
 
    - name: Wait for the health endpoint


===== PAGE 137 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 137
      ansible.builtin.uri:
        url: "http://127.0.0.1:8080/health"
        status_code: 200
      register: health
      retries: 12
      delay: 5
      until: health.status == 200
 
    - name: Return the host to the load balancer
      ansible.builtin.uri:
        url: "http://lb.internal/api/enable/{{ inventory_hostname }}"
        method: POST
      delegate_to: localhost
4. Error handling with block/rescue/always
 - name: Deploy with rollback
  hosts: appservers
  become: yes
  tasks:
    - block:
        - name: Deploy the new release
          ansible.builtin.unarchive:
            src: "app-{{ app_version }}.tgz"
            dest: /opt/app/releases/{{ app_version }}
 
        - name: Point current at the new release
          ansible.builtin.file:
            src: /opt/app/releases/{{ app_version }}
            dest: /opt/app/current
            state: link
 
        - name: Restart the service
          ansible.builtin.systemd: { name: app, state: restarted }
 
        - name: Health check
          ansible.builtin.uri: { url: "http://127.0.0.1:8080/health", status_code: 200 }
          retries: 6
          delay: 5
 
      rescue:
        - name: Roll back the symlink to the previous release
          ansible.builtin.file:
            src: "/opt/app/releases/{{ previous_version }}"
            dest: /opt/app/current
            state: link
 
        - name: Restart on the previous release
          ansible.builtin.systemd: { name: app, state: restarted }
 
        - name: Report the failure
          ansible.builtin.fail:
            msg: "Deployment of {{ app_version }} failed; rolled back to {{ previous_version }}"
 
      always:
        - name: Record the outcome
          ansible.builtin.lineinfile:
            path: /var/log/deploy-history.log
            line: "{{ ansible_date_time.iso8601 }} {{ app_version }} {{ 'OK' if not ansible_failed_task is
    defined else 'ROLLED BACK' }}"
            create: yes
10.11 Terraform vs Ansible - Say This Clearly
 Terraform
Ansible
Category
Provisioning / IaC
Configuration management
Model
Declarative with a state file
Task-based; each task idempotent, no state file
Knows what it owns
Yes - the state file
No - it checks reality each run


===== PAGE 138 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 138
Terraform
Ansible
Creates infrastructure
Yes - VPCs, instances, load balancers
Can, through cloud modules, but it is not its strength and there
is no state to reconcile
Configures an OS
Not its job (provisioners are discouraged)
Yes - packages, files, services, users
Drift handling
Detects and can revert, because it has state
Re-running converges each task, but it will not remove things it
never created
Agent
None
None (agentless over SSH/WinRM)
Typical order
Runs first
Runs second, against what Terraform created
THE COMBINED-WORKFLOW ANSWER
"They are complementary. Terraform creates the infrastructure - VPC, subnets, security groups, EC2 instances - and
because it holds state, it knows exactly what it manages and can show me a plan before changing anything. Ansible
then configures those machines: installs packages, renders configuration from templates, manages services and deploys
the application. In practice Terraform outputs the instance details, either into a dynamic inventory or through the
aws_ec2 inventory plugin keyed on tags, and Ansible picks them up from there. Using Terraform provisioners to do
configuration, or using Ansible to build a whole VPC, is possible but it fights each tool's design."
10.12 Ansible Interview Questions
Q. 1. What is Ansible and how does it differ from Puppet or Chef?
 An agentless configuration management and automation tool that connects over SSH or WinRM, pushes modules to the
target, executes them and removes them. Puppet and Chef are agent-based and pull-based - an agent on each node
periodically fetches and applies the catalogue. Ansible's advantage is nothing to install or patch on managed nodes and a
very low barrier to entry; the trade-offs are that it is push-based so it does not continuously enforce state, and SSH becomes
a bottleneck at very large scale.
Q. 2. What are the requirements on a managed node?
 SSH access and Python for Linux targets; WinRM (or SSH) plus PowerShell for Windows. No Ansible agent. The control node
must be Linux - Ansible does not run its control process natively on Windows.
Q. 3. What is idempotency and why does it matter?
 Running the same playbook repeatedly produces no further changes because each task checks current state and only acts if
reality differs from the desired state. It matters because it makes automation safe to re-run - I can apply the same playbook
to a new server or an existing one. It breaks with command and shell, which always report changed, so I guard them with
creates:, removes: or when:.
Q. 4. What is the difference between a playbook and a role?
 A playbook is the top-level YAML file mapping hosts to tasks - it is the thing you run. A role is a reusable, standard directory
structure packaging tasks, handlers, templates, files and defaults, which a playbook includes. Roles are how you avoid
copying the same fifty tasks into ten playbooks.
Q. 5. What is a handler?
 A task that runs only when notified by another task that actually changed something, and only once at the end of the play
regardless of how many tasks notified it. The classic use is restarting a service after a configuration file changed - so the
service restarts only if the config genuinely changed, not on every run.
Q. 6. What if you need a handler to run immediately rather than at the end?
 meta: flush_handlers forces all pending handlers to run at that point. You need it when a later task depends on the service
having already been restarted - for example reloading systemd before starting a newly installed unit.
Q. 7. What are facts?
 System information gathered from the target at the start of a run - OS family, distribution and version, memory, CPU count,
network interfaces, IP addresses. They let a playbook adapt to the host, for example installing with apt or yum depending on
ansible_facts['os_family']. Disabling fact gathering with gather_facts: no noticeably speeds up playbooks that do not
need them.
Q. 8. What is the variable precedence order, roughly?


===== PAGE 139 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 139
From lowest to highest: role defaults, inventory group vars, inventory host vars, playbook vars, vars_files, role vars, and finally
--extra-vars, which always wins. The practical rule is to put overridable values in defaults/ and things that must not be
overridden in vars/.
Q. 9. What is Ansible Vault?
 Encryption for sensitive data at rest - whole files or individual values with encrypt_string - decrypted at run time with a
vault password. It stops secrets sitting in plaintext in Git. For production I would prefer looking secrets up from a secrets
manager at run time, since vault passwords are static shared secrets that are awkward to rotate.
Q. 10. What is the difference between copy and template?
 copy transfers a file as-is. template renders a Jinja2 template with variables and facts before transferring it, so one template
produces host-specific configuration. Templates also support validate:, which runs a syntax check before the file is put in
place - that is how you avoid deploying a config that breaks the service.
Q. 11. What is check mode?
 --check is a dry run: Ansible reports what would change without changing anything. Combined with --diff it shows the
exact file differences. It is the closest equivalent to terraform plan, with the caveat that tasks depending on earlier changes
can report inaccurately, and command/shell tasks are skipped unless marked check_mode: no.
Q. 12. How do you run a playbook on just one host?
 --limit hostname, which also accepts patterns and exclusions such as --limit 'webservers:!web03'. Combined with --tags
and --start-at-task, it is how you re-run part of a playbook after fixing a failure instead of starting over.
Q. 13. What are tags used for?
 Selectively running or skipping parts of a playbook - --tags deploy or --skip-tags slow. In a long playbook it turns a
20-minute full run into a 30-second targeted change.
Q. 14. How do you achieve a rolling update with Ansible?
 serial: controls how many hosts are processed at a time - serial: 1 for one at a time or a percentage for batches -
combined with removing the host from the load balancer, deploying, health-checking, and putting it back.
max_fail_percentage: 0 stops the rollout as soon as one host fails, so a bad release does not propagate across the fleet.
Q. 15. What does delegate_to do?
 Runs a task on a different host from the one currently being processed - typically delegate_to: localhost to call a load
balancer API or update DNS while iterating over target hosts. run_once: true combined with it runs a task a single time for
the whole play rather than per host.
Q. 16. How do you handle errors?
 block/rescue/always for structured handling - deploy in the block, roll back in rescue, log in always. failed_when and
changed_when to define what actually constitutes failure or change for a command task. ignore_errors: yes only where a
failure is genuinely acceptable, because it hides real problems.
Q. 17. How do you register and use a command's output?
 register: result captures the return structure, then you use result.stdout, result.rc or result.stdout_lines in later
tasks and conditions - often with until, retries and delay to poll until a condition is met, which is how health checks are
written.
Q. 18. What is the difference between include_tasks and import_tasks?
 import_tasks is static - processed at parse time, so tags and conditionals apply to each imported task individually and the
tasks are visible to --list-tasks. include_tasks is dynamic - evaluated at run time, so it can use variables set during the run
and loop, but it is opaque to some pre-run tooling. The rule of thumb is import when it is static, include when the decision
depends on runtime data.
Q. 19. What is dynamic inventory and why do you need it in the cloud?
 An inventory generated by querying a live source rather than maintained by hand. In AWS the aws_ec2 plugin queries EC2
and groups instances by tag, region or availability zone. It is necessary because instances in an Auto Scaling Group come
and go - a static file goes stale immediately and you end up configuring hosts that no longer exist while missing ones that
do.
Q. 20. How do you connect Ansible to Windows hosts?
 Over WinRM, with ansible_connection: winrm and an appropriate transport such as NTLM or Kerberos, plus WinRM
enabled and listening on the target. Then you use the win_* modules - win_feature, win_service, win_copy - rather than the
Linux ones. This is directly relevant to my current role, where we run both Linux and Windows servers.


===== PAGE 140 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 10  |  ANSIBLE COMPLETE GUIDE
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 140
Q. 21. What is ansible.cfg and what do you put in it?
 The configuration file controlling defaults - inventory path, remote user, private key, forks, whether host key checking is
enabled, roles path, and performance settings such as SSH pipelining. Committing it with the project means everyone runs
with the same settings rather than depending on personal environments.
Q. 22. How do you speed up Ansible?
 Increase forks for more parallelism, enable SSH pipelining to reduce round trips, turn off fact gathering where it is not
needed or use fact caching, use async with poll: 0 for long-running independent tasks, avoid unnecessary loops over large
lists, and use --limit and --tags so you are not re-running everything.
Q. 23. What is the difference between apt/yum and the package module?
 package is a generic wrapper that dispatches to the right package manager based on the target's OS family, so one task
works on Debian and RedHat. The specific modules expose options the generic one does not, such as update_cache and
cache_valid_time for apt. I use package for portability and the specific module when I need its features.
Q. 24. How do you ensure a config change actually restarts the service?
 notify a handler from the task that writes the config. The handler runs only if that task reported changed, and only once at
the end. That is the whole point of handlers - a naive service: restarted task would restart on every run, causing needless
disruption.
Q. 25. What is lineinfile and when would you use blockinfile instead?
 lineinfile ensures a single line is present or absent, matched by a regex. blockinfile manages a whole marked block of
text, which is better for multi-line insertions because it wraps the block in markers it can find again. Both are for editing files
you do not own; if you own the file, a template is cleaner because it declares the whole desired content.
Q. 26. How would you test Ansible code?
 --syntax-check and ansible-lint in CI; --check --diff against a staging host; and Molecule for role testing, which spins up
a container or VM, applies the role, asserts with Testinfra, and verifies idempotency by running it twice and requiring zero
changes on the second pass. That idempotency test is the one that catches the most real bugs.
Q. 27. How does Ansible fit into CI/CD?
 The pipeline builds and publishes the artifact, then triggers the playbook for the target environment, with credentials
injected from the CI credential store and the inventory generated dynamically from cloud tags. The playbook does the
deploy and the health check, and its exit code determines whether the pipeline stage passes. Rolling deployment with
serial and rollback with block/rescue live inside the playbook rather than in the pipeline.
Q. 28. What is the difference between become and become_user?
 become: yes escalates privileges, by default to root using sudo. become_user specifies a different target user - for example
becoming the application user to run a database migration. become_method chooses the mechanism: sudo, su, doas, runas on
Windows.
Q. 29. What happens when a task fails on one host out of ten?
 By default that host is removed from the play and the remaining hosts continue; at the end the recap shows the failure.
any_errors_fatal: true aborts the entire play immediately, and max_fail_percentage sets a tolerance. For a rolling
deployment I set these so a failure stops the rollout instead of continuing to break the rest of the fleet.
Q. 30. What are the most common Ansible mistakes?
 Using shell and command where a module exists, which destroys idempotency; not using handlers, so services restart on
every run; hardcoding values instead of using variables and defaults; secrets in plaintext; no --check before production;
monolithic playbooks with no roles; and ignoring the idempotency test - if the second run reports changes, the playbook is
not doing what the author thinks it is.
PART 10 RECALL POINTS

 Agentless: SSH/WinRM, push modules, execute, remove.
 Idempotency is the core promise; command/shell break it unless guarded.
 Handlers fire only on change, once, at the end.
 serial: is how you do rolling, zero-downtime configuration changes.
 --check --diff is the Ansible equivalent of terraform plan.
 Vault for secrets at rest; a secrets manager is better in production.
 --extra-vars beats every other variable source.
 Terraform provisions, Ansible configures - and Ansible has no state file.

PART 11
KUBERNETES COMPLETE GUIDE
Architecture, objects, YAML, kubectl, 15 troubleshooting scenarios, 70 questions
Part 11. KUBERNETES COMPLETE GUIDE
Your CV says you "support Kubernetes deployments, troubleshooting Pods, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps and
application connectivity issues", and it lists Kubernetes administration as something you are currently strengthening.
That is an honest position and you should hold it: you troubleshoot workloads, you do not administer control planes.
This part gives you enough depth to be strong on workloads and credible on architecture.
HOW TO ANSWER KUBERNETES QUESTIONS HONESTLY
For workload questions - pods, deployments, services, configmaps, secrets, probes, rollouts, debugging - answer with
confidence; that is your CV's claim. For cluster administration - etcd backups, upgrading control planes, CNI selection,
cluster autoscaler tuning - answer with the concept and then say: "I have not administered a cluster in production; my
Kubernetes work is at the workload level - deploying and troubleshooting. This is my understanding of how it works."
That combination scores far better than a bluff.
11.1 Architecture
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
  |                        CONTROL PLANE                              |
  |                                                                   |
  |  +-------------------+   +-------------------+   +-------------+  |
  |  |   API SERVER      |<->|      etcd         |   |  SCHEDULER  |  |
  |  | the ONLY component|   | key-value store   |   | picks a node|  |
  |  | that talks to etcd|   | ALL cluster state |   | for each pod|  |
  |  | authn, authz,     |   | back this up!     |   +-------------+  |
  |  | admission, REST   |   +-------------------+                    |
  |  +---------^---------+   +-----------------------------------+    |
  |            |             |   CONTROLLER MANAGER              |    |
  |            |             | deployment, replicaset, node,     |    |
  |            |             | endpoint, job controllers - each  |    |
  |            |             | reconciles desired vs actual      |    |
  |            |             +-----------------------------------+    |
  +------------|------------------------------------------------------+
               |  kubectl / kubelet / controllers all talk to the API server
  +------------v------------------------------------------------------+
  |                        WORKER NODE                                |
  |   +-----------+   +----------------------+   +-----------------+  |
  |   |  kubelet  |   | container runtime    |   |   kube-proxy    |  |
  |   | watches   |   | containerd / CRI-O   |   | implements      |  |
  |   | API for   |-->| pulls images,        |   | Service virtual |  |
  |   | its pods, |   | runs containers      |   | IPs via iptables|  |
  |   | reports   |   +----------------------+   | or IPVS         |  |
  |   | status,   |                              +-----------------+  |
  |   | runs probes|   +--------+ +--------+ +--------+               |
  |   +-----------+   |  POD   | |  POD   | |  POD   |               |
  |                   +--------+ +--------+ +--------+               |
  +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
Control plane and worker nodes
Component
Responsibility
If it fails
API server
Front door: REST API, authentication, authorisation,
admission control, the only writer to etcd
Nothing can be changed; running workloads keep
running
etcd
Consistent key-value store holding all cluster state
Catastrophic - this is what you back up
Scheduler
Assigns pending pods to nodes based on resources,
taints, affinity
New pods stay Pending; existing pods unaffected
Controller manager
Runs the reconciliation loops - deployment, replicaset,
node, endpoints
Self-healing stops; nothing replaces failed pods

ager

working

THE RECONCILIATION LOOP IS THE CORE IDEA
"Kubernetes is a set of controllers each running a loop: read the desired state from the API server, observe the actual
state, and take action to close the gap. That is why it is declarative - I do not tell it to start three pods, I declare that
three should exist, and the ReplicaSet controller makes that true and keeps it true. Self-healing is not a feature bolted
on; it is a consequence of the architecture."
11.2 Pods
A pod is the smallest deployable unit: one or more containers that share a network namespace (so they share an IP and can
reach each other on localhost), share storage volumes, and are always scheduled together on one node.
A pod with the settings that matter in production
 apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: payments-api
  labels:
    app: payments-api
    tier: backend
spec:
  serviceAccountName: payments-api
  securityContext:
    runAsNonRoot: true
    runAsUser: 10001
    fsGroup: 10001
 
  initContainers:                       # runs to completion BEFORE app containers start
    - name: wait-for-db
      image: busybox:1.36
      command: ['sh', '-c', 'until nc -z postgres 5432; do echo waiting; sleep 2; done']
 
  containers:
    - name: api
      image: registry.example.com/payments-api:1.4.2
      imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent
      ports:
        - containerPort: 8080
          name: http
 
      env:
        - name: APP_ENV
          value: production
        - name: DB_HOST
          valueFrom:
            configMapKeyRef: { name: payments-config, key: db_host }
        - name: DB_PASSWORD
          valueFrom:
            secretKeyRef: { name: payments-secret, key: db_password }
 
      resources:
        requests:                        # used for SCHEDULING - guaranteed
          memory: "256Mi"
          cpu: "250m"

Page 144
        limits:                          # enforced ceiling
          memory: "512Mi"
          cpu: "1000m"
 
      livenessProbe:                     # failing -> container is RESTARTED
        httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8080 }
        initialDelaySeconds: 30
        periodSeconds: 10
        timeoutSeconds: 3
        failureThreshold: 3
 
      readinessProbe:                    # failing -> pod removed from Service ENDPOINTS
        httpGet: { path: /ready, port: 8080 }
        initialDelaySeconds: 5
        periodSeconds: 5
        failureThreshold: 3
 
      startupProbe:                      # protects slow starters from the liveness probe
        httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8080 }
        failureThreshold: 30
        periodSeconds: 10
 
      volumeMounts:
        - name: config
          mountPath: /etc/app
          readOnly: true
        - name: tmp
          mountPath: /tmp
 
      securityContext:
        allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
        readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
        capabilities: { drop: ["ALL"] }
 
  volumes:
    - name: config
      configMap: { name: payments-config }
    - name: tmp
      emptyDir: {}
Pod phase
Meaning
Pending
Accepted but not running - waiting for scheduling, image pull, or a volume
Running
Bound to a node, at least one container running
Succeeded
All containers exited 0 and will not restart (Jobs)
Failed
All containers terminated, at least one with a non-zero exit
Unknown
The node cannot be reached
Prob
e
Failure action
Purpose
Trap
Liven
ess
Restart the container
Detect a deadlocked or wedged
process
Too aggressive = restart loop on a healthy but slow
app. Never point it at a dependency
Readi
ness
Remove the pod from Service
endpoints
Do not send traffic to a pod that
cannot serve
The correct place to check dependencies
Start
up
Delay liveness until the app has
started
Slow-starting applications, e.g. large
JVMs
Without it you must inflate initialDelaySeconds,
which delays real failure detection
THE LIVENESS/READINESS DISTINCTION WINS INTERVIEWS
"Readiness controls traffic; liveness controls restarts. If a pod cannot reach its database, readiness should fail so it stops
receiving traffic - but liveness should NOT fail, because restarting the container will not fix the database and you will
just churn the whole deployment into a restart loop while the database recovers. That is the most common probe
mistake I have seen."

11.3 Deployments and ReplicaSets
  DEPLOYMENT  payments-api  (desired: 3 replicas, image 1.4.2)
      |
      | manages, one per pod-template revision
      v
  REPLICASET  payments-api-7d4f8   (image 1.4.2, desired 3)   <- current
  REPLICASET  payments-api-6b2c1   (image 1.4.1, desired 0)   <- kept for rollback
      |
      | creates
      v
  POD payments-api-7d4f8-abc12   POD ...-def34   POD ...-ghi56
 
  Rolling update: the Deployment scales the NEW ReplicaSet up and the OLD one down,
  step by step, honouring maxSurge and maxUnavailable, waiting for readiness at each step.
  Rollback = scale the old ReplicaSet back up. That is why old ReplicaSets are retained.
The ownership chain, and how a rollout works
A Deployment with a controlled rollout
 apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: payments-api
  labels: { app: payments-api }
spec:
  replicas: 3
  revisionHistoryLimit: 5
  minReadySeconds: 10                    # a pod must stay ready this long before counting
  progressDeadlineSeconds: 600           # rollout fails after this -> "progress deadline exceeded"
 
  selector:
    matchLabels: { app: payments-api }   # IMMUTABLE - must match the template labels
 
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxSurge: 1                        # 1 extra pod allowed above replicas
      maxUnavailable: 0                  # never go below 3 ready - zero downtime
 
  template:
    metadata:
      labels: { app: payments-api }
      annotations:
        prometheus.io/scrape: "true"
        prometheus.io/port: "8080"
    spec:
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: registry.example.com/payments-api:1.4.2
          ports: [{ containerPort: 8080 }]
          resources:
            requests: { memory: "256Mi", cpu: "250m" }
            limits:   { memory: "512Mi", cpu: "1000m" }
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet: { path: /ready, port: 8080 }
            initialDelaySeconds: 5
            periodSeconds: 5
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 45
Rollout management
 kubectl rollout status deployment/payments-api          # wait for it to finish - use in CI
kubectl rollout history deployment/payments-api
kubectl rollout history deployment/payments-api --revision=3
kubectl rollout undo deployment/payments-api            # back to the previous revision
kubectl rollout undo deployment/payments-api --to-revision=3
kubectl rollout pause deployment/payments-api           # freeze mid-rollout (manual canary)
kubectl rollout resume deployment/payments-api
kubectl rollout restart deployment/payments-api         # recreate all pods (pick up a new Secret)
kubectl set image deployment/payments-api api=registry.example.com/payments-api:1.4.3
kubectl scale deployment/payments-api --replicas=6

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK

Page 146
KUBECTL ROLLOUT RESTART IS A USEFUL THING TO KNOW
Changing a ConfigMap or Secret does NOT restart pods automatically - environment variables from them are injected
at pod start, so the running pods keep the old values. kubectl rollout restart deployment/x triggers a rolling
recreation. The production-grade alternative is annotating the pod template with a checksum of the config so any
config change naturally produces a new pod template and a rollout.
11.4 Services and Networking
 Service type
What it does
Use
ClusterIP (default)
A stable virtual IP reachable only inside the cluster

Opens the same high port (30000-32767) on every
node

Provisions a cloud load balancer pointing at the nodes
Exposing a service on a cloud provider

Returns a CNAME to an external DNS name
Pointing at a managed database or an external

None)

Service and Ingress
 apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: payments-api
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector:
    app: payments-api          # THIS is what wires the Service to pods - labels, not names
  ports:
    - name: http
      port: 80                 # the Service's port
      targetPort: 8080         # the container's port
      protocol: TCP
---
apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: payments-api
  annotations:
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/ssl-redirect: "true"
    nginx.ingress.kubernetes.io/proxy-body-size: "10m"
    cert-manager.io/cluster-issuer: letsencrypt-prod
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx
  tls:
    - hosts: [api.example.com]
      secretName: api-tls
  rules:
    - host: api.example.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /payments
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service:
                name: payments-api
                port: { number: 80 }

How a request reaches a pod
ENDPOINTS ARE THE DIAGNOSTIC
"A Service does not point at pods by name - it selects them by label, and the set of READY matching pods becomes its
Endpoints. So when a Service returns nothing, the first command is kubectl get endpoints <svc>. Empty endpoints
means either the selector does not match any pod's labels, or the pods exist but are failing their readiness probe. That
one command splits the problem in half immediately."
11.5 ConfigMaps and Secrets
Both, and the three ways to consume them
 apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata: { name: payments-config }
data:
  db_host: "postgres.data.svc.cluster.local"
  log_level: "INFO"
  app.properties: |
    server.port=8080
    server.threads=32
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Secret
metadata: { name: payments-secret }
type: Opaque
data:
  db_password: c3VwZXJzZWNyZXQ=        # base64 - ENCODING, not encryption
stringData:
  api_key: "plain-text-here"            # Kubernetes base64-encodes this for you
---
# Consuming them
spec:
  containers:
    - name: api
      env:
        - name: LOG_LEVEL
          valueFrom: { configMapKeyRef: { name: payments-config, key: log_level } }
        - name: DB_PASSWORD
          valueFrom: { secretKeyRef: { name: payments-secret, key: db_password } }
      envFrom:
        - configMapRef: { name: payments-config }     # every key becomes an env var
      volumeMounts:
        - name: config
          mountPath: /etc/app                          # each key becomes a file
  volumes:
    - name: config
      configMap: { name: payments-config }
ConfigMap
Secret
For
Non-sensitive configuration
Passwords, tokens, keys, certificates
Storage
Plaintext in etcd
base64 in etcd - encrypted at rest only if encryption-at-rest is
enabled
Size limit
1 MiB
1 MiB

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK

Page 148
ConfigMap
Secret
Mounted as a volume
Updates propagate after a delay
Same
As environment
variables
Does NOT update - pod restart required
Same
SECRETS ARE NOT ENCRYPTED BY DEFAULT
base64 is encoding, and anyone who can run kubectl get secret -o yaml can decode it instantly. Real protection
requires: encryption at rest for etcd, RBAC restricting who can read Secrets in that namespace, and ideally an external
store - AWS Secrets Manager via the Secrets Store CSI driver, or External Secrets Operator. Saying "Secrets are base64,
not encrypted" is a strong signal in an interview; assuming they are secure is a weak one.
11.6 The Other Objects
 Object

Key property

Logical partition of a cluster
Scope for names, RBAC, quotas and network policies

Maintains N identical pods
You rarely create one directly - Deployments own them

Pods with stable identity and storage
Ordered creation, stable names (app-0, app-1), a PVC per

One pod per node (or per matching node)

completions, parallelism, backoffLimit

A piece of storage in the cluster
Provisioned by an admin or dynamically by a StorageClass

(PVC)
A request for storage by a pod
Binds to a PV; the pod references the claim

A template for dynamic provisioning
Defines the provisioner, parameters and reclaim policy

CPU, memory or custom metrics

Pod-level firewall
Default is allow-all; a policy selecting a pod makes it

Namespace capacity control and per-pod
defaults
Prevents one team consuming the cluster

Minimum availability during voluntary
