monitored - CloudWatch has no memory
metric without the agent, which is a very
common gap
PREVENTI
ON
Memory metrics and alerting; container limits
set from measured usage; heap sized against
the limit; alert on restart count, not just on
memory
16.5 Incident 4 - Disk Full
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTO
M
"No space left on device", writes fail,
database refuses connections, services will
not start, sshd may fail
POSSIBL
E
CAUSES
Unrotated logs; a log level left at DEBUG;
Docker images and stopped containers;
core dumps; a runaway temp file;
deleted-but-open files; inode exhaustion
with blocks free
FIRST
CHECK
df -h AND df -i - inodes are the case
people miss
COMMA
NDS
df -h, df -i, `du -xh --max-depth=1 / \
sort -h, then descend; lsof
+L1 or lsof \
grep deleted; docker system df; journalctl
--disk-usage`
LOGS
Which directory grew, and since when - ls
-ltr in the offender
ISOLATI
ON
One filesystem or all? One host or the
whole fleet? A fleet-wide fill usually means a
logging change shipped in a release
FIX
Reclaim safely: compress and archive logs
rather than deleting live ones; journalctl
--vacuum-size=200M; docker system
prune -af --filter "until=72h"; clear
package caches. If space does not return
after deleting, a process holds the
descriptor - restart it. Then grow the
volume: aws ec2 modify-volume,
growpart, resize2fs
VALIDAT
ION
df -h shows headroom, services recovered,
writes succeed
RCA
What produced the data and why nothing
bounded it

16.6 Incident 5 - Application Down
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Users report errors; health checks failing; 5xx at the load balancer
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Process crashed or never started after a deploy; dependency unavailable; config or secret error;
port conflict; resource exhaustion; certificate expiry
FIRST CHECK
Is the process running and listening? systemctl status app and `ss -tulpn \
grep :8080`
COMMANDS
systemctl status app -l, journalctl -u app -n 200 --no-pager, ss -tulpn, curl -v
localhost:8080/health, kubectl get pods and kubectl logs --previous
LOGS
Application log first, then the proxy's error log, then the platform's events
ISOLATION
Works on localhost but not remotely = network/bind address. Fails on localhost = the
application. One instance or all?
FIX
If it followed a deploy, roll back first. Otherwise restart with evidence preserved, fix the config,
or restore the dependency
VALIDATION
Health endpoint 200 from the load balancer's perspective, error rate back to baseline, and
confirm with real traffic not just a local curl
RCA
The failure and the detection gap - how long between failure and alert?
PREVENTION
Readiness probes and load balancer health checks; alert on healthy host count; automated
rollback on a failed deployment health check
16.7 Incident 6 - Database Unavailable
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Application errors mentioning connection refused, timeout, too many connections, or authentication failure
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Database down or failing over; connection pool exhausted or leaked; max_connections reached; credentials
rotated; security group change; disk full on the database; a long-running lock; DNS pointing at the old
endpoint after failover
FIRST CHECK
Can the application host reach the database port at all? nc -zv db-host 5432 - that separates network from
database
COMMANDS
nc -zv, dig db-host, psql -h host -U user -c "select 1" or mysql -h host -e "select 1", RDS
CloudWatch DatabaseConnections, FreeStorageSpace, CPUUtilization, ReplicaLag, and SHOW PROCESSLIST /
pg_stat_activity
LOGS
Application connection errors with exact messages; RDS logs; slow query log
ISOLATION
Refused = reachable, not listening or not accepting. Timeout = network/security group. Auth error =
credentials. Too many connections = pooling
FIX
Restart the application to release leaked connections as mitigation; raise max_connections or add a pooler
like PgBouncer; kill a blocking query; fail over; correct the security group or endpoint
VALIDATION
Connections succeed, error rate falls, connection count stable rather than climbing
RCA
Pool leak versus real load versus a change; also why the application did not degrade gracefully
PREVENTION
Connection pooling with sane limits and timeouts; alert on connection count approaching max and on replica
lag; retry with backoff in the application; Multi-AZ with tested failover
16.8 Incident 7 - Jenkins Failure


===== PAGE 205 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 16  |  PRODUCTION TROUBLESHOOTING HANDBOOK
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 205
Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Builds failing, queued forever, or the UI is unavailable
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Agent offline; disk full on controller or agent; expired credentials; plugin upgrade; Git host unreachable; a
moved base image or dependency; JVM heap exhaustion on the controller
FIRST CHECK
Compare the console log of the last good build with the first bad one and find the first line that differs
COMMANDS
On the agent: df -h, systemctl status jenkins, agent log. In Jenkins: Manage Jenkins -> Nodes, System
Log; journalctl -u jenkins
LOGS
Build console output from the top; the node's log; $JENKINS_HOME/logs
ISOLATION
One job or all jobs? One agent or all? A single job means the job or its dependency; all jobs means the
controller, the network or a plugin
FIX
Free disk (the number one cause of an agent going offline), reconnect or replace the agent, roll back the
plugin, rotate the credential, pin the dependency that moved
VALIDATION
Re-run the failing build and a known-good build; confirm agents are online with free executors
RCA
Usually "something external moved" - an unpinned dependency or plugin - or disk
PREVENTION
buildDiscarder and cleanWs(); disk alerts on agents; pinned plugin versions tested on a staging controller;
pinned shared library versions; ephemeral agents so state cannot accumulate
16.9 Incident 8 - Docker Failure
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Containers will not start, restart repeatedly, or the daemon is unresponsive
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Image pull failure; bad configuration or missing env var; port conflict; OOM; disk full on /var/lib/docker;
permissions on a bind mount; daemon crash
FIRST CHECK
docker ps -a for exit codes, then docker logs --tail 100 <name>
COMMANDS
docker ps -a, docker logs --tail 200 <c>, docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}}
{{.State.OOMKilled}}' <c>, docker system df, systemctl status docker, journalctl -u docker
LOGS
Container logs; the daemon's journal
ISOLATION
Exit 137 = OOM. 127 = command not found. 126 = not executable. 1 = application error. 0 repeatedly = the
process finishes (a daemonising process that needs foreground mode)
FIX
Correct the image tag or registry credentials; fix the env/config; raise the memory limit; prune disk; correct
bind-mount ownership or run with the right UID
VALIDATION
Container stays up through several minutes and passes its health check
RCA
Configuration, resources or image
PREVENTION
Immutable image tags; health checks; memory limits; log driver size limits; scheduled prune on build hosts
16.10 Incident 9 - Kubernetes Failure
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Pods not running, service unreachable, rollout stuck
POSSIBLE CAUSES
See Part 11 - every Kubernetes incident reduces to one of: scheduling, image, crash, readiness, endpoints,
DNS, resources or storage
FIRST CHECK
kubectl get pods -o wide then kubectl describe pod <pod> - the Events section states the cause
COMMANDS
kubectl get pods -o wide, kubectl describe pod, kubectl logs --previous, kubectl get events
--sort-by=.lastTimestamp, kubectl get endpoints, kubectl top pods, kubectl describe node
LOGS
Container logs including --previous; cluster events; kubelet journal on the node
ISOLATION
Failures on one node = node problem. Across all nodes = application or config. Pending = scheduling.
CrashLoop = application. Running but unreachable = readiness or Service
FIX
kubectl rollout undo to restore service first, then fix. Cordon and drain a bad node
VALIDATION
Pods Ready, endpoints populated, rollout status completes, health endpoint responds through the
Service

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 16  |  PRODUCTION TROUBLESHOOTING HANDBOOK
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 206
Stage
Detail
RCA
Which of the eight categories, and why the pipeline did not catch it
PREVENTION
Resource requests and limits; correct probes; PodDisruptionBudgets; anti-affinity; a deployment gate that
waits on rollout status and rolls back on failure
16.11 Incident 10 - Network Timeout
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Requests hang then fail; intermittent failures; slow responses from one dependency
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Security group or NACL; route table; NAT gateway problem; packet loss on a path; MTU on a tunnel; DNS
returning a dead IP; the target genuinely overloaded
FIRST CHECK
Timeout or refused? Timeout means dropped packets, refused means reachable-but-not-listening
COMMANDS
nc -zv host port, mtr host over a few minutes for per-hop loss, traceroute, dig, ip route get IP, VPC
Flow Logs filtered on REJECT
LOGS
Flow logs; load balancer access logs; application client-side timeout logs
ISOLATION
Test from the same subnet, then from another subnet, then from outside. Each hop that works eliminates a
layer
FIX
Correct the security group, NACL (remember the ephemeral return range) or route; replace a failing NAT
gateway; lower MTU or enable MSS clamping
VALIDATION
Consistent connectivity over a sustained test, not a single successful curl
RCA
Which layer dropped the traffic and what changed
PREVENTION
Flow logs enabled; NAT gateway per AZ; connection and read timeouts set in every client; synthetic checks
between tiers
16.12 Incident 11 - DNS Issue
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
"Could not resolve host"; intermittent failures; traffic going to an old endpoint
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Record missing or wrong; TTL caching an old value; resolver unreachable; CoreDNS down or throttled in
Kubernetes; a stale /etc/hosts; split-horizon DNS; JVM caching resolution forever
FIRST CHECK
dig name and dig @8.8.8.8 name - if the authoritative answer is right but yours is wrong, it is caching or
your resolver
COMMANDS
dig, dig +trace, getent hosts, cat /etc/resolv.conf, nslookup from inside a pod, CoreDNS pod status
and logs
LOGS
CoreDNS logs; resolver logs; application DNS error messages
ISOLATION
Fails everywhere = the record. Fails from one network = that resolver. Fails intermittently = one of several
records is dead, or CoreDNS is under-provisioned
FIX
Correct the record; flush or wait out the TTL; restore the resolver; scale CoreDNS; remove the stale hosts entry
VALIDATION
Resolution correct from multiple vantage points and consistently over time
RCA
Change management around DNS, and TTL planning
PREVENTION
Lower TTLs before planned changes; monitor resolution latency and CoreDNS errors; avoid hardcoding IPs;
set the JVM DNS TTL explicitly
16.13 Incident 12 - SSL/TLS Issue
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTO
M
Browser warnings; certificate verify
failed; handshake failures; a client that
suddenly cannot connect

POSSIBL
E
CAUSES
Expired certificate; missing intermediate
chain; hostname/SAN mismatch; untrusted
internal CA; protocol or cipher mismatch
after hardening; clock skew
FIRST
CHECK
`echo \
openssl s_client -connect host:443
-servername host 2>/dev/null \

ON
Fails in curl but works in a browser =

16.14 Incident 13 - Deployment Failure
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Pipeline red at the deploy stage, or green but the application is broken
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Bad artifact; missing config or secret in the target environment; incompatible database migration; insufficient
capacity; health check misconfigured; permissions on the deploy credential
FIRST CHECK
Which stage failed and what was the exit code? Then: are the new instances or pods healthy?
COMMANDS
Pipeline console log; kubectl rollout status, kubectl describe pod, kubectl logs --previous;
target group health; systemctl status on the target
LOGS
Deploy logs, application startup logs, platform events
ISOLATION
Did the artifact change, the config change, or the environment change? Diff the working release against the
failing one
FIX
Roll back immediately - previous image tag, rollout undo, or previous release symlink - then reproduce in a
lower environment
VALIDATION
Health checks pass, error rate at baseline, and a smoke test of the critical user path
RCA
Why staging did not catch it - usually an environment difference in config, data or scale
PREVENTION
Build once and promote the same artifact; environment parity; smoke tests in the pipeline; automated
rollback on a failed health check; progressive delivery so the blast radius is small
16.15 Incident 14 - Rollback Required
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
The new release is live and causing errors


===== PAGE 208 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 16  |  PRODUCTION TROUBLESHOOTING HANDBOOK
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 208
Stage
Detail
DECISION
Roll back if: users are affected, the cause is not obvious within a few minutes, or the fix is not trivially safe. Fix forward
only if the fix is one line, tested, and rollback is impossible - for example after an irreversible migration
COMMANDS
kubectl rollout undo deployment/app; redeploy the previous image tag; re-run the last good pipeline; repoint
the blue-green load balancer; ln -sfn the previous release directory
CAUTION
Rollback covers code, not data. A migration that dropped a column or rewrote rows cannot be undone by
redeploying the old image. Check migrations before rolling back
VALIDATION
Error rate returns to baseline; confirm the running version by image digest, not by the tag
RCA
Then, with service restored, investigate the failing artifact - which you still have because tags are immutable
PREVENTION
Backward-compatible migrations using expand-and-contract; feature flags so a behaviour change can be disabled
without redeploying; rehearsed, automated rollback
16.16 Incident 15 - Monitoring Alert Storm
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Dozens of alerts at once, or an alert that fires and clears repeatedly
POSSIBLE CAUSES
One root cause with many symptom alerts (a node or AZ failure); a monitoring outage producing false alarms;
thresholds too tight; no for: duration; a maintenance window without a silence
FIRST CHECK
Look at the earliest alert by timestamp - the first one is usually the cause and the rest are symptoms
COMMANDS
Alertmanager UI grouped view; kubectl get nodes; cloud health dashboard; check whether Prometheus
itself is healthy
ISOLATION
Do all the alerts share a label - the same node, AZ, cluster or service? That is your root cause
FIX
Address the single root cause; silence the symptom alerts while you work so the channel stays readable
VALIDATION
Alerts clear on their own, not by being silenced
RCA
Both the technical cause and the alerting design that produced fifty notifications for one event
PREVENTION
Inhibition rules so a cause suppresses symptoms; grouping in Alertmanager; for: durations; symptom-based
alerting; a regular review of which alerts fired and what action followed
16.17 Incident 16 - Application Latency
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
Requests slow, p95/p99 up, timeouts at the edge, no outright errors
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Slow database query or missing index; connection or thread pool saturation; GC pauses; CPU throttling from
a container limit; a slow downstream dependency; disk I/O or exhausted EBS burst balance; DNS resolution
latency; traffic increase without capacity
FIRST CHECK
Where is the time spent? Compare edge latency with application latency with database latency - the gap tells
you which layer
COMMANDS
curl -w '%{time_namelookup} %{time_connect} %{time_starttransfer} %{time_total}' to split
DNS, connect and server time; kubectl top pods; container_cpu_cfs_throttled_seconds_total; slow
query log; iostat -xz 1; EBS BurstBalance
LOGS
Application timing logs, slow query log, proxy upstream response time
ISOLATION
All endpoints or one? All instances or one? Latency proportional to load, or constant? Constant added latency
usually means DNS, a retry, or a handshake; load-proportional means saturation
FIX
Add the index, raise the pool, remove the CPU limit bottleneck, scale out, cache, or make the slow path
asynchronous
VALIDATION
p95 and p99 back to baseline under the same traffic, not just the average
RCA
Which resource saturated first, and why capacity headroom was not monitored
PREVENTION
Percentile-based latency SLOs and alerts; capacity headroom alerting; load testing before releases; query
performance review in code review
16.18 Incident 17 - HTTP 500


===== PAGE 209 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 16  |  PRODUCTION TROUBLESHOOTING HANDBOOK
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 209
Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
The application returns 500 to users
MEANING
The application code raised an unhandled error - so DNS, TCP, TLS and routing all worked
FIRST CHECK
The application log for the stack trace, correlated to the exact request time; then whether it started at a deploy
COMMANDS
kubectl logs -f deploy/app, journalctl -u app, Logs Insights filtered on ERROR and grouped by endpoint,
and the ALB HTTPCode_Target_5XX_Count metric
ISOLATION
Every request or some? One endpoint or all? One instance or all? Started with a deploy or with a dependency
failure?
FIX
Roll back if it followed a release; otherwise restore the dependency or correct the configuration
VALIDATION
5xx rate back to zero at the load balancer, not just "the error disappeared from the log"
RCA
The specific exception, plus why an unhandled exception reached the user
PREVENTION
Error rate alerting; tests covering that path; graceful degradation so a dependency failure returns a controlled
response instead of a 500
16.19 Incident 18 - HTTP 502
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
The proxy or load balancer returns 502 Bad Gateway
MEANING
The proxy reached a backend but got an invalid response or the connection was closed unexpectedly
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Backend crashed or is restarting (OOMKilled is classic); proxy pointing at the wrong port; backend speaking
HTTPS while the proxy expects HTTP; response headers too large; backend killed mid-response; keep-alive
timeout mismatch where the backend closes the connection before the load balancer does
COMMANDS
Proxy error log (/var/log/nginx/error.log); curl -v http://backend:8080/health from the proxy
host; kubectl get pods restart counts; ALB HTTPCode_ELB_5XX versus Target_5XX
ISOLATION
Intermittent 502 with healthy pods usually means the keep-alive timeout mismatch; constant 502 means
wrong port, wrong protocol, or a dead backend
FIX
Restore the backend; correct the upstream port or protocol; increase header buffers; set the backend's
keep-alive timeout above the load balancer's idle timeout
VALIDATION
Sustained clean requests, including at the rate that previously produced errors
RCA
Which of the causes, and whether the backend was crashing silently
PREVENTION
Restart-count alerting; correct memory limits; a documented port and protocol contract between proxy and
service; keep-alive timings reviewed
16.20 Incident 19 - HTTP 503
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
503 Service Unavailable
MEANING
There is no healthy backend to serve the request
POSSIBLE CAUSES
All targets failing health checks; a Kubernetes Service with zero endpoints because no pod is Ready; a
deployment that scaled to zero; deliberate maintenance mode; rate limiting; the upstream queue full
COMMANDS
kubectl get endpoints <svc> - empty is the answer; kubectl get pods for readiness; ALB target group
health and its failure reason; nginx upstream status
ISOLATION
Zero endpoints = readiness or selector. Unhealthy targets = the health check path, port or timing. Rate
limiting = check the proxy's configuration
FIX
Get one backend Ready. Very often the fix is the readiness probe itself - wrong path, wrong port, or a timeout
shorter than the application's response time under load
VALIDATION
Healthy host count above zero and stable; 503 rate at zero
RCA
Why every replica became unhealthy simultaneously - a common cause is a liveness or readiness probe that
checks a shared dependency


===== PAGE 210 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 16  |  PRODUCTION TROUBLESHOOTING HANDBOOK
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 210
Stage
Detail
PREVENTION
Probes that test the process, not its dependencies; minimum replica counts; PodDisruptionBudgets; alerting
on healthy host count with missing data treated as breaching
16.21 Incident 20 - HTTP 504
 Stage
Detail
SYMPTOM
504 Gateway Timeout after a long wait
MEANING
The backend was reached but did not respond within the proxy's timeout
POSSIBLE CAUSES
Slow database query; a downstream API hanging; thread or connection pool exhausted; deadlock; proxy
timeout shorter than the real processing time; an unbounded operation such as a full-table report
COMMANDS
curl -w '%{time_total}' directly against the backend, bypassing the proxy; slow query log; thread dump
for a JVM; kubectl top for throttling; upstream response time metrics
ISOLATION
If the backend is also slow when called directly, it is the application or its dependencies. If it is fast directly but
times out through the proxy, it is the proxy configuration or connection pooling in between
FIX
Fix the slow path - index the query, add a cache, bound the result set. Raising the timeout is only correct
when the work genuinely takes that long, and then the right answer is usually to make it asynchronous with a
job and a status endpoint
VALIDATION
p99 latency well below the timeout; zero 504s under the same load
RCA
Which operation exceeded the budget, and why there was no timeout inside the application
PREVENTION
Timeouts at every layer, decreasing inward; circuit breakers on downstream calls; query performance gates;
latency SLOs alerting before the timeout is reached
16.22 The Incident Communication Template
What to post in the incident channel, every 15-30 minutes
 [INCIDENT] SEV2 - Payments API elevated errors
Status:   INVESTIGATING | IDENTIFIED | MONITORING | RESOLVED
Started:  2026-09-23 14:02 UTC (detected 14:05 by alert HighErrorRate)
Impact:   ~12% of payment requests returning 500. Checkout affected; browsing unaffected.
Current:  Error rate 12%, stable, not increasing.
Actions:  Rolled back release 1.4.3 to 1.4.2 at 14:18. Monitoring recovery.
Next:     Confirm error rate at baseline by 14:35, then investigate 1.4.3 in staging.
Owner:    Aasim  |  Next update: 14:35 UTC
COMMUNICATION IS HALF OF INCIDENT RESPONSE
Interviewers ask "what do you do during an incident?" partly to see whether you mention communication at all. Say: "I
post a status update on a fixed cadence even when there is nothing new, because silence makes people interrupt the
responders to ask. The update says impact in user terms, what we have done, what we are doing next, and when the
next update will be."
16.23 The RCA Template
A blameless RCA that produces real change
 TITLE       Payments API 500 errors for 34 minutes on 2026-09-23
 
SUMMARY     Release 1.4.3 introduced a configuration key that was absent in production,
            causing the payment service to throw on startup for 12% of requests.
            Resolved by rolling back to 1.4.2.
 
IMPACT      34 minutes. Approximately 12% of payment requests failed.
            No data loss. No customer data exposed.
 
TIMELINE (UTC)
  14:02  Release 1.4.3 deployed to production
  14:05  HighErrorRate alert fired (3 min detection)
  14:07  On-call acknowledged; incident channel opened
  14:12  Error correlated with the deploy; stack trace identified a missing config key


===== PAGE 211 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 16  |  PRODUCTION TROUBLESHOOTING HANDBOOK
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 211
  14:18  Rollback to 1.4.2 initiated
  14:24  Error rate returned to baseline
  14:36  Monitoring period complete; incident closed
 
ROOT CAUSE  The new config key was added to the staging config map but not to production.
            The application read it at startup without a default and raised an exception.
 
DETECTION   Alert fired 3 minutes after deploy. Adequate, but the deployment pipeline
            did not gate on post-deploy error rate, so the bad release stayed live.
 
MITIGATION  Rollback to the previous immutable image tag.
 
WHAT WENT WELL
  - Immutable tags made rollback a one-command operation.
  - The alert fired quickly and named the affected service.
 
WHAT DID NOT
  - Configuration drift between staging and production went undetected.
  - No automated rollback on post-deploy error-rate regression.
  - The application failed hard on a missing optional key instead of defaulting.
 
ACTION ITEMS
  1. Config schema validation in CI, comparing environments   OWNER: Aasim   BY: 2026-10-07
  2. Post-deploy error-rate gate with automatic rollback      OWNER: Aasim   BY: 2026-10-14
  3. Application defaults for non-critical config keys        OWNER: Dev     BY: 2026-10-14
PART 16 RECALL POINTS
 Always: what changed, blast radius, when it started, is it worsening.
 Mitigate first, preserve evidence, diagnose after.
 Refused versus timeout splits every network problem in half.
 500 = app, 502 = bad backend response, 503 = no healthy backend, 504 = too slow.
 df -i as well as df -h; lsof | grep deleted for space that does not return.
 Look at available memory, not free; exit 137 means OOM.
 Roll back on any doubt - but check whether a migration makes rollback unsafe.
 Every RCA ends in action items with an owner and a date.
 Communicate on a fixed cadence, even with nothing new.

Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
PART 17
SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
50 scenarios, each with what is being tested and how to answer
Part 17. SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
Scenario questions are where offers are decided. The interviewer is not looking for the right answer - they are looking
for a method. This part gives you, for each scenario: what the interviewer is actually testing, a strong answer, the
step-by-step approach, the commands, likely follow-ups, and the wrong answers that lose marks.
THE UNIVERSAL SCENARIO-ANSWER STRUCTURE
1) Clarify and scope - "Is it all users or some? When did it start? Has anything been deployed?" Asking this first is itself
a scoring point.
2) State the goal - "My first priority is restoring service; I will preserve evidence as I go."
3) Work in layers - narrow from user-visible symptom to the failing component, naming the command at each step.
4) Say what each result would mean - "If it is refused rather than timing out, I know the network path is fine."
5) Fix, validate, and prevent - always finish with how you would stop it recurring.
17.1 Scenarios 1-10: Availability
Q. 1. Production application suddenly stopped responding. What do you do?
 Testing: whether you panic, whether you have a method, and whether you mitigate before diagnosing. Answer: "First I
establish scope and timing - is it all users or a subset, one environment or all, and when did it start, because that usually
points at a change. In parallel I check whether anything was deployed in the last hour, since most sudden failures follow a
change. Then I work from the outside in: is DNS resolving correctly, is the load balancer reporting healthy targets, are the
instances or pods running and Ready, is the application listening on its port, and can it reach its dependencies. The load
balancer's healthy host count and the split between ELB 5xx and target 5xx tell me immediately whether it is the platform or
the application. If it correlates with a deploy, I roll back first and investigate afterwards - restoring users is the priority, and
the rollback also confirms the hypothesis. I communicate status on a fixed cadence throughout, and once it is stable I do the
RCA and turn it into action items." Follow-ups: what if there was no deploy; how do you preserve evidence; what if rollback
does not fix it. Wrong answers: jumping straight to "restart the server"; diagnosing root cause for twenty minutes while
users are down; not mentioning communication.
Q. 2. Users report the site is slow but nothing is erroring. Where do you start?
 Testing: whether you can reason about latency rather than availability. Answer: "I split the request path and find where the
time goes. curl -w gives me DNS lookup, connect, TLS and time-to-first-byte separately, which immediately tells me
whether it is name resolution, the network, or the server. Then I compare load balancer latency with application latency with
database latency - the layer where the jump appears is the culprit. I look at percentiles, not averages, because an average
hides the tail that users actually notice. The usual causes are a slow query or missing index, a saturated connection or thread
pool, CPU throttling from a container limit, GC pauses, or exhausted EBS burst credits. Then I check whether latency is
proportional to load, which means saturation, or constant, which usually means a handshake, a retry or DNS." Follow-ups:
how would you find the slow query; what is CPU throttling; how do you decide between scaling and optimising.
Q. 3. Only some users are affected. What does that tell you?
 Testing: whether you use blast radius as a diagnostic. Answer: "It eliminates whole classes of cause. If it were DNS or a
certificate, everyone would be affected. A subset usually means one of a few things: one instance or pod in the pool is
unhealthy but still receiving traffic, so I check per-target metrics rather than aggregates; or one availability zone is affected;
or it is data-dependent - a particular account, region or feature flag. I test each backend directly rather than through the
load balancer, because an aggregate graph averages away a single bad host. If it is a canary or partial rollout, that is the
obvious first suspect."
Q. 4. Everything looks healthy in monitoring but users say it is broken. What now?
 Testing: whether you trust dashboards over users. Answer: "The users are right and my monitoring has a gap - that is the
starting assumption. I reproduce it from the user's position: from outside the network, through the real DNS name, with a
real browser or client. Internal checks bypass DNS, the CDN, the WAF and certificate validation, which is exactly where these


===== PAGE 213 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 213
failures live. Synthetic monitoring from outside exists for this reason. Then I close the monitoring gap as an action item,
because the incident was really two failures - the outage and the blind spot."
Q. 5. Production is down and you have no idea why. What are your first three commands?
 Testing: prioritisation under pressure. Answer: "It depends on the platform, but the shape is the same: first something that
tells me whether the service is reachable at all - the load balancer's healthy target count, or curl -v against the health
endpoint. Second, the state of the workloads - kubectl get pods -o wide or systemctl status, which tells me whether
things are running, restarting or gone. Third, df -h and free -h on the hosts, because a full disk or exhausted memory
causes an astonishing proportion of unexplained outages. In parallel I ask what changed in the last hour - that question
often answers the whole incident before any command does."
Q. 6. An entire availability zone goes down. What happens to your application?
 Testing: architectural understanding of failure domains. Answer: "If the design is correct, capacity drops but the service
stays up: the load balancer stops sending traffic to targets in that AZ, the Auto Scaling Group launches replacements in the
healthy AZs, and an RDS Multi-AZ deployment fails over automatically. What breaks it is anything that exists only once - a
single NAT gateway, a single-AZ database, or a stateful workload with a volume bound to that AZ. So my answer is that the
honest test is to trace the request path and name every component with only one instance, because that list is the real
availability of the system."
Q. 7. A deployment completed successfully but users still see the old version.
 Testing: understanding of immutability and caching layers. Answer: "I verify what is actually running rather than what the
pipeline believes - the image digest, not the tag. The most common cause by far is a mutable tag like latest with
imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent, so the node reused the cached image. Second, check whether pods actually restarted - if
their age predates the deploy, the pod template never changed, which happens when only a ConfigMap was updated. Third,
whether old targets are still in the load balancer's rotation. Fourth, caching further out - CDN, browser, or a client holding a
keep-alive connection to an old instance. The permanent fix is immutable tags per build and deploying by digest."
Q. 8. Your health check passes but the application does not work.
 Testing: whether you understand what a health check should assert. Answer: "The health check is testing the wrong thing -
typically it returns 200 from the web framework without touching anything the application actually needs. A useful readiness
check verifies the things whose absence makes the instance unable to serve: can it reach its database, is its cache available,
has it finished loading configuration. The balance matters though - a readiness check may test dependencies, but a liveness
check must not, otherwise a database blip restarts every replica and turns a small problem into an outage."
Q. 9. The application works in staging but fails in production.
 Testing: whether you can reason about environment parity. Answer: "Something differs and my job is to find what. In order
of likelihood: configuration and secrets that exist in one environment and not the other; data - production has volume, edge
cases and legacy rows staging does not; scale - concurrency, connection pool limits and timeouts only bite under real load;
network - production has stricter security groups, private subnets and a proxy; and permissions - a production IAM role that
is more restricted. I diff the configuration between environments first, because that is the single most common answer, and
it is also the one worth automating a check for."
Q. 10. Traffic doubled unexpectedly. What happens and what do you do?
 Testing: capacity thinking. Answer: "First, is it legitimate traffic or an attack or a retry storm - checking the source
distribution and the request pattern answers that. If legitimate, I check where saturation is appearing: application CPU and
memory, database connections, and any fixed-size pool. Autoscaling handles the stateless tier if the policy is on a
meaningful metric, but the database usually does not scale that way, so the bottleneck moves there. Short term I scale out,
raise pool sizes carefully, and shed load if necessary via rate limiting rather than letting everything degrade. Then the RCA is
about capacity headroom - whether we would have been alerted before saturation rather than at it."
17.2 Scenarios 11-20: CI/CD and Deployment
Q. 11. The Jenkins pipeline worked yesterday and fails today with no code change.
 Testing: systematic reasoning about "nothing changed". Answer: "Something always changed, just not in our repository. I
diff the console log of the last successful build against the first failing one and find the first line that differs - that usually
names the cause directly. Then the candidates: a plugin or Jenkins upgrade on the controller; an agent rebuilt with different
tool versions; a mutable dependency or base image tag that moved upstream; an expired credential or certificate; disk filling
on an agent; or an external service such as the registry or artifact repository being unavailable. Disk on the agent and a
moved upstream dependency are the two most frequent in my experience." Follow-up: how do you prevent it - pin
everything, ephemeral agents, disk alerts.


===== PAGE 214 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 214
Q. 12. A build passes locally but fails in CI.
 Testing: understanding of build reproducibility. Answer: "The difference is almost always environmental. Locally I have a
warm cache that may be hiding a broken step, a different tool version, different environment variables, and often different
architecture. CI has a clean workspace, restricted network and different credentials. I reproduce by building with --no-cache
and in a container matching the CI image. The structural fix is to make the build hermetic - containerised builds with pinned
tool versions, so local and CI are the same environment by construction."
Q. 13. Your pipeline deploys to production automatically. Is that a good idea?
 Testing: judgement, not dogma. Answer: "It depends on what supports it. Continuous deployment needs high automated
test confidence, feature flags so unfinished work can ship dark, progressive rollout so a bad release reaches few users, and
automated rollback triggered by metrics. With those, it is safer than batching changes into a large monthly release. Without
them, it just ships failures faster. In my current environment we do continuous delivery with a controlled production step,
which fits a product where QA validates releases. I would build the safety mechanisms first and then remove the manual
gate, not the other way round."
Q. 14. A pipeline takes 45 minutes. Reduce it.
 Testing: whether you measure before optimising. Answer: "First I measure per-stage duration - timestamps() in Jenkins or
the stage timings - because optimising the wrong stage is wasted work. Then: parallelise independent stages like unit tests,
integration tests and static analysis; shallow-clone; cache dependencies between builds; use Docker layer caching with a
correctly ordered Dockerfile; only run heavy stages when the relevant paths changed; split a long test suite across parallel
agents; and move to ephemeral agents if builds are queuing for capacity. I would also ask which stages actually need to gate
the merge - some can run after merge rather than blocking it."
Q. 15. A developer says "the pipeline is blocking me". How do you respond?
 Testing: collaboration and product thinking. Answer: "I treat that as a real problem, not a complaint. I find out specifically
what is blocking - a slow pipeline, a flaky test, an unclear failure message, or a gate that is not adding value. Flaky tests are
the most corrosive, because they teach people to re-run rather than read failures. I would quarantine the flake immediately
so it stops blocking everyone, then fix it properly. The principle is that a pipeline nobody trusts gets bypassed, and a
bypassed pipeline protects nothing."
Q. 16. Terraform wants to destroy production infrastructure. What do you do?
 Testing: whether you stop and think under pressure. Answer: "I do not apply. I read the plan and find exactly which
resources and which attribute is forcing replacement - the plan says 'forces replacement' next to it. Then I work out why: an
immutable field changed such as an availability zone or a name; a module version bump changed a default; the state was
rolled back, partially removed, or I am pointed at the wrong workspace or state key. I verify which state file and which
account I am actually using with aws sts get-caller-identity. If the change is genuinely necessary, I plan a safe migration
- create before destroy, or a manual cutover - rather than letting one apply do it. And this is exactly why prevent_destroy on
databases and mandatory plan review exist."
Q. 17. Two engineers ran terraform apply at the same time. What happened?
 Testing: understanding of state and locking. Answer: "With DynamoDB locking configured, the second one waits or fails
cleanly and nothing bad happens. Without locking, both read the same state, both apply, and the second write overwrites
the first - so state no longer matches reality and the next plan may propose destroying resources Terraform has lost track of.
Recovery is restoring the previous state version from the versioned S3 bucket, or importing the orphaned resources. The
prevention is locking, which should never be optional, plus applying only from CI so there is one serialised path."
Q. 18. Someone changed a security group in the console. What is the problem and what do you do?
 Testing: drift and process. Answer: "The immediate problem is that the code no longer matches reality, so the next
Terraform apply will revert it - possibly during an unrelated change and possibly causing an outage. I detect it with a
scheduled terraform plan that alerts on a non-empty plan. Then it is a decision: if the change was correct, I codify it and
reconcile with -refresh-only; if not, I re-apply to revert. The real fix is process - remove console write access in production
so Terraform is the only path, and make the emergency-change procedure explicit so people are not forced to go around
the tooling."
Q. 19. Your deploy succeeded but the database migration failed halfway.
 Testing: the hardest part of deployment. Answer: "This is the scenario where rollback does not save you, so the answer is
mostly about prevention. In the moment: stop further rollout, determine exactly which migration steps applied, and decide
whether the application version running can work with the partially migrated schema. If the old version cannot, rolling back
the code makes things worse. Usually the safest path is to complete or explicitly reverse the migration rather than roll back
blindly. Preventing it: migrations must be backward compatible - expand and contract, so the schema change ships before
the code that needs it and the old column is dropped a release later; migrations run as a separate gated step, not on every


===== PAGE 215 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 215
replica's startup; and they are tested against a production-sized dataset because a migration that is instant on staging can
lock a table for minutes in production."
Q. 20. How would you deploy a change that cannot be rolled back?
 Testing: risk management. Answer: "I would first try to make it rollback-able, because that is usually possible with more
work - feature flags, expand-and-contract schema changes, dual writes. If it genuinely cannot be, then the mitigations are:
deploy to a small percentage first and watch real metrics; have a tested forward-fix path and the people who can execute it
available; take a verified backup and rehearse the restore; schedule it in a low-traffic window; and communicate it as a
higher-risk change. The key point is that 'no rollback' raises the bar on everything else, it does not mean you proceed as
normal and hope."
17.3 Scenarios 21-32: Kubernetes and Containers
Q. 21. A Kubernetes pod is in CrashLoopBackOff. What do you check?
 Testing: the single most common Kubernetes question. Answer: "kubectl logs <pod> --previous first - the --previous
flag is essential because the current container may not have started yet, and the dead one holds the error. Then kubectl
describe pod for the exit code and Last State. Exit 1 is an application error, usually a missing environment variable, an
unreachable dependency at startup or bad config. Exit 137 with OOMKilled means the memory limit is too low or there is a
leak. Exit 0 repeatedly means the process finishes and exits - a wrong command, or an app that daemonises instead of
running in the foreground. Exit 127 means the command is not in the image. A liveness probe failing during a slow startup
also produces this, and the fix there is a startupProbe, not disabling liveness. If I cannot exec in because it keeps dying, I run
the same image with a sleep command to get a shell and inspect the environment."
Q. 22. A pod is stuck in Pending.
 Testing: scheduling. Answer: "kubectl describe pod and read the scheduler's message in Events - it states the reason
explicitly. Insufficient CPU or memory means no node has enough allocatable capacity for the pod's requests, so I either
reduce requests, add nodes, or check why the cluster autoscaler is not scaling. A taint message means I need a toleration. A
node affinity or selector message means nothing matches. 'Unbound immediate PersistentVolumeClaims' means the
storage is the problem - often a zone mismatch. Reading that one message solves this scenario almost every time."
Q. 23. A Service returns nothing but the pods are running.
 Testing: service networking. Answer: "kubectl get endpoints <service> - if it is empty, that is the whole diagnosis. Either
the Service's label selector does not match the pods' labels, which I confirm with kubectl get pods --show-labels, or the
pods are Running but not Ready, so they are excluded from endpoints. If endpoints are populated, I check targetPort
against the actual container port, and then test the pod directly with kubectl port-forward to separate the pod from the
Service from the Ingress."
Q. 24. Pods are being OOMKilled. Walk me through it.
 Answer: "kubectl describe pod confirms it - Last State Terminated, Reason OOMKilled, exit code 137. Then I compare
actual usage against the limit. If usage is steady just above the limit, the limit is too low and I raise it based on measured
usage. If usage climbs steadily over hours until it hits the limit and repeats, that is a leak and raising the limit only lengthens
the interval between crashes. For a JVM or Node application I check whether the runtime is sizing its heap from the host's
memory rather than the container limit, because that guarantees an OOM kill - the fix is -XX:MaxRAMPercentage or
--max-old-space-size. Memory limits are a hard kill, unlike CPU which is throttled, so the limit must be above the real peak."
Q. 25. A node goes NotReady. What happens to the pods and what do you do?
 Answer: "After the toleration period, the node is tainted and its pods are evicted and rescheduled on healthy nodes -
assuming there is capacity and their storage allows it. For the node itself, kubectl describe node shows the conditions:
DiskPressure is the most common in practice, then memory, then a kubelet or container runtime failure, then network or an
expired kubelet certificate. I check the kubelet journal on the node. In a cloud environment, replacing the node is usually
faster and more reliable than repairing it - cordon, drain, terminate, let the ASG replace it."
Q. 26. A rollout is stuck and kubectl rollout status never completes.
 Answer: "The new pods are failing in one of the ways we have already covered, so I look at them directly - Pending,
ImagePullBackOff, CrashLoopBackOff, or Running but never Ready. kubectl describe rs on the new ReplicaSet shows
events about why pods could not even be created, such as a quota being exceeded. A specific trap is maxUnavailable: 0
with no spare capacity: the new pod cannot be scheduled, the old one cannot be removed, and the rollout stalls at step one
forever. A PodDisruptionBudget can block progress the same way. Operationally I roll back first to restore a known state,
then diagnose."
Q. 27. A container cannot connect to the database. Where do you look?


===== PAGE 216 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 216
Answer: "First, is the application using localhost? Inside a container that means the container itself, and it is the single most
common containerisation mistake. Then, is name resolution working - nslookup from inside the pod for the service or
endpoint name. Then network reachability - can it open the port. Then credentials from the Secret, and whether the Secret
in that namespace holds what I expect. Then the database side - is it accepting connections, is it at max connections, does
the security group allow the source. I test each step from inside the pod, because the answer from my laptop is not the
same question."
Q. 28. Explain what happens when a pod is deleted.
 Testing: graceful shutdown. Answer: "The pod is marked Terminating and removed from Service endpoints; a preStop hook
runs if defined; the container receives SIGTERM; after the termination grace period anything still running gets SIGKILL. Two
things commonly go wrong. First, the application ignores SIGTERM - usually because a shell is PID 1 rather than the app,
which is why entrypoints must exec. Second, endpoint removal propagates asynchronously across nodes, so for a brief
window traffic still arrives at the terminating pod; a short preStop sleep covers that. Without both, every deployment drops
in-flight requests."
Q. 29. You need to debug a production pod but the image has no shell.
 Answer: "kubectl debug with an ephemeral container attached to the pod's namespaces using an image that does have
tools - that is the supported mechanism and it does not require restarting the pod. Otherwise kubectl logs, kubectl
describe, and port-forward to test it directly. The deeper point is that distroless images make this harder deliberately, in
exchange for a much smaller attack surface, and the right answer is better instrumentation rather than adding a shell back
into production images."
Q. 30. Your cluster is running out of capacity. What do you do?
 Answer: "Short term, either add nodes or free capacity. Longer term I look at whether requests reflect reality - the most
common cause of a 'full' cluster is over-requested pods where the requests are three times the actual usage, so nodes look
full while sitting at 20% real utilisation. I compare requests to actual usage across the top consumers and right-size. Then
cluster autoscaler configuration, whether there are pods that should have lower priority, and whether any workload should
be on different instance types. It is a capacity planning problem, not just a scaling button."
Q. 31. A container works on your laptop but fails in the cluster.
 Answer: "The differences are usually: configuration and secrets, which exist in your local env file and not in the cluster;
networking, since localhost and host-mounted services do not exist in a pod; storage, where a bind mount becomes a
volume with different ownership; user and permissions, because production runs non-root with a read-only root filesystem;
resource limits, which do not exist locally; and architecture if you are on Apple silicon building for amd64 nodes. I check the
pod's actual environment with kubectl exec ... env and compare it against what the app expects."
Q. 32. How would you find which pod is consuming the most memory across a cluster?
 Answer: "kubectl top pods -A --sort-by=memory for the immediate answer, and in Prometheus topk(10,
container_memory_working_set_bytes) for history, which matters more because I usually want to see the trend rather than
the instant. The more useful query is usage as a fraction of the limit, because a pod using 4 GB of an 8 GB limit is fine while
one using 480 MB of a 512 MB limit is about to be killed."
17.4 Scenarios 33-42: Linux, AWS and Networking
Q. 33. EC2 instance is running but SSH does not work.
 Testing: layered network diagnosis. Answer: "First I distinguish the two failure modes, because they lead to completely
different investigations. 'Connection timed out' is a network problem - security group inbound 22, NACL inbound and
outbound because it is stateless, route table, public IP, or the host being unreachable. 'Permission denied (publickey)' means
I reached sshd, so it is the key, the username or the server's authorized_keys - and the correct default user differs by AMI. I
run ssh -v and read where it stops. If neither applies, I check the instance status checks and get-console-output - a bad
/etc/fstab entry blocking boot or a full disk breaking sshd both show up there. If SSH is unrecoverable, SSM Session
Manager, or detaching the root volume and mounting it on another instance to fix the file."
Q. 34. Disk reaches 95% on a production server at 2am. What do you do?
 Answer: "Mitigate first, safely. df -h and df -i to see whether it is blocks or inodes, then du -xh --max-depth=1 / and
descend to find the offender. I compress and archive logs rather than deleting anything live, vacuum the journal, and prune
Docker objects with a time filter if it is a container host. If deleting a large file does not return space, a process is holding the
descriptor - lsof | grep deleted - and I restart that process. Once stable, I grow the volume if that is the real answer. Then
the RCA is why nothing alerted at 80%, and the prevention is logrotate, log driver limits, retention policies and a predictive
alert that gives hours of warning."


===== PAGE 217 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 217
Q. 35. CPU suddenly hits 100% on all application servers simultaneously.
 Answer: "Simultaneous across all servers rules out a single bad host and points at either a traffic change or something we
shipped. I check request rate first - if traffic is flat and CPU jumped, it is a release or a cron job; if traffic doubled, it is
demand. top with 1 tells me whether it is user, system, iowait or steal, which are four different problems. If it followed a
deploy, I roll back. If it is demand, I scale out. If it is a batch job that should not run during peak, I kill it and reschedule. High
steal time on burstable instances is a different answer entirely - we have outgrown the instance type."
Q. 36. An application cannot connect to MySQL. Walk me through it.
 Answer: "I go layer by layer from the application host. Does the hostname resolve, and to the IP I expect? Can I open port
3306 - and is the failure refused or a timeout, because refused means the network is fine and MySQL is not accepting, while
timeout means a security group or route. Then I try an actual connection with credentials, since an authentication error is a
completely different problem from a connection failure. Then the database side: is it at max_connections, is a query holding
a lock, is its disk full, did it just fail over so the DNS endpoint points somewhere new. 'Too many connections' usually means
the application is leaking connections rather than that the limit is wrong, and restarting the application is a mitigation while
the pool configuration is the fix."
Q. 37. S3 uploads suddenly fail with AccessDenied, and nothing changed.
 Answer: "I start from the error, which names the principal, action and resource. Then I confirm which identity is actually
being used - aws sts get-caller-identity - because a stale profile or an environment variable overriding the role is
common. Then I look for an explicit Deny, in the bucket policy, a permissions boundary or an SCP, since Deny always wins.
Then the ARN form: object actions need bucket/* while ListBucket needs bucket - mixing those up is the classic bug. If the
bucket is KMS-encrypted, the principal also needs kms:GenerateDataKey, and the error still says S3 AccessDenied, which is
misleading. CloudTrail shows the exact denied call with full context."
Q. 38. Your application in a private subnet cannot reach an external API.
 Answer: "Private subnets need a NAT gateway for outbound traffic, so I check the subnet's route table for 0.0.0.0/0
pointing at a NAT gateway, and that the NAT gateway itself is in a public subnet with a route to the internet gateway. Then
outbound security group rules and NACL rules including the inbound ephemeral range for return traffic. I test from the
instance to separate DNS from connectivity - dig the hostname, then curl -v. If only S3 or DynamoDB are failing, it is a
gateway endpoint issue instead. And if this started suddenly, someone deleting a NAT gateway to save cost is a surprisingly
common cause."
Q. 39. A load balancer shows all targets unhealthy.
 Answer: "I read the health check failure reason, which the target group provides - a 404 means the health check path is
wrong, a timeout means the security group or the application is not responding. Then: does the target's security group allow
the load balancer's security group on the target port; is the application listening on 0.0.0.0 rather than loopback; is the
health check port the right one; and are the thresholds and timeouts sane - a timeout shorter than the app's response time
under load will take out a healthy fleet. If all targets went unhealthy at once, either something was deployed or the health
check endpoint depends on a shared resource that is down."
Q. 40. A cron job stopped running and nobody noticed for a week.
 Answer: "Two problems: why it stopped, and why nobody noticed. For the first, I check whether cron is running, whether the
crontab still exists, whether the script path or permissions changed, whether the host was replaced, and whether output was
going anywhere - cron's minimal PATH silently breaks scripts that call aws or kubectl by name. For the second, which
matters more: the job needed a heartbeat. Alerting on failure only catches it when it runs; the dangerous case is when it
stops running entirely, which only a staleness check catches - the age of the last success exceeding its interval. I would add
that and move the job to a systemd timer or a Kubernetes CronJob so its output lands somewhere visible."
Q. 41. Certificate expired in production. How did that happen and how do you prevent it?
 Answer: "It happened because renewal was manual, or automated renewal failed silently and nobody was watching.
Immediate fix: issue and deploy a new certificate with the full chain, and verify from outside with openssl s_client.
Prevention is layered - automated renewal through ACM or cert-manager so no human is in the path, plus monitoring of
actual expiry as observed on the live endpoint via blackbox_exporter, alerting at 30 days and again at 7. The monitoring
matters even with automation, because the failure mode is the automation breaking quietly."
Q. 42. You inherit an environment with no documentation. Where do you start?
 Answer: "I map reality before changing anything. What is running - instances, clusters, databases, load balancers - and how
traffic reaches the users, traced from DNS inwards. Where the code and the pipelines are. What monitoring and alerting
exist and who receives them. What the backup and restore situation is, and whether a restore has ever been tested. Then I
write that down, because the documentation gap is itself a risk. My first changes would be the cheap, high-value ones:
alerting on the things that cause outages - disk, certificates, healthy host count - and getting infrastructure into code


===== PAGE 218 =====

incrementally, starting with new resources rather than attempting a big-bang import."
17.5 Scenarios 43-50: Judgement, Process and People
Q. 43. You are asked to make a change to production during peak hours.
 Testing: risk judgement and communication. Answer: "I would ask what makes it urgent, because the answer changes the
decision entirely. If it is fixing a live incident, the risk of waiting is higher than the risk of changing, so we proceed carefully
with a rollback ready. If it is routine, I would explain the risk and propose a scheduled window, giving a concrete alternative
rather than just saying no. If the business decides it must happen now, that is a legitimate decision - I make sure it is made
with the risk understood, we have a tested rollback, someone is watching the dashboards, and the change is as small as
possible."
Q. 44. You realise you caused the outage. What do you do?
 Testing: honesty and psychological safety. Answer: "I say so immediately, because knowing the cause is the fastest route to
resolution and withholding it costs everyone time. Restore service first, then contribute what I know to the timeline. In the
RCA I focus on the system, not myself - if one person's action could take production down, the real finding is the missing
guard rail: no review, no staging verification, no confirmation step. Teams where people hide mistakes have longer outages,
and I would rather be the person who says it early."
Q. 45. A developer wants production database access to debug. How do you respond?
 Answer: "I would find out what they actually need, because 'production access' is usually a proxy for 'I cannot see what is
happening'. Often the real need is logs, metrics or a read-only query, and the right answer is better observability rather than
credentials. If direct access is genuinely needed, it should be read-only, time-limited, audited, ideally through a break-glass
process with approval, and against a replica rather than the primary. The goal is to unblock them without creating a
standing risk - saying no without offering a path just gets worked around."
Q. 46. Your manager wants to skip testing to meet a deadline.
 Answer: "I would make the trade-off explicit rather than argue. I would say which specific tests would be skipped and what
risk that carries - for example, we would not know whether the payment path still works. Then offer options: ship a smaller
scope that is fully tested, ship behind a feature flag so it can be disabled instantly, or ship with a reduced test set and an
agreed rollback plan and someone watching. That turns it into an informed business decision, which it legitimately is, rather
than a disagreement about process."
Q. 47. Two teams disagree about a technical approach and you are in the middle.
 Answer: "I would try to get the disagreement onto facts. Usually both sides are optimising for different things - one for
delivery speed, one for operability - and neither is wrong. I would write down the requirements and constraints both agree
on, then evaluate the options against them, including what each costs to run and to reverse. If it is genuinely close, I would
favour the option that is easiest to change later. And if a decision is needed and consensus is not coming, I would say so and
escalate rather than let it drift - an unmade decision is usually worse than either option."
Q. 48. How do you prioritise when three incidents happen at once?
 Answer: "By user impact and blast radius, not by who is shouting. A complete outage affecting all users beats a degraded
feature beats a single internal tool. I would also check whether they are actually one incident with three symptoms, which is
common - a shared dependency failing produces alerts everywhere. Then I would get help rather than trying to work three
at once: assign owners, keep a single incident channel per issue, and make sure someone is coordinating and
communicating rather than everyone debugging in silence."
Q. 49. You are on call and an alert fires at 3am for something that is not actually broken.
 Answer: "I handle it, then I fix the alert - because an alert that pages a human for nothing is a defect, and the cost is that
people start ignoring alerts, which is how real incidents get missed. Concretely that means checking whether the threshold is
right, whether it needs a for: duration, whether it should be a ticket rather than a page, and whether it is a symptom of
something already alerted. I would track which alerts fire and what action was taken, and delete or demote any that never
lead to action."
Q. 50. What would you do in your first 90 days in this role?
 Testing: whether you think beyond tasks. Answer: "First month, understand before changing: the architecture and how a
request flows end to end, the deployment path from commit to production, what monitoring and alerting exist, where the
pain is - I would ask the team what wakes them up and what they dread doing. I would also pick up on-call early, because
nothing teaches you a system faster. Second month, fix the cheap high-value things: alerting gaps around disk, certificates
and availability; pipeline friction; documentation for the things only one person knows. Third month, take on something
structural - whatever the first two months showed was the biggest recurring cost, whether that is environment drift, manual

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 17  |  SCENARIO-BASED INTERVIEW QUESTIONS
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 219
deployment steps or toil in the on-call rotation. I would rather earn the right to change things by fixing the obvious pain
first."
PART 17 RECALL POINTS
 Always open by scoping: who, when, what changed, getting worse?
 Mitigate before diagnosing; roll back on doubt.
 Refused vs timeout; Pending vs CrashLoop vs not-Ready - each splits the problem.
 Say what each check would tell you, not just what you would type.
 Name the prevention at the end of every scenario answer.
 For people scenarios: make trade-offs explicit, offer options, never just say no.

Part 18. PROJECT EXPLANATION
Your CV names three projects: CI/CD Deployment Automation, Infrastructure as Code with Terraform, and
Containerized Deployment and Observability. This part builds one consistent narrative across all three - because in an
interview they are not three projects, they are one platform - and then cross-questions it the way an interviewer will.
THE EXACT WORDING YOU MUST STAY CONSISTENT WITH
 CI/CD Deployment Automation: "Designed Jenkins pipeline-as-code workflows integrating Git, automated builds,
testing and deployment stages to standardize application release workflows."
 Infrastructure as Code with Terraform: "Built reusable Terraform modules to provision AWS infrastructure,
enabling repeatable environment creation and reducing manual configuration."
 Containerized Deployment and Observability: "Deployed Dockerized components on Kubernetes (Pods,
Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, Secrets) with Prometheus, Grafana and CloudWatch dashboards and alerting."
THE RULES FOR THIS ENTIRE PART
Do not add numbers your CV does not contain - no traffic volumes, no percentage improvements, no team sizes, no
uptime figures, no customer names. Describe mechanism and responsibility. If an interviewer presses for scale, say what
you know and be explicit about what you did not own. A confident "I did not own that measurement" costs you
nothing; an invented figure that collapses under follow-up costs you the offer.

18.2 The 30-Second Version
 30 SECONDS - FOR "TELL ME ABOUT A PROJECT YOU WORKED ON"
"The project I'd point to is our deployment automation platform. I built Jenkins pipelines as code that take a commit
from Git through build, test, packaging and deployment, so releases became a triggered job rather than a manual
sequence. Underneath it, the AWS infrastructure is provisioned with reusable Terraform modules and configured with
Ansible, so environments are repeatable rather than hand-built. Application components are containerised with Docker
and deployed to Kubernetes, and we monitor all of it with Prometheus, Grafana and CloudWatch, with dashboards and
alerting so we see problems before users report them."
18.3 The One-Minute Version
 1 MINUTE - ADDS THE MECHANISM
"The work spans three connected pieces that are really one platform.
The first is CI/CD. I designed Jenkins pipeline-as-code workflows - the Jenkinsfile lives in the repository, so the build
definition is versioned and reviewed like any other change. A push or pull request triggers a webhook, and the pipeline
checks out that commit, builds, runs tests, packages the application, builds a Docker image tagged with the build
number and commit SHA, pushes it to the registry, and deploys. Because the image tag is immutable, the same artifact
moves through environments and rollback is redeploying a previous tag.
The second is infrastructure. I built reusable Terraform modules that provision the AWS infrastructure - VPC, subnets,
security groups, EC2, IAM, S3 and the monitoring resources - so a new environment is a set of variable values, not a new
set of resource blocks. Ansible then handles configuration on the instances: packages, config files rendered from
templates, users and services, with handlers so a service only restarts when its configuration actually changed.
The third is containers and observability. Components are containerised with Docker and run on Kubernetes using
Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps and Secrets, and Prometheus, Grafana and CloudWatch give us metrics,
dashboards and alerting across both the application and the AWS services it depends on."
18.4 The Three-Minute Version
 3 MINUTES - ADD THE WHY AND THE DECISIONS


===== PAGE 222 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 18  |  PROJECT EXPLANATION
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 222
"I'll take it in the order a change actually flows.
The problem. Deployments were manual and the steps varied between environments, which is where release
inconsistency comes from - not from anyone being careless, but because a sequence a person performs is never
identical twice. Infrastructure was configured by hand, so environments drifted apart and 'works in test' stopped
meaning anything.
CI/CD. I built the pipelines as code. The Jenkinsfile sits in the repository, so changing the build is a pull request with
review, and each branch can have its own pipeline through a multibranch job. GitHub webhooks trigger it rather than
polling, so feedback is immediate. The stages are checkout, build, test with results published so the build goes unstable
rather than silently green, package, Docker build, push to the registry, then deploy followed by a health check.
Credentials come from the Jenkins credential store and are bound only to the stage that needs them, never hardcoded.
Concurrent builds are disabled on anything that deploys, because two simultaneous deploys to one environment is a
self-inflicted outage.
The artifact discipline. The image is built once and tagged with the build number and the short commit SHA. That tag
is immutable, so what was tested is exactly what is deployed, I can tie anything running back to a commit, and rollback
is deploying the previous tag rather than rebuilding.
Infrastructure as code. Terraform provisions AWS - VPC and subnets, security groups, EC2, IAM roles, S3 and the
monitoring resources. I built it as reusable modules rather than one flat configuration, so an environment is a module
call with different variables. That is what makes environments reproducible and what makes a change to the pattern a
single edit rather than three. State is remote and locked so two applies cannot collide, and plans are reviewed before
apply.
Configuration. Ansible handles what Terraform deliberately does not - packages, configuration files rendered from
Jinja2 templates with per-environment variables, users, and service state. Tasks are idempotent, so re-running is safe,
and handlers mean a service restarts only when its configuration actually changed.
Containers and Kubernetes. Components are containerised with Docker, which is what removed the environment
inconsistency - the runtime and libraries travel with the application. On Kubernetes we use Deployments for rollout and
self-healing, Services for stable addressing, and ConfigMaps and Secrets for configuration. Most of my Kubernetes work
is deploying and troubleshooting workloads - pods, deployments, services, configmaps and connectivity - rather than
administering the cluster itself.
Observability. Prometheus scrapes application and infrastructure metrics, Grafana provides the dashboards, and
CloudWatch covers the AWS services we cannot instrument ourselves - load balancers, RDS - plus log aggregation.
Alerts go on symptoms rather than raw resource numbers, so we are paged for things users would notice.
Operations. When something breaks, I work through application logs, service state on both Linux and Windows,
database connectivity and network diagnostics - HTTP, DNS, TCP and RTSP in our case, because video components are
part of the stack. I do the RCA, coordinate the fix with development and QA, and validate the upgrade or release
afterwards."
18.5 The Five-Minute Technical Version
 5 MINUTES - WHAT A SENIOR ENGINEER WANTS TO HEAR


===== PAGE 223 =====

AASIM RAZA  |  COMPLETE DEVOPS INTERVIEW HANDBOOK
Part 18  |  PROJECT EXPLANATION
DevOps Engineer Interview Preparation
Page 223
"Let me go through it as a technical walkthrough of one change reaching production, then the operational side.
1. Trigger and checkout. A developer works on a short-lived branch and opens a pull request. A GitHub webhook hits
Jenkins, and a multibranch pipeline builds that branch - which also means pull requests are validated before merge, not
after. The pipeline does checkout scm, so it builds the exact commit that triggered it rather than whatever is currently on
a named branch, and I capture the short SHA into an environment variable because it becomes part of the artifact
identity.
2. Build and test. Build produces the application artifact. Tests run with results published through the JUnit step, which
marks the build unstable rather than failed - that distinction matters because a compile failure and a flaky test deserve
different responses. Anything that does not need to gate the merge runs in parallel with the tests to keep the pipeline
short, because a slow pipeline is one people route around.
3. Package. Docker build produces the image. The Dockerfile copies dependency manifests and installs dependencies
before copying source, so the expensive layer stays cached across code commits; the final image runs as a non-root
user and has a health check. The image is tagged with the build number and the commit SHA - never just latest,
because a mutable tag means you cannot tell what is running and cannot roll back precisely.
4. Publish. The image is pushed to the registry using credentials bound from the Jenkins credential store inside a
withCredentials block, with the password piped to docker login --password-stdin so it never appears on a command
line or in the console output.
5. Deploy. The deploy stage targets the environment. For EC2-based components that is Ansible - a playbook that
renders configuration from templates, manages services, and uses serial so hosts are updated in batches rather than
all at once, with a health check before moving to the next batch. For containerised components it is Kubernetes -
updating the Deployment's image and then waiting on kubectl rollout status, so the pipeline fails if the new pods
never become Ready rather than reporting success and walking away.
6. Verify. A health check with retries against the application's health endpoint. This is the gate that makes the
difference between 'the deployment command returned zero' and 'the application is actually serving'.
7. Rollback. Because artifacts are immutable and versioned, rollback is deploying the previous tag, or kubectl rollout
undo, which works because Kubernetes keeps the previous ReplicaSet. The important caveat I would raise is that rollback
covers code, not data - schema changes need to be backward compatible, expand-and-contract, or rollback is not
actually available to you.
8. The infrastructure underneath. Terraform modules provision it: VPC with public and private subnets across
availability zones, security groups that reference each other rather than CIDR ranges so the tiering is explicit and
survives instance replacement, EC2 with instance profiles rather than access keys, S3, Route 53 and the CloudWatch
resources. Remote state in S3 with DynamoDB locking, one state key per environment so a mistake in one cannot affect
another, and providers and modules pinned so a build is reproducible. In a pipeline, plan on the pull request and apply
the saved plan file on merge, so what a human reviewed is exactly what runs.
9. Observability. Prometheus scrapes the application and node exporters; Grafana dashboards show request rate, error
rate and latency percentiles alongside resource saturation; CloudWatch covers the managed AWS services and log
aggregation, because you cannot install an exporter inside an ALB or RDS. Alerting is symptom-based with a for:
duration so transient spikes do not page anyone.
10. Security posture. No credentials in code or images; secrets from the credential store and from AWS; IAM roles
rather than long-lived keys; least privilege on the deployment identity; image scanning in the pipeline; and
infrastructure changes going through pull request review like application code.
11. Operations. On the production support side, incidents come in through alerts or from QA and development. I work
through application logs, systemd services on Linux and Windows services on the Windows side, database connectivity,
and network diagnostics across HTTP/HTTPS, TCP/IP, DNS and RTSP. I do root cause analysis, coordinate the fix, and
validate application upgrades and releases afterwards - which is really the feedback loop closing back into the backlog."
HOW TO ADAPT THIS TO THE JOB DESCRIPTION
If the role is Kubernetes-heavy, spend longer on stages 5-7 and the observability section and be explicit that your
Kubernetes work is workload-level. If it is cloud-infrastructure-heavy, spend longer on stage 8. If it is SRE or production
support, lead with stage 11 - that is your differentiator and most 3-year candidates cannot describe a troubleshooting
method at all.
